Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Take notes during live or recorded lectures that capture the lecture's structure, main points, key terms, the content the lecturer flags as important, and the sources mentioned, in real time and with minimal loss of attention to the speaker. For most students, lecture notes are the main record of oral content for revision and assignments.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points), [Abbreviations and symbols](#ntkabbreviations-symbols) (soft), [Note formats](#ntknote-formats)

<a id="ntknotes-from-reading"></a>
#### Making notes from reading

`ntk.notes-from-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Make notes from academic reading that record full source details with page or location numbers and keep three kinds of content visibly separate: direct quotation (marked as such), condensed notes in one's own words, and one's own comments, questions and evaluations. Well-made reading notes are the main practical safeguard against unintentional plagiarism and the raw material for synthesis.

Prerequisites: [Purposeful annotation](#rdsannotation) (soft), [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

<a id="ntkreviewing-notes"></a>
#### Reviewing and reworking notes

`ntk.reviewing-notes` · STRATEGIC · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Review and rework notes after a lecture or reading through generative activities — completing gaps, summarising, generating and answering questions, reorganising into another format and self-testing at spaced intervals — rather than rereading or recopying them. Review is what turns notes from a record into learning.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques)

<a id="ntkdigital-notes"></a>
#### Digital and handwritten note-taking

`ntk.digital-notes` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Choose between handwritten, typed, stylus and app-based note-taking according to the task, the content (for example equations or diagrams) and personal effectiveness, use devices in ways that avoid verbatim transcription and multitasking, and organise digital notes so they can be found and reused. The evidence on laptop versus longhand notes is mixed, so the choice should rest on how notes are taken rather than on the device alone.

Prerequisites: [Taking notes in lectures](#ntklecture-notes), [File and version management](#digfile-management) (soft)

<a id="ntkcollaborative-notes"></a>
#### Collaborative notes

`ntk.collaborative-notes` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Co-construct and share notes in study groups — agreeing a common format, dividing responsibilities, cross-checking accuracy and pooling gaps — while keeping individual processing and staying within institutional rules on collusion where notes feed into individually assessed work. Shared notes can fill individual gaps but can also spread errors and reduce the processing that taking notes provides.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [Digital collaboration tools](#digdigital-collaboration) (soft)

## Academic Speaking

### Seminars & Discussion

Seminars, tutorials and discussion groups are where students are expected to co-construct understanding of readings and ideas in real time, and where participation is often assessed formally or informally. This domain treats discussion as negotiated, multi-turn interaction rather than a list of functional phrases: learners need to prepare substantively, find and hold turns, state and justify positions, explain concepts and readings to peers, mitigate disagreement, build on and summarise others' contributions and, at higher levels, facilitate. Participation is also shaped by anxiety, positioning, disability and the way tutors and peers distribute the floor, so the domain addresses the conditions for participation as well as individual skill, and accepts more than one mode of contribution, including written and chat contributions.

<a id="semseminar-purposes"></a>
#### Purposes and expectations of seminars

`sem.seminar-purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Explain what seminars, tutorials and discussion classes are for in a given programme — co-constructing understanding of readings and concepts, testing ideas and showing engagement — and how participation is expected, and sometimes assessed. Because these expectations are largely tacit and vary by discipline, level, class size and tutor, knowing them helps learners interpret silence, interruption and critique and decide how to contribute.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="sempreparation"></a>
#### Preparing for seminars

`sem.preparation` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Prepare for a seminar by reading the set texts with the discussion questions in mind, noting key claims with page references, reactions and points of uncertainty, and planning two or three possible contributions and questions in note form. Preparation reduces real-time processing load so that learners can attend to the discussion and enter it with substantive content rather than summary.

Prerequisites: [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Purposes and expectations of seminars](#semseminar-purposes), [Critical questioning and question sets](#crtquestioning) (soft)

<a id="semturn-taking"></a>
#### Turn-taking and taking the floor

`sem.turn-taking` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Enter, hold and yield turns in multi-party academic discussion by recognising likely transition points, using brief floor-claiming and floor-holding moves, and judging when overlap or interruption is acceptable in a particular group. Because turns in student discussion are often taken by self-selection rather than allocated by the tutor, learners who wait to be invited may have few opportunities to contribute.

Prerequisites: [Grammar of spoken academic interaction](#grmspoken-grammar) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="semopinions"></a>
#### Expressing and justifying views

`sem.opinions` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

State a position on a question or reading clearly and support it with reasons, examples or evidence, using language for stating positions, giving reasons and exemplifying, and signalling how firmly the view is held. In seminars both unsupported assertion and unpositioned summary are weak contributions; a short claim–reason–support move that can be extended under questioning is the basic unit of contribution.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="semagreeing-disagreeing"></a>
#### Agreeing and disagreeing

`sem.agreeing-disagreeing` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Agree, partially agree and disagree with peers, tutors and authors in ways that make the point of difference explicit while maintaining collegial relationships, typically by acknowledging the other position, locating the specific difference and giving a reason, with mitigation adjusted to the relationship and the stakes. Seminars value disagreement about ideas, but bald disagreement can damage relationships and over-hedged disagreement can obscure the speaker's position.

Prerequisites: [Expressing stance in speech](#staspoken-stance), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="sembuilding-on-others"></a>
#### Building on others' contributions

`sem.building-on-others` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Link a contribution explicitly to what others have said by extending, qualifying, exemplifying, connecting or contrasting their points, and attribute those points to the speaker. Building on others is how ideas are co-constructed across turns in seminars, and it makes listening visible as well as thinking.

Prerequisites: [Following multi-party discussion](#lismulti-party-discussion) (soft), [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking) (soft), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft)

<a id="semquestions-clarification"></a>
#### Asking questions and seeking clarification

`sem.questions-clarification` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Formulate questions that seek clarification, elaboration, evidence or implications from tutors and peers, pinpointing which term, claim or step the question concerns and choosing direct or mitigated forms suited to the relationship. Asking questions is both a comprehension-repair strategy and a legitimate form of contribution, and subject lecturers have identified it as an oral demand that multilingual students can find difficult.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive listening strategies](#lismetacognitive-listening) (soft), [Critical questioning and question sets](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="semchecking-understanding"></a>
#### Checking and confirming understanding

`sem.checking-understanding` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Confirm mutual understanding during discussion by paraphrasing others' points for confirmation, checking that one's own point has been understood, and reformulating it with simpler wording, an example or a definition when it has not. In multilingual groups whose members differ in accent, proficiency and disciplinary background, explicit confirmation and reformulation are routine collaborative moves rather than signs of weakness.

Prerequisites: [Asking questions and seeking clarification](#semquestions-clarification), [Self-repair and reformulation](#prnrepair) (soft)

<a id="semciting-in-speech"></a>
#### Referring to readings and evidence in discussion

`sem.citing-in-speech` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Refer to set readings, data and other sources in discussion with brief spoken attribution (author or text, the relevant point and, where useful, a page or section), keeping the source's claim distinct from one's own interpretation. Oral attribution makes contributions checkable, shows engagement with the reading and lets peers locate the passage under discussion.

Prerequisites: [Preparing for seminars](#sempreparation) (soft), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs)

<a id="semfacilitating"></a>
#### Facilitating discussion

`sem.facilitating` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Lead or chair a discussion by framing the task with an open question, inviting and distributing contributions, probing and redirecting, signalling stages and time, and closing with a fair summary. Facilitation is commonly assessed in student-led seminars and is a mediation skill that requires attending to others' participation and the development of ideas as much as to one's own content.

Prerequisites: [Summarising discussion](#semsummarising-discussion), [Explaining concepts and readings to peers](#semexplaining-concepts) (soft), [Inclusive collaboration in multilingual groups](#grwinclusive-collaboration) (soft)

<a id="semsummarising-discussion"></a>
#### Summarising discussion

`sem.summarising-discussion` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Summarise a discussion orally or in brief notes by selecting the main positions, points of agreement and disagreement, unresolved questions and outcomes, organising them thematically and attributing them fairly. Summarising consolidates co-constructed understanding and is required when groups report back to a class or when a facilitator closes a discussion.

Prerequisites: [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content) (soft), [Building on others' contributions](#sembuilding-on-others)

<a id="semspeaking-anxiety"></a>
#### Managing speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate

`sem.speaking-anxiety` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate (WTC) as situational and changeable — shaped by preparation, task familiarity, group relations, how the floor is distributed and perceived evaluation — and use preparation and in-the-moment strategies to participate. Treating reticence as a product of conditions rather than a fixed personal or cultural trait lets learners and teachers change the conditions as well as the behaviour.

Prerequisites: [Purposes and expectations of seminars](#semseminar-purposes) (soft), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="semonline-discussion"></a>
#### Synchronous online discussion

`sem.online-discussion` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Participate in synchronous online and hybrid seminars by using raise-hand, chat and reaction functions to bid for and supplement turns, compensating for reduced gaze and gesture and for audio delay with explicit addressing, yielding and confirmation, and organising breakout-room tasks. Online discussion changes how turns are taken and how participation is made visible, so face-to-face strategies need adapting.

Prerequisites: [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="semdebate"></a>
#### Structured academic debate

`sem.debate` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Take part in structured academic debates by building a case within a time limit, anticipating and rebutting the strongest opposing arguments, conceding where necessary and responding concisely to challenges. Debate trains rapid oral argument, but its adversarial logic differs from the exploratory, idea-developing norms of most seminars, and learners need to know when each mode is appropriate.

Prerequisites: [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession), [Arguing orally](#argoral-argument), [Exploratory and adversarial argument](#argexploratory-adversarial) (soft)

<a id="semexplaining-concepts"></a>
#### Explaining concepts and readings to peers

`sem.explaining-concepts` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Explain a concept, reading or dataset to classmates who have not met it, or who meet it through a different language or discipline: selecting what matters, breaking it into steps, defining and exemplifying, linking it to what listeners already know, checking understanding and reformulating, and drawing on shared languages where this helps. This everyday mediation work in seminars, jigsaw reading, study groups and peer teaching is often assessed within presentation and seminar tasks, and it underpins later teaching and demonstrating roles.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

### Presentations

Academic presentations range from short assessed talks on foundation and pre-sessional programmes to conference papers, posters, research seminars and short-format pitches at doctoral level. This domain treats the presentation as a spoken, multimodal genre designed for listeners who process information once, in real time: it needs explicit structure and signposting, visuals that support rather than duplicate speech, intelligible prosody and a delivery mode between reading a script and improvising. It gives particular attention to the interactive phases — openings, question-and-answer sessions and poster conversations — which research on conference presentations shows are central to how presenters engage audiences and establish credibility, and it treats poster design as part of the poster genre.

<a id="prspurpose-audience"></a>
#### Purpose and audience analysis

`prs.purpose-audience` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Analyse the purpose of a presentation (to inform, explain, persuade, report research or propose) and the audience's prior knowledge, expectations and relationship to the speaker, and adapt content selection, technical density, register and interaction accordingly. Audience analysis drives later decisions about structure, visuals and delivery, and is what distinguishes a presentation from a reading of an essay.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="prsstructure"></a>
#### Structuring a presentation

`prs.structure` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Organise a presentation into an introduction, body and conclusion with an explicit roadmap, a limited number of main points, internal summaries and a conclusion that returns to the purpose, designed for listeners who cannot re-read. Spoken structure needs more redundancy and explicit signalling than written text, so an essay cannot simply be read or summarised section by section.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience), [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft)

<a id="prsopenings"></a>
#### Opening a presentation

`prs.openings` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Open a presentation by establishing context and relevance, engaging the audience, stating the purpose or central claim and previewing the structure, selecting and ordering these moves to suit the audience and genre. The opening sets the audience's interpretive frame and the speaker's credibility, and research on conference introductions shows that interpersonal engagement matters here as much as content.

Prerequisites: [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure)

<a id="prssignposting"></a>
#### Signposting in presentations and spoken explanations

`prs.signposting` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Use spoken signposting to announce structure, mark transitions, flag key points, summarise and conclude in presentations, extended seminar contributions and spoken explanations, combining macro-markers that open and close sections and spoken micro-markers such as so and now with pausing and pitch reset at boundaries. Because listeners cannot scan back, explicit structural signalling carries more of the organisational load in speech than in writing, but its density should match the length and audience of the talk, since mechanical or excessive markers distract.

Prerequisites: [Spoken academic formulas](#phrspoken-academic-formulas) (soft), [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure), [Thought groups and pausing](#prnthought-groups) (soft)

<a id="prsslide-design"></a>
#### Designing slides

`prs.slide-design` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Design slides and other visual aids that support the spoken message: one main point per slide, headings that state that point, minimal text, readable type, purposeful and simplified figures, clear visual hierarchy, accessible colour and contrast, and attribution of images and data. Slides should complement speech rather than duplicate it, because dense slide text competes with the speaker for the audience's attention.

Prerequisites: [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure) (soft), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Accessible academic documents](#mmdaccessibility) (soft)

<a id="prsexplaining-visuals"></a>
#### Talking about visuals and data

`prs.explaining-visuals` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (sciences, engineering, economics, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Guide an audience through a chart, table or diagram by orienting them to what it shows (type, axes, units, categories), highlighting the one to three features that matter, and stating their significance for the argument, while synchronising speech with pointing or slide builds. Audiences cannot study a visual at their own pace, so the presenter must direct attention selectively.

Prerequisites: [Designing slides](#prsslide-design) (soft), [Saying numbers, formulae and symbols aloud](#prnnumbers-aloud) (soft), [Describing trends and change](#datdescribing-trends), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft)

<a id="prsdelivery-voice"></a>
#### Vocal delivery

`prs.delivery-voice` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Control pace, volume, pausing, thought groups, prominence and intonation so that a presentation is intelligible and easy to follow, prioritising the prosodic features that most affect listeners' understanding over accent reduction. Prosody signals structure, new and contrastive information, and the speaker's engagement with the audience.

Prerequisites: [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence), [Intonation for discourse functions](#prnintonation) (soft), [Developing speech fluency](#prnfluency) (soft)

<a id="prsnonverbal"></a>
#### Non-verbal communication

`prs.nonverbal` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Use gaze, facial expression, gesture, posture and position in the room to support the spoken message, direct attention to visuals and connect with the audience, adapting to the setting and to one's own physical and neurological needs. Non-verbal norms vary across contexts and individuals, so the aim is purposeful, non-distracting use rather than conformity to a single model of 'confident' body language.

Prerequisites: [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience) (soft), [Intercultural communication](#idnintercultural-communication) (soft)

<a id="prsspeaking-from-notes"></a>
#### Speaking from notes

`prs.speaking-from-notes` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Deliver a presentation from brief prompts (key words, cue cards, slide notes) rather than reading a full script or reciting a memorised text, using the notes to retrieve structure and key wording while formulating sentences in the moment, and scripting only selected high-risk segments. Read-aloud written prose is lexically dense and usually prosodically flat, which makes it hard for listeners to process.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Grammar of spoken academic interaction](#grmspoken-grammar) (soft), [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure) (soft), [Developing speech fluency](#prnfluency) (soft)

<a id="prshandling-questions"></a>
#### Handling questions

`prs.handling-questions` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Manage question-and-answer sessions by listening to the whole question, clarifying or reformulating it, answering concisely and relevantly, acknowledging limits honestly, and deferring or redirecting when appropriate, including when the turn is a critical comment rather than a question. Q&A is the least predictable part of a presentation and a key site for demonstrating understanding and establishing credibility.

Prerequisites: [Expressing stance in speech](#staspoken-stance) (soft), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding), [Managing speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate](#semspeaking-anxiety) (soft)

<a id="prsgroup-presentations"></a>
#### Group presentations

`prs.group-presentations` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Plan and deliver a presentation as a group by agreeing a single message and structure, allocating sections by content rather than by person, making handovers that link sections, keeping slides, terminology and referencing consistent, and sharing the Q&A. Assessed group presentations test coordination as well as individual delivery, and a poorly integrated talk often reveals divided rather than shared planning.

Prerequisites: [Signposting in presentations and spoken explanations](#prssignposting), [Rehearsal and timing](#prsrehearsal-timing) (soft), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning)

<a id="prsposters"></a>
#### Academic posters: design and presentation

`prs.posters` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (sciences, health-medicine, engineering, psychology)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Design an academic poster that communicates one main message at a glance, with a headline finding, a clear reading path, economical text, visuals that carry the evidence and legibility at viewing distance, and present it in a poster session through short and longer spoken overviews adapted to each visitor and a conversation that includes questions, critique and follow-up. The poster is a genre with its own norms, not a shrunken paper, and much of its value lies in the conversations it supports.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Talking about visuals and data](#prsexplaining-visuals) (soft), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="prsresearch-presentations"></a>
#### Research and conference presentations

`prs.research-presentations` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Present research to specialist audiences in conference papers, research seminars and dissertation or progression presentations, framing the niche and contribution early, selecting findings and visuals that carry a clear take-home message, keeping to strict time limits and engaging with the discussion session. These presentations socialise researchers into disciplinary communities, and their introductions and discussion phases are particularly consequential for credibility.

Prerequisites: [Opening a presentation](#prsopenings), [Signposting in presentations and spoken explanations](#prssignposting) (soft), [Talking about visuals and data](#prsexplaining-visuals), [Vocal delivery](#prsdelivery-voice) (soft), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Rehearsal and timing](#prsrehearsal-timing), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche) (soft)

<a id="prsrecorded-online"></a>
#### Recorded and online presentations

`prs.recorded-online` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Produce recorded and live online presentations by planning for microphone, camera and screen-sharing, maintaining spoken-style delivery and audience engagement without live feedback, editing within the assessment's rules, and providing accurate captions or transcripts. Recorded formats invite scripting and repeated re-recording, so learners need to manage naturalness, authenticity and compliance with task regulations.

Prerequisites: [Designing slides](#prsslide-design) (soft), [Vocal delivery](#prsdelivery-voice), [Speaking from notes](#prsspeaking-from-notes) (soft)

<a id="prsrehearsal-timing"></a>
#### Rehearsal and timing

`prs.rehearsal-timing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Rehearse a presentation aloud, with timing and under realistic conditions, using recordings and peer feedback to refine content, transitions and delivery, and adjust content in advance so that it fits the time limit. Rehearsal reduces the planning and formulation load of live delivery and is the main safeguard against overrunning.

Prerequisites: [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure), [Developing speech fluency](#prnfluency) (soft), [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft)

<a id="prsresearch-pitches"></a>
#### Short-format research pitches

`prs.research-pitches` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Compress a research project into a short spoken pitch, such as a three-minute thesis-style talk, an elevator pitch at a conference or interview, or a one-slide lightning talk, that a mixed or non-specialist audience can follow: one narrative from problem to contribution, a concrete hook, accessible framing and a clear statement of why the work matters. The format rewards selection and audience adaptation rather than coverage, and it is used in competitions, networking, interviews and funding contexts.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience), [Vocal delivery](#prsdelivery-voice) (soft), [Rehearsal and timing](#prsrehearsal-timing)

### Pronunciation & Fluency

The domain adopts the intelligibility principle (Levis, 2005): the goal is speech that listeners can understand with reasonable effort, not a native-like accent. Building on Munro and Derwing's (1995) distinction between accentedness, comprehensibility and intelligibility, priority goes to the features most likely to affect understanding in academic speaking — word stress in academic vocabulary, thought groups and prominence, intonation for discourse functions, high-functional-load segmentals — together with fluency and repair. Pronunciation is linked to listening, since perception supports production, and to the presentations, seminars and teaching in which students use it; accent is treated as part of speakers' identity rather than as an error to be removed. Listeners share responsibility for understanding, so assessment should judge intelligibility rather than accentedness, and speakers who stammer or have other speech differences should not be assessed against fluency norms they cannot meet.

<a id="prnintelligibility"></a>
#### Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent

`prn.intelligibility` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, pgr: revisit

Distinguish intelligibility (how much of a speaker's message a listener actually understands), comprehensibility (how easy the listener finds it to understand) and accentedness (how different the speech sounds from the listener's reference variety), and use the finding that these are partly independent to set pronunciation goals around being understood rather than around imitating a particular reference accent. Intelligibility depends on listeners as well as speakers, and accent is part of a speaker's identity.

<a id="prnword-stress"></a>
#### Word stress in academic vocabulary

`prn.word-stress` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Place primary stress correctly on multi-syllable academic and technical words, including the shifts that occur across word families (ANalyse, aNALysis, anaLYtical) and the regularities associated with suffixes such as -ic, -ion, -ity and -ical, and reduce unstressed vowels appropriately. Misplaced word stress can cause listeners to misidentify words even when individual sounds are accurate.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology) (soft), [Academic affixes and Greco-Latin roots](#vocaffixes-roots) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnprominence"></a>
#### Sentence stress and prominence

`prn.prominence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Place prominence (nuclear or focal stress) on the word that carries new or contrastive information in each thought group, and de-accent information that is already given, so that listeners can identify what is important and what is being contrasted. Misplaced or absent prominence can lead listeners to misunderstand the point and to recall less of an explanation.

Prerequisites: [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new) (soft), [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress), [Thought groups and pausing](#prnthought-groups)

<a id="prnthought-groups"></a>
#### Thought groups and pausing

`prn.thought-groups` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Divide speech into thought groups — short, meaningful units usually corresponding to grammatical phrases or clauses — with pauses at group boundaries rather than within them and one main prominence in each group. Thought groups help listeners parse speech and give speakers natural planning points; pauses inside phrases make speech harder to follow.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnintonation"></a>
#### Intonation for discourse functions

`prn.intonation` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use pitch movement for discourse purposes in academic speaking: falling tones to present new information and close points, rising or fall-rise tones to refer to shared information, signal continuation or invite response, and pitch range and key to mark new topics and contrasts. Tone choice helps listeners follow organisation and affects how interactive and approachable a speaker sounds.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence)

<a id="prnsegmental-priorities"></a>
#### Prioritised segmental features

`prn.segmental-priorities` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Identify and work on the consonant and vowel features in one's own speech that most affect intelligibility — typically contrasts with high functional load, word-final consonants and clusters that carry grammatical meaning, and vowel length or quality contrasts — rather than trying to perfect every sound. Priorities are individual: they depend on the speaker's other languages, on the listeners and on which features actually cause misunderstanding.

Prerequisites: [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility)

<a id="prnconnected-speech-production"></a>
#### Connected speech in production

`prn.connected-speech-production` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Use linking within thought groups, weak forms of function words where they are unstressed, and natural reductions so that speech flows and stressed information stands out, while keeping content words and key terms clear. Moderate use of connected speech supports fluent delivery; how far full reduction helps intelligibility, particularly in lingua franca settings, is contested.

Prerequisites: [Recognising connected speech](#lisconnected-speech) (soft), [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence) (soft)

<a id="prntechnical-terms"></a>
#### Pronouncing technical terms and names

`prn.technical-terms` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Find, check and practise the pronunciation of discipline-specific terms, proper names (theorists, places, organisations) and acronyms before using them in speech, recognising when an abbreviation is said as a word (UNESCO) or as letters (DNA) and that disciplinary communities may have their own conventions. Because key terms recur throughout an explanation, a mispronounced term can disrupt understanding of the whole.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress)

<a id="prnfluency"></a>
#### Developing speech fluency

`prn.fluency` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Develop speech that flows at a rate appropriate to the audience, with pauses mainly at natural boundaries, few long hesitations inside clauses and efficient recovery, through planning, formulaic language and repeated practice. Fluency here concerns the listener's experience of smooth delivery and the speaker's processing efficiency, not speed for its own sake.

Prerequisites: [Spoken academic formulas](#phrspoken-academic-formulas) (soft), [Thought groups and pausing](#prnthought-groups) (soft), [Self-repair and reformulation](#prnrepair) (soft)

<a id="prnrepair"></a>
#### Self-repair and reformulation

`prn.repair` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Notice when one's own speech has gone wrong or a listener has not understood, and repair efficiently: correcting errors that affect meaning, reformulating, and using compensatory strategies such as paraphrase, description or approximation when a word is unavailable. Effective repair keeps communication going without excessive self-correction of minor errors.

Prerequisites: [Code glosses and exemplification](#stacode-glosses) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnnumbers-aloud"></a>
#### Saying numbers, formulae and symbols aloud

`prn.numbers-aloud` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP (sciences, engineering, mathematics-statistics, economics, business-management, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Say numbers and quantities, formulae, units, dates and symbols aloud accurately and intelligibly — large numbers and decimals, fractions and percentages, ranges and approximations, equations and units — following the conventions of one's discipline, and use the final syllable and stress to keep confusable numbers apart (thirTEEN versus THIRty). Errors with numbers in presentations and seminars can mislead listeners about findings.

Prerequisites: [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions), [Language of mathematical expression](#nummathematical-expression) (soft)

### Group Work & Collaboration

Group projects, assessed group presentations and collaborative reports are common across disciplines, yet students often receive little guidance on how to collaborate, and multilingual students can be positioned as less competent and allocated marginal roles. This domain covers why universities assess collaboration and how groups plan and allocate work, run meetings, negotiate disagreement, write together, and document and evaluate contributions. It treats equitable participation as a shared responsibility of all group members and of course design, not as a deficit of particular students.

<a id="grwpurposes"></a>
#### Purposes of group work

`grw.purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Explain why universities use group work and assessed collaboration — for example to develop teamwork, to tackle tasks too large for individuals and to bring together diverse perspectives — and identify what a given task assesses: the product, the process, individual contributions or a combination. Understanding purposes and assessment design helps learners set realistic expectations and treat group tasks as collaborative learning rather than as work to be divided and stapled together.

Prerequisites: [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="grwroles-planning"></a>
#### Roles and planning

`grw.roles-planning` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Plan a group project by analysing the task, agreeing ground rules (communication channels, response times, decision-making, quality standards), allocating roles and tasks on the basis of expertise and development goals, and setting milestones with internal deadlines, recorded in a shared place. Explicit early planning is intended to reduce later conflict and to ensure that every member, including multilingual members, has a defined and substantive role.

Prerequisites: [Purposes of group work](#grwpurposes), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft)

<a id="grwmeetings"></a>
#### Running group meetings

`grw.meetings` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Run and take part in group meetings by preparing and circulating an agenda, chairing or timekeeping, reaching and recording decisions, and producing brief minutes with action points, owners and deadlines that are followed up at the next meeting. Well-structured meetings make decisions and responsibilities visible and accountable, which matters when contributions are later assessed.

Prerequisites: [Collaborative notes](#ntkcollaborative-notes) (soft), [Facilitating discussion](#semfacilitating) (soft), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning)

<a id="grwconflict"></a>
#### Managing disagreement and conflict

`grw.conflict` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Manage disagreement and conflict in group work by distinguishing disagreements about ideas from problems of process or contribution, raising concerns early and specifically with reference to agreed ground rules, negotiating solutions, and escalating to tutors through appropriate channels with evidence when internal resolution fails. Unaddressed problems tend to surface late, when options are limited.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning) (soft), [Intercultural communication](#idnintercultural-communication) (soft)

<a id="grwinclusive-collaboration"></a>
#### Inclusive collaboration in multilingual groups

`grw.inclusive-collaboration` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Collaborate equitably in multilingual groups by accommodating to varied accents and proficiency levels, creating space for all members to contribute substantively, drawing on members' different linguistic, disciplinary and educational backgrounds, and challenging exclusionary patterns such as allocating tasks by assumed language ability. Equitable participation is a responsibility of every member, including the most proficient users of English, and supports the quality of the group's work.

Prerequisites: [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking) (soft), [Explaining concepts and readings to peers](#semexplaining-concepts) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

<a id="grwcollaborative-writing"></a>
#### Collaborative writing

`grw.collaborative-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (business-management, engineering)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Co-author a group report or document by agreeing a structure and style sheet, using shared documents with comments, suggestion mode and version history, integrating sections into a coherent whole with consistent terminology, voice and referencing, and revising the whole text together. Collaborative writing requires coordination of text, not only division of labour, and must respect the boundary between group work and individually assessed components.

Prerequisites: [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning), [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [Digital collaboration tools](#digdigital-collaboration) (soft)

<a id="grwcontribution-assessment"></a>
#### Assessing contributions

`grw.contribution-assessment` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Evaluate one's own and peers' contributions to group work against agreed criteria, document one's own role with evidence (minutes, version history, task logs), and describe contributions accurately in peer-evaluation forms and individual reflections. Peer evaluation can affect individual marks and can reproduce biases, for example equating fluent talk with contribution, so criterion-referenced, evidenced judgement matters.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Self- and peer-assessment](#fbkself-peer-assessment)

## Academic Writing

### Writing Process

Covers how academic texts are produced rather than what they look like: representing coursework and exam tasks, generating and organising content, drafting, writing from sources, and managing time, language choice and affect across extended projects. It draws on cognitive process models (Flower & Hayes, 1981; Bereiter & Scardamalia, 1987) and L2 composing research (Zamel, 1983), which show that skilled writers plan, draft and review recursively and use writing to develop ideas. Hyland (2003) argues that process pedagogy needs to be complemented by explicit attention to genre and reader expectations, so these topics should be taught alongside PAR, ESS and the genre strands rather than as a free-standing ‘process approach’.

<a id="wprwriting-process"></a>
#### Writing as a recursive process

`wpr.writing-process` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: develop

Understand academic writing as interleaved planning, translating (putting ideas into language) and reviewing, revisited recursively rather than performed once in sequence, and distinguish knowledge-telling (writing down what one knows in the order it is recalled) from knowledge-transforming (reworking content to meet rhetorical goals). The model explains why much of the thinking in academic writing happens during drafting and revision, and why early surface correction can be counter-productive.

<a id="wprtask-analysis"></a>
#### Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks

`wpr.task-analysis` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Unpack a coursework brief or an exam question to identify the command word(s), topic, focus and limiting conditions, implicit sub-questions, genre, reader, required sources, length and marking criteria, and turn these into a working statement of what the text must do; then keep the text answering that task through planning, drafting and revision by checking the thesis, topic sentences and conclusion against it. The same procedure applies under exam conditions, where it must be done quickly. Misreading or drifting from the task is a common, avoidable cause of low marks.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="wprgenerating-ideas"></a>
#### Generating ideas

`wpr.generating-ideas` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Use deliberate techniques—brainstorming, focused freewriting, question heuristics, mapping and talking ideas through—to produce more and better content than first recall provides, and then select from it in the light of the task. Separating generation from evaluation helps writers get beyond the first, most obvious points.

Prerequisites: [Writing as a recursive process](#wprwriting-process) (soft), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft)

<a id="wprplanning-outlining"></a>
#### Planning and outlining

`wpr.planning-outlining` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Turn a position and selected evidence into a hierarchical plan that sequences points, attaches support to each and shows how each part advances the answer, in a format (linear outline, map, table) suited to the writer and task. A plan is a working hypothesis to be revised during drafting, not a contract.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats) (soft), [Generating ideas](#wprgenerating-ideas), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="wprdrafting"></a>
#### Drafting

`wpr.drafting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Produce a complete first draft from a plan, keeping momentum by deferring sentence-level correction, marking gaps to fill later and allowing the argument to develop as it is written. Treating the first draft as provisional makes revision possible and reduces the paralysis of trying to write a final version first time.

Prerequisites: [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft), [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit) (soft)

<a id="wprreading-to-write"></a>
#### Reading to write: from task to source-based text

`wpr.reading-to-write` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Carry out the whole discourse-synthesis process for a writing task: set reading priorities from the task, select sources and sections, read and make notes that record source details and separate quotation, paraphrase and own comment, organise the notes around one's own points, draft from the notes with the sources closed, and connect source content with commentary, revising reading priorities as the argument develops. Most university writing is source-based, and both the quality and the integrity of source use depend on how the reading was done.

Prerequisites: [Managing reading load](#rdpmanaging-reading-load) (soft), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft)

<a id="wpraudience-purpose"></a>
#### Audience and purpose

`wpr.audience-purpose` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Recognise that most assessed university writing addresses an expert reader who knows the content but expects the writer to display understanding, make reasoning explicit and argue a position, and adjust content, explicitness and register to that reader and to the text's purpose. Mismatched assumptions about the reader explain many ‘too descriptive’ or ‘explain this’ comments.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft)

<a id="wprmanaging-long-projects"></a>
#### Managing extended writing projects

`wpr.managing-long-projects` · STRATEGIC · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Plan and sustain projects of several thousand words or more by breaking them into dated milestones, budgeting words and time across sections, writing regularly rather than in a final burst, scheduling feedback, and tracking versions and progress. Extended projects such as pre-sessional research projects, dissertations and theses often falter through poor management rather than weak language.

Prerequisites: [Working within word limits](#wprword-limits) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management)

<a id="wprwriting-blocks"></a>
#### Overcoming writing blocks

`wpr.writing-blocks` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Recognise likely causes of being unable to start or continue writing—perfectionism, rigid rules, an unclear task, anxiety about language or evaluation, fatigue—and apply matched strategies such as freewriting, generative writing, lowering the stakes of drafts, starting with an easier section or breaking the task down. Blocks are common at every level, including among experienced researchers, and are usually solvable.

Prerequisites: [Drafting](#wprdrafting) (soft), [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft)

<a id="wprl1-in-composing"></a>
#### Using the first language in composing

`wpr.l1-in-composing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: develop

Use the first language and other languages strategically while composing in English—for generating ideas, planning, note-making, checking meaning and solving lexical problems—decide, within the language requirements of each task, how much of the text to draft directly in English, and use machine translation transparently and within local rules. Plurilingual composing is a resource. Evidence on drafting fully in another language and then translating is mixed: it can support content and organisation, especially at lower proficiency (Kobayashi & Rinnert, 1992), but may carry over source-language structures, reduce L2 practice and, with machine translation, breach assessment rules.

Prerequisites: [Generating ideas](#wprgenerating-ideas) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="wprword-limits"></a>
#### Working within word limits

`wpr.word-limits` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Plan to a word limit by allocating words across sections in proportion to their importance, cut over-length drafts by removing low-value content before trimming sentences, expand under-length drafts by developing analysis rather than adding background, and follow local conventions on what counts (references, headings, tables, appendices) and on tolerances. Meeting a limit is a matter of prioritisation, not only concision.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft)

<a id="wprformatting"></a>
#### Formatting and presentation

`wpr.formatting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Present a text according to the required conventions—style guide, departmental template, cover sheet, page layout, heading levels, numbering of figures and tables, font and spacing—and apply them consistently using word-processing styles. Presentation rarely carries much weight in marking, but it affects readability, the reader's first impression and sometimes compliance with submission rules.

Prerequisites: [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft)

### Paragraph Development

Covers the paragraph as the main unit through which academic writers develop and support a point: controlling ideas, support and analysis, internal coherence, closure, division, paragraph functions and the integration of sources. Paragraph scaffolds such as topic-sentence models and PEEL are useful entry points for foundation and undergraduate-entry pre-sessional writers, but expert paragraphs vary considerably (Braddock, 1974), so teaching should move learners from templates towards functional judgements about what a paragraph needs to do in its context. Coherence is better taught through information flow and logical relations than through connector lists (Crossley & McNamara, 2012).

<a id="parparagraph-unit"></a>
#### The academic paragraph

`par.paragraph-unit` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: revisit

Understand the academic paragraph as a unit that typically develops one controlling idea, signalled to the reader and developed through explanation and evidence, and that serves the purpose of the whole text. The one-idea paragraph is a reliable default for planning and reading, although expert writers vary paragraph structure according to function and discipline.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft)

<a id="partopic-sentences"></a>
#### Writing topic sentences

`par.topic-sentences` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Write sentences that state a paragraph's controlling idea precisely enough to predict and constrain what follows, and that connect the paragraph to the thesis or the previous paragraph. Effective topic sentences make claims rather than announce topics, and read in sequence they give the reader an outline of the argument.

Prerequisites: [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit)

<a id="parsupport"></a>
#### Developing support

`par.support` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Develop a controlling idea with a suitable combination of explanation, reasoning, evidence, examples and analysis, choosing support that is relevant and sufficient for the strength of the claim and stating how it supports the claim. Paragraphs that assert without support, or pile up examples without explanation, are a frequent cause of ‘underdeveloped’ or ‘descriptive’ feedback.

Prerequisites: [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="parpeel"></a>
#### Point–evidence–explanation–link structures

`par.peel` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: revisit

Use PEEL-type scaffolds (point, evidence, explanation, link, and variants such as PEE or PEEL-C) to construct supported paragraphs while learning, and recognise their limits: many paragraphs need several pieces of evidence, a counter-point or no explicit link, and mechanical application produces formulaic text. The aim is to internalise the functions the scaffold names and then write without it.

Prerequisites: [Developing support](#parsupport), [Concluding and linking sentences](#parconcluding-sentences) (soft)

<a id="parinternal-coherence"></a>
#### Internal paragraph coherence

`par.internal-coherence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Order and connect the sentences of a paragraph so that each follows logically from the previous one and from the controlling idea, using given-before-new information structure, thematic progression, consistent reference and lexical chains, with explicit connectors where a logical relation needs signalling. Coherence depends on logic and information flow far more than on the number of linking words.

Prerequisites: [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences)

<a id="parconcluding-sentences"></a>
#### Concluding and linking sentences

`par.concluding-sentences` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: revisit

Close a paragraph, where useful, with a sentence that draws out the significance of its content for the argument, qualifies it, or prepares the next point, rather than repeating the topic sentence. Not every paragraph needs a separate concluding sentence; the choice depends on the paragraph's length and function.

Prerequisites: [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences)

<a id="parlength-division"></a>
#### Paragraph length and division

`par.length-division` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Decide where to begin a new paragraph according to shifts in point, function or stage of the argument, and keep paragraphs long enough to develop an idea but short enough not to overload the reader, generally avoiding one-sentence paragraphs and page-long blocks in essays. Conventions vary with genre, discipline and medium.

Prerequisites: [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit)

<a id="parparagraph-functions"></a>
#### Paragraph functions

`par.paragraph-functions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit

Recognise and write paragraphs that serve different functions within a text—introductory, body (argument, evidence, counter-argument), transitional and concluding—and understand that each function calls for different internal organisation and language. Treating every paragraph as a body paragraph produces weak introductions, conclusions and transitions.

Prerequisites: [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure)

<a id="parintegrating-evidence"></a>
#### Integrating sources in paragraphs

`par.integrating-evidence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Integrate source material into a paragraph so that the writer's point leads, sources are introduced and attributed, and each piece of evidence is followed by commentary that interprets, evaluates or connects it (introduce–cite–comment). This keeps the writer's voice in control and distinguishes argument from a chain of summaries.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution) (soft), [Developing support](#parsupport), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft)

### Essay Construction

Covers how an essay is built around a central argument: structure, thesis, introductions, body organisation, conclusions, depth of focus, criticality, headings and titles; essay types are treated under ges.genre-families and answering the question set under wpr.task-analysis. The essay is a major assessed genre in many humanities and social science programmes but is only one of many genre families in university writing (Nesi & Gardner, 2012), and the five-paragraph essay taught in many schools and test-preparation courses is a scaffold that does not match university essays in length, structure or source use (Caplan & Johns, 2019; Moore & Morton, 2005). Teaching should centre on argument (Wingate, 2012) and treat structural templates as means to that end.

<a id="essessay-structure"></a>
#### Academic essay structure

`ess.essay-structure` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences, business-management, law, education)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: revisit

Understand how an essay's introduction, body and conclusion relate to a central argument: the introduction frames the question and states the position, the body develops it through a sequence of supported points, and the conclusion draws the argument together. Structure serves the argument, so the number, order and length of sections follow from the line of reasoning rather than from a fixed template.

Prerequisites: [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="essthesis-statement"></a>
#### Thesis statements

`ess.thesis-statement` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences, business-management, law, education)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Write a thesis statement that answers the question with a specific, arguable position, scoped to what the essay can support within its length and evidence, and that may preview the main lines of reasoning. A thesis states what the essay will argue, not merely what it will discuss, and is revised as the argument develops.

Prerequisites: [Hedging and qualifying generalisations](#stahedging) (soft), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure), [Types of claims](#argclaim-types) (soft)

<a id="essintroductions"></a>
#### Writing introductions

`ess.introductions` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences, business-management, law, education)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Write an introduction that establishes the context and relevance of the topic, narrows to the specific focus and any key definitions, states the thesis and, where appropriate, outlines the structure, moving efficiently from general to specific. The introduction sets the reader's expectations for the whole essay and is usually best finalised after the body is drafted.

Prerequisites: [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers) (soft), [Paragraph functions](#parparagraph-functions) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement)

<a id="essconclusions"></a>
#### Writing conclusions

`ess.conclusions` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences, business-management, law, education)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Write a conclusion that synthesises the argument rather than repeating it, gives a direct answer to the question, and indicates implications, limitations or questions for further work without introducing new evidence. The conclusion is where the essay's overall judgement is made fully explicit and calibrated to the evidence presented.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Paragraph functions](#parparagraph-functions) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement)

<a id="essbody-organisation"></a>
#### Organising the essay body

`ess.body-organisation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences, business-management, law, education)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Sequence and group body paragraphs so that they build the argument logically—by importance, causal chain, comparison criteria, or concession and rebuttal—and organise source material thematically around the writer's points rather than source by source. The organisation should be visible to the reader through topic sentences and transitions.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept) (soft)

<a id="essheadings"></a>
#### Headings and sections

`ess.headings` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Judge whether headings are expected, permitted or discouraged in a given essay (practice varies by discipline, length and department) and, where they are used, write informative headings in a consistent hierarchy without letting them replace topic sentences and transitions. Reports and dissertations use headings conventionally; many essay traditions do not.

Prerequisites: [Formatting and presentation](#wprformatting) (soft), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation)

<a id="essdepth-focus"></a>
#### Depth versus breadth

`ess.depth-focus` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Narrow the scope of an essay to a manageable number of points, cases or perspectives so that each can be analysed in depth, and justify the focus explicitly. Covering everything relevant at a superficial level is a common cause of ‘descriptive’ feedback; within a word limit, depth of analysis is usually valued over breadth of coverage.

Prerequisites: [Working within word limits](#wprword-limits) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question) (soft)

<a id="esscriticality"></a>
#### Demonstrating criticality in essays

`ess.criticality` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core

Move beyond describing content to analysing how and why, evaluating the strength of evidence and arguments, comparing perspectives and reaching reasoned judgements, and make these moves visible in the text through evaluative language, concession and explicit reasoning. Criticality is among the qualities markers mention most often, and it is understood somewhat differently across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language) (soft), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="esstitles"></a>
#### Writing titles

`ess.titles` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write informative titles for self-chosen essays, reports, dissertations and articles that indicate the topic, focus and, where appropriate, the argument or method, using conventions such as two-part colon titles, and apply the capitalisation rules of the required style. A title is often the first thing a marker, examiner or database user sees.

Prerequisites: [Punctuation and mechanics beyond the comma](#senpunctuation-mechanics) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft)

### Revising, Editing & Proofreading

Covers how writers improve drafts: distinguishing revision of argument and structure from sentence-level editing and surface proofreading, and the techniques, feedback sources, tools and ethical limits involved. Sommers (1980) found that inexperienced writers tend to treat revision as rewording, so teaching should put global revision and criteria-based self-assessment before editing. The value of written corrective feedback has been contested (Truscott, 1996; Ferris, 1999), but meta-analytic evidence suggests it can improve accuracy (Kang & Han, 2015); learners therefore need routines for targeted self-editing, trained peer review (Lundstrom & Baker, 2009) and critical use of automated tools (see dig.automated-feedback-tools) and of the third-party help their institution permits, which varies between countries and institutions.

<a id="edtrevision-editing-proofreading"></a>
#### Revision, editing and proofreading

`edt.revision-editing-proofreading` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Distinguish three levels of work on a draft—revision (changing argument, content and structure), editing (improving sentences for clarity, accuracy and style) and proofreading (correcting surface errors in the final version)—and sequence them so that effort is not spent polishing text that may later be cut. The distinction underpins efficient self-review, well-targeted feedback requests and institutional rules on third-party help.

Prerequisites: [Writing as a recursive process](#wprwriting-process)

<a id="edtglobal-revision"></a>
#### Global revision

`edt.global-revision` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Revise a draft at whole-text level by re-examining the thesis, the line of argument, the selection and sufficiency of evidence, the order of sections and the fit with the task and reader, and make substantial changes—adding, cutting, moving or rewriting sections—where needed. Global revision usually improves a text far more than sentence-level correction does.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtreverse-outlining"></a>
#### Reverse outlining

`edt.reverse-outlining` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Test the structure of a draft by writing a one-line statement of each paragraph's point (and, optionally, its function), then reading the statements in sequence to find gaps, repetition, misplaced paragraphs, paragraphs with more than one point and mismatches with the thesis. The technique turns a vague sense that ‘something is wrong’ into specific revision decisions.

Prerequisites: [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft)
