<a id="edtstyle-editing"></a>
#### Editing for style and clarity

`edt.style-editing` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Edit sentences to make them clearer, more concise and appropriate in register—bringing subjects and verbs together, reducing wordiness and redundancy, managing information density, choosing precise vocabulary and revising informal or vague expressions—while preserving meaning and the writer's voice. Style editing follows global revision and precedes proofreading.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Clarity and end-weight](#senclarity-end-weight) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtgrammar-editing"></a>
#### Editing for grammatical accuracy

`edt.grammar-editing` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Edit drafts for grammatical accuracy by targeting one's own recurrent error types (for example articles, subject–verb agreement, tense, sentence boundaries) in focused passes, using feedback history, reference resources and personal checklists, rather than reading once for ‘all errors’. Targeted self-editing is more efficient than undirected checking and builds independence from teacher correction.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring recurrent grammatical errors](#grmerror-monitoring), [Sentence boundaries](#sensentence-boundaries) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtproofreading"></a>
#### Proofreading

`edt.proofreading` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Check a final version for surface errors—spelling, typing errors, punctuation, missing or repeated words, formatting, and consistency of spelling variety, terms and numbers—using techniques that counter the tendency to read what one meant rather than what is on the page, such as reading aloud, changing the font or layout, reading in reverse order for spelling, and using a checklist. Proofreading is the last stage, after revision and editing.

Prerequisites: [Spelling conventions and consistency](#vocspelling-conventions) (soft), [Punctuation and mechanics beyond the comma](#senpunctuation-mechanics) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtcriteria-self-assessment"></a>
#### Self-assessing drafts against criteria

`edt.criteria-self-assessment` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Use marking criteria, rubrics, checklists and exemplars to judge the quality of one's own draft, identify the gap between current and target performance, and plan the revisions most likely to close it. Developing this evaluative judgement is central to becoming an independent writer and to making good use of feedback.

Prerequisites: [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria)

<a id="edtpeer-review"></a>
#### Peer review of drafts

`edt.peer-review` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Give specific, criteria-referenced and constructive feedback on a peer's draft, prioritising global issues, and receive and evaluate peer feedback, deciding what to act on. Reviewing others' writing also develops the reviewer's own evaluative judgement and revision skills.

Prerequisites: [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft), [Self-assessing drafts against criteria](#edtcriteria-self-assessment) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="edtproofreading-ethics"></a>
#### Acceptable third-party proofreading

`edt.proofreading-ethics` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand institutional policies on proofreading and editing by third parties (friends, paid proofreaders, editors, AI tools): which kinds of change are typically permitted (surface errors, formatting consistency) and prohibited (rewriting content, argument or structure), what must be disclosed, and how editorial support for theses and publications differs from that permitted for assessed coursework. Crossing these limits can constitute misconduct even when help is well intended.

Prerequisites: [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft)

## Sources & Academic Integrity

### Information Literacy & Source Evaluation

Covers how scholarly knowledge is produced and published, how learners turn an assignment topic into a searchable question, how they search catalogues and databases, and how they judge whether a source is trustworthy and fit for a specific purpose. Evaluation is treated as contextual judgement, in line with the ACRL frame 'Authority Is Constructed and Contextual', rather than as checklist compliance, and lateral reading is included as an empirically grounded alternative to judging a site on its own terms. The language demands are real: generating search terms draws on vocabulary depth and disciplinary terminology, and judging a source depends on reading its genre signals and paratext.

<a id="infscholarly-communication"></a>
#### How scholarly knowledge is produced

`inf.scholarly-communication` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Explain how academic knowledge is made public and checked: peer review, journal articles, monographs and edited books, conference papers, preprints and grey literature, and the versions a single study may pass through. This understanding underpins calibrated judgements about authority, currency and status, for example why a preprint is cited with caution and why a textbook lags behind the journal literature.

Prerequisites: [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft)

<a id="infsource-types"></a>
#### Types of sources

`inf.source-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Classify sources by their relation to the object of study (primary, secondary, tertiary), by the kind of knowledge they offer (empirical study, review, theoretical or conceptual work, practice or policy document) and by their intended readership (scholarly or popular). Classification tells the learner what a source can legitimately support, and it depends on the discipline and question: a newspaper report is a secondary source for a chemist but may be a primary source for a historian.

<a id="infresearch-question"></a>
#### Focusing a topic for searching

`inf.research-question` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Narrow a broad assignment topic into a focused question by specifying context, population, period, variables or perspective, and test whether it is answerable within the word limit and the available literature. A focused question yields usable search concepts and inclusion decisions; an unfocused one produces unmanageable or irrelevant results and descriptive writing.

Prerequisites: [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis)

<a id="infsearch-strategies"></a>
#### Search strategies

`inf.search-strategies` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Build and refine searches by breaking a question into concepts, generating synonyms, spelling variants and broader or narrower terms for each, and combining them with Boolean operators, truncation, phrase searching and filters. Searching is iterative: learners read early results to harvest better terms and revise the strategy rather than typing a whole question into a search box.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question)

<a id="infdatabases"></a>
#### Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools

`inf.databases` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Choose between the library catalogue, the discovery layer, subject databases and Google Scholar according to what each indexes and how it can be searched, and use each tool's features (subject headings, filters, export, citation links, full-text links). Knowing what a tool covers stops learners concluding that 'there is nothing on this topic' after one search in an unsuitable tool.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Search strategies](#infsearch-strategies)

<a id="infsource-evaluation"></a>
#### Evaluating sources

`inf.source-evaluation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Judge whether a source is credible and fit for a specific purpose by weighing its authority, currency, relevance, purpose and evidence base against the task. Evaluation is contextual: the same source may be acceptable as an example of public debate but inadequate as evidence for a causal claim, and checklists help only when applied with judgement.

Prerequisites: [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading) (soft), [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="inflateral-reading"></a>
#### Lateral reading

`inf.lateral-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Evaluate an unfamiliar website, organisation, author or claim by leaving it and opening new tabs to find out what independent, reliable sources say about it, rather than judging it from its appearance and 'About' page. Wineburg and McGrew found that professional fact-checkers worked this way and reached sounder judgements more quickly than historians and students who read vertically within the site.

Prerequisites: [Reading on screen](#rdpdigital-reading) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation)

<a id="infproblematic-publishing"></a>
#### Predatory publishing, retractions and misinformation

`inf.problematic-publishing` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Recognise the main routes by which unreliable material enters the record: deceptive ('predatory') venues that take fees without genuine peer review, published work that is later corrected or retracted, and misinformation that circulates through repeated citation, social media and fabricated references from generative AI tools. Learners need to verify venues and article status, and to understand that publication alone is not proof.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation), [Open access and legal access](#infopen-access) (soft)

<a id="infcitation-chaining"></a>
#### Citation chaining

`inf.citation-chaining` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Use a relevant source as a node in the scholarly conversation: follow its references backward to earlier work and use 'cited by' tools to follow later work forward. Chaining finds sources that keyword searches miss, reveals key works in a debate and shows how a claim has been taken up, extended or challenged.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems) (soft)

<a id="infsearch-records"></a>
#### Recording searches and sources

`inf.search-records` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Keep records that allow searches to be repeated and sources to be found and cited accurately: tools searched, search strings, filters, dates and result counts, together with complete bibliographic details and locators for each source used. Good records prevent lost references and inaccurate citations, and protect against unattributed borrowing when the origin of a note has been forgotten.

Prerequisites: [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft), [Search strategies](#infsearch-strategies)

<a id="infopen-access"></a>
#### Open access and legal access

`inf.open-access` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand the routes by which scholarly work is made freely available (open-access journals with or without author charges, self-archiving in repositories, preprints) and the legitimate ways of obtaining paywalled work (library subscriptions, interlibrary loan, author copies, legal open-access finders). This lets learners find accessible versions, cite the right version and recognise the legal and security problems of unauthorised download sites.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft)

<a id="infsystematic-searching"></a>
#### Systematic and scoping searching

`inf.systematic-searching` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · ESAP (health-medicine, sciences, psychology, social-sciences, education, business-management, computing)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Design, run and document a transparent, reproducible search for a systematic, scoping or other structured review: a protocol with a defined question, sources, full search strings for each database, supplementary methods such as chaining, explicit eligibility criteria and a record of screening decisions. Expectations depend on the field: such searching is standard in health and other evidence-synthesis fields and common in education, psychology, social policy, management and software engineering; writing up the review itself is covered in res.systematic-review-writing.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases), [Citation chaining](#infcitation-chaining) (soft), [Recording searches and sources](#infsearch-records)

### Summarising, Paraphrasing & Quoting

Covers the core textual practices by which writers bring others' words and ideas into their own texts: quoting, paraphrasing and summarising, the reporting language that attributes and evaluates source content, and the signalling that keeps the writer's voice distinct from the sources'. The domain follows research suggesting that source-use problems in L2 writing are often developmental, linked to comprehension, vocabulary and an incomplete sense of why sources are used, rather than to intent to deceive (Howard; Pecorari; Shi; Keck). Instruction therefore pairs language work with purpose: learners transform sources in order to serve their own argument, and close dependence on source wording is treated as something to be taught through, not only policed.

<a id="srcquoting"></a>
#### Quoting

`src.quoting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP (humanities, law, social-sciences)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Decide when direct quotation is warranted (the wording itself is the object of analysis, is a definition or formulation that cannot be bettered, or carries authority in the field) and quote accurately and economically, marking omissions and insertions and giving a locator. Norms differ by discipline: quotation is routine in the humanities and much social science but rare in the natural sciences and engineering.

Prerequisites: [Punctuation and mechanics beyond the comma](#senpunctuation-mechanics), [Rhetorical functions of sources and citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft)

<a id="srcintegrating-quotations"></a>
#### Integrating quotations

`src.integrating-quotations` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP (humanities, law)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Embed quotations grammatically and rhetorically: introduce them with a frame that names the source and signals relevance or stance, fit quoted fragments into the syntax of the surrounding sentence, and follow them with commentary that interprets the quotation and connects it to the writer's point. Unframed, 'dropped' quotations leave the reader to infer their relevance and blur the writer's voice.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs)

<a id="srcparaphrasing"></a>
#### Paraphrasing

`src.paraphrasing` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Restate a specific idea from a source in substantially new wording and sentence structure while preserving its meaning, scope and degree of certainty, and attribute it. Paraphrase lets writers integrate sources into their own argument rather than stitching quotations together, and it depends on full comprehension of the source before reformulation begins.

Prerequisites: [Hedging and qualifying generalisations](#stahedging) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences) (soft), [Paraphrasing techniques](#srcparaphrase-techniques), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft)

<a id="srcparaphrase-techniques"></a>
#### Paraphrasing techniques

`src.paraphrase-techniques` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Control the lexicogrammatical operations used in reformulation: substitution with near-synonyms, word-class change, voice change, nominalisation and its reverse, clause reordering and combining, and change of perspective. Learners also need the limits of each operation: synonym substitution alone yields patchwriting, near-synonyms can shift meaning or strength, and technical terms should usually be kept.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology), [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms) (soft), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation) (soft), [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft)

<a id="srcpatchwriting"></a>
#### Patchwriting

`src.patchwriting` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand patchwriting (reproducing a source's structure and much of its wording with some words deleted, substituted or rearranged) as a common and contested stage in learning to write from sources, distinct from deliberate copying, and use strategies to move beyond it. Howard and Pecorari argue that it often reflects incomplete comprehension and limited linguistic resources rather than intent to deceive, although institutional policies may still classify it as plagiarism.

Prerequisites: [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

<a id="srcsummarising-passage"></a>
#### Summarising a passage

`src.summarising-passage` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Condense a paragraph or short section to its main idea and essential support, in the writer's own words and to a stated length, and attribute it to the source. Passage summary is the building block for note-making, summary–response writing and synthesis, and it depends on identifying main ideas and on reformulation.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas), [Paraphrasing techniques](#srcparaphrase-techniques) (soft), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft)

<a id="srcsummarising-text"></a>
#### Summarising a whole text

`src.summarising-text` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Summarise an article or chapter by building its macrostructure: deleting trivial and redundant material, replacing lists with superordinate terms, and selecting or constructing statements that capture each section's gist (Brown and Day's macrorules). The summary represents the author's purpose, argument and main findings in proportion, using reporting language to show the author's moves.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft)

<a id="srcreporting-verbs"></a>
#### Reporting verbs and signal phrases

`src.reporting-verbs` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Introduce source content with signal phrases (according to X, X argues that, as X notes) and keep attribution going across several sentences, and choose reporting verbs by what the source did (research acts such as find; cognitive acts such as believe; discourse acts such as argue and note) and by the stance they convey towards the claim (accepting, neutral or distancing). Together these are the main resources for showing readers which propositions belong to a source and how far the writer accepts them, and they underpin quotation, paraphrase and summary.

Prerequisites: [Reporting structures](#grmreporting-structures), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft)

<a id="srccitation-forms"></a>
#### Integral and non-integral citation

`src.citation-forms` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Choose between integral citation, where the cited author is an element of the sentence ('Smith (2020) argues that…'), and non-integral citation, where the reference sits in brackets or a number and the information is foregrounded, according to the rhetorical effect required. Author-prominent forms highlight who said something, often to contrast positions; information-prominent forms present content as established or bundle several supporting sources. The balance between forms varies across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft)

<a id="srcvoice-boundaries"></a>
#### Distinguishing own voice from source voice

`src.voice-boundaries` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Make clear where source content begins and ends and where the writer's own interpretation, evaluation or argument takes over, using attribution, citation placement, averral markers and evaluative commentary. Readers need these signals to assign responsibility for claims, and markers judge both originality and integrity by them.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution) (soft), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs)

<a id="srcsecondary-citation"></a>
#### Secondary citation

`src.secondary-citation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Cite a source known only through another author's account transparently (for example with 'as cited in' in APA style), recognise when this is acceptable (the original is unobtainable or in an inaccessible language), and otherwise locate and read the original, since secondary accounts can simplify or misrepresent it. Citing an unread original as if it had been read misrepresents the writer's reading.

Prerequisites: [Citation chaining](#infcitation-chaining) (soft), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft)

<a id="srcmultilingual-sources"></a>
#### Using sources in other languages

`src.multilingual-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use sources written in languages other than English transparently: translate or paraphrase their content accurately, mark one's own translations of quotations, give the original wording where conventions require, and reference the original-language work with a translated title as the style requires. Non-English scholarship can be a valuable resource, especially on local contexts, and using it well depends on clear signalling rather than silent translation.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting) (soft), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft), [Translingual writing and code-meshing](#idntranslingual-writing) (soft)

### Synthesis

Covers discourse synthesis: building a new text that integrates several sources around the writer's own organising ideas rather than summarising them one by one. The domain moves from the concept of synthesis, through tools for organising sources (matrices and thematic grouping), to the language of relating sources, identifying gaps and tensions, and writing synthesis paragraphs, and includes integrated reading- and listening-into-writing tasks in coursework, progression assessment and proficiency tests. Spivey's account of synthesis as organising, selecting and connecting, together with research on integrated writing (Plakans; Plakans and Gebril; Grabe and Zhang), frames synthesis as a constructive reading–writing process rather than a set of linking phrases.

<a id="synsynthesis-concept"></a>
#### What synthesis is

`syn.synthesis-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Understand synthesis as building a new, writer-organised account from several sources (selecting relevant content, organising it around themes or claims, and connecting sources to one another) rather than summarising sources in turn. Synthesis underlies literature reviews and source-based essays, and markers commonly distinguish it from author-by-author description.

Prerequisites: [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft), [Rhetorical functions of sources and citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft)

<a id="synsynthesis-matrix"></a>
#### Synthesis matrices

`syn.synthesis-matrix` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Record sources in a grid, with sources as rows and themes, aims, methods, findings or positions as columns, so that comparisons across sources become visible. The matrix is a note-making and planning tool that supports the shift from source-by-source to idea-by-idea organisation; columns can be set by the teacher or task at first and generated by the learner later.

Prerequisites: [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text) (soft), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept)

<a id="synthematic-grouping"></a>
#### Grouping sources thematically

`syn.thematic-grouping` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Identify themes, issues or positions that cut across a set of sources and group the sources, or parts of them, under those themes so that a text can be organised by idea. Thematic grouping requires classifying by explicit criteria and recognising that one source may contribute to several themes.

Prerequisites: [Classifying and categorising](#funclassifying) (soft), [Reading to write: from task to source-based text](#wprreading-to-write) (soft), [Synthesis matrices](#synsynthesis-matrix) (soft)

<a id="synrelating-sources"></a>
#### Writing relationships between sources

`syn.relating-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Express how sources relate to one another (agreement, corroboration, extension, qualification, contrast and contradiction) using precise reporting verbs, comparison and contrast language, and citation forms that group sources or set them against each other. Making these relationships explicit is what turns a set of citations into synthesis.

Prerequisites: [Comparing and contrasting](#funcomparing-contrasting) (soft), [Reading across multiple texts](#rdcmultiple-texts), [Grouping sources thematically](#synthematic-grouping)

<a id="syngaps-tensions"></a>
#### Identifying gaps and tensions

`syn.gaps-tensions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Identify what a body of literature leaves unresolved (conflicting findings, competing interpretations, methodological limitations, under-studied populations or contexts, and untested assumptions) and express it accurately and with appropriate hedging. Gaps and tensions justify new work and give reviews and essays an argumentative direction, but a claimed gap must be grounded in the literature actually reviewed.

Prerequisites: [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique) (soft), [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources)

<a id="synsynthesis-paragraphs"></a>
#### Writing synthesis paragraphs

`syn.synthesis-paragraphs` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Write paragraphs controlled by the writer's point, in which several sources are brought into dialogue as evidence, contrast or qualification, and the writer's commentary explains what the combination shows. A synthesis paragraph typically opens with the writer's claim, uses grouped and contrasted citations, keeps voice boundaries clear, and closes by drawing out significance for the wider argument.

Prerequisites: [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Integral and non-integral citation](#srccitation-forms) (soft), [Distinguishing own voice from source voice](#srcvoice-boundaries), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources)

<a id="synintegrated-tasks"></a>
#### Integrated source-based tasks in coursework and tests

`syn.integrated-tasks` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop

Complete tasks that require reading and/or listening to sources and then writing (or speaking) a response that selects, transforms and integrates their content, such as summary–response tasks, reading-into-writing progression assessments and the integrated tasks of some proficiency and in-house tests. Success depends on comprehension, selective notes that capture how the sources relate, transformation of source language and organisation around the task's specific demand rather than around the sources; under test conditions the same process must fit strict time limits and the format of the test being taken.

Prerequisites: [Taking notes in lectures](#ntklecture-notes) (soft), [Reading to write: from task to source-based text](#wprreading-to-write) (soft), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept) (soft), [Timed writing](#exmtimed-writing) (soft), [Academic proficiency test formats](#tsttest-formats) (soft)

### Citation & Referencing

Citation is both a technical system and a rhetorical practice. This domain moves from why and when academics cite, through the mechanics of the main referencing systems (author–date, numeric and note-based), locators and reference lists, to the jobs sources and citations do in an argument, how citation varies across disciplines, and whose knowledge citation and indexing practices make visible. Formatting is treated as learnable and checkable, ideally with reference-management tools, so that teaching time goes to the harder judgements: what needs citing, whether a cited source really supports the claim, how citation positions the writer, what disciplinary readers expect, and which perspectives a reference list leaves out.

<a id="citwhy-cite"></a>
#### Why academics cite

`cit.why-cite` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Explain the purposes citation serves in academic writing: giving credit, letting readers verify and follow up claims, showing how a text joins an ongoing scholarly conversation, and positioning the writer in relation to other work. Seeing citation as communicative rather than only as a rule for avoiding penalties underpins every later decision about what, when and how to cite.

<a id="citwhen-to-cite"></a>
#### When to cite

`cit.when-to-cite` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Decide which content requires citation (others' specific ideas, findings, data, wording, visuals, frameworks and lines of argument) and which does not (the writer's own analysis and genuinely common knowledge). Because common knowledge is relative to discipline, readership and level of study, learners need a working procedure for doubtful cases rather than a fixed list.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft)

<a id="citreferencing-systems"></a>
#### Referencing systems and style guides

`cit.referencing-systems` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Distinguish the main families of referencing system — author–date (APA 7, Chicago author–date, Harvard variants), MLA's author–page system, numeric (IEEE, Vancouver) and note-based (Chicago notes and bibliography, OSCOLA) — and recognise that the departmental, module or journal guide, not a universal rule, decides which applies. 'Harvard' has no single authoritative edition, so learners must follow the version their institution specifies.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite) (soft)

<a id="citauthor-date-in-text"></a>
#### In-text citation: author–date

`cit.author-date-in-text` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Format author–date in-text citations accurately in narrative and parenthetical forms — one, two and three or more authors, et al., group authors, several works in one citation, same-author same-year works and secondary citation — following the specified guide (usually APA 7 or an institutional Harvard). Accurate, well-placed in-text citation lets readers move without friction from a claim to its reference entry and see exactly which content is attributed.

Prerequisites: [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citnumeric"></a>
#### Numeric citation systems

`cit.numeric` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · ESAP (engineering, computing, sciences, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Use numeric systems such as IEEE and Vancouver: number sources in order of first citation, reuse a source's number when citing it again, format bracketed or superscript numbers, multiple citations and ranges, and compile the reference list in numerical order. Numeric styles dominate engineering, computing and much of medicine, where cited authors usually stay in the background and naming them is a deliberate choice.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citnote-based"></a>
#### Note-based citation

`cit.note-based` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (law, humanities)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use footnote or endnote systems — Chicago notes and bibliography and, in UK law, OSCOLA — formatting full and shortened notes, pinpoint references, subsequent references and, where required, a bibliography or tables of cases and legislation. Notes are the norm in history, much of the humanities and law; they can also carry brief discursive commentary, which must be kept distinct from citation and from the main argument.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="citreference-list"></a>
#### Compiling a reference list

`cit.reference-list` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Build reference-list entries from their core elements — who, when, what and where, including DOIs or stable URLs — for the source types most often cited, and order, format and label the list as the specified guide requires. Learners need to identify the source type and locate the elements in the source itself rather than copy entries from search results or model lists.

Prerequisites: [Punctuation and mechanics beyond the comma](#senpunctuation-mechanics) (soft), [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citdiverse-sources"></a>
#### Citing diverse source types

`cit.diverse-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Cite sources beyond books and journal articles — web pages, reports and other grey literature, datasets, software, standards, audiovisual material, social media, personal communications and outputs of generative AI tools — by identifying who is responsible, which version was used, when, and how a reader can retrieve it. Where the reader cannot retrieve a source, learners follow how their guide and institution handle it rather than inventing a format.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="citlocators"></a>
#### Page numbers and locators

`cit.locators` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Give the precise location of cited material — page, paragraph, section, timestamp, table or figure — whenever quoting, when the guide requires or recommends it for paraphrase, and when pinpointing a specific claim in a long work. Locators let readers verify exactly what was taken from where; their form differs between systems and source types.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft)

<a id="citaccuracy-consistency"></a>
#### Citation accuracy and consistency

`cit.accuracy-consistency` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Check that every in-text citation has a matching reference entry and vice versa, that names, dates, titles, page ranges and identifiers are correct and consistently formatted, and that each cited source actually supports the claim attached to it. Reference-manager output, database exports and references produced by generative AI tools all need verifying against the source or an authoritative record.

Prerequisites: [Proofreading](#edtproofreading) (soft), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list), [Reference management software](#digreference-managers) (soft)

<a id="citcitation-functions"></a>
#### Rhetorical functions of sources and citation

`cit.citation-functions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C2 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Recognise and use the jobs that sources and citations do in academic texts — attributing an idea, supporting a claim, supplying an example or definition, establishing what is known, contrasting or qualifying positions, justifying a method, evaluating others' work, linking sources, establishing a gap and comparing one's own findings with the literature — and choose sources, citation form and reporting language to do those jobs. Function moves learners from showing that they have read to using reading to build and position their own argument: at foundation level this starts with support, example and definition, and in postgraduate writing it extends to evaluation, linking and comparison with one's own findings.

Prerequisites: [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft), [Integral and non-integral citation](#srccitation-forms) (soft), [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="citdisciplinary-citation"></a>
#### Disciplinary variation in citation

`cit.disciplinary-citation` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, social-sciences, humanities)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Describe how citation practice differs across disciplines — in citation density, use of direct quotation, integral versus non-integral forms, reporting-verb choice and preferred systems — and relate these differences to how each field builds knowledge. Learners use this to read their own discipline's texts as models rather than applying one generic EAP template.

Prerequisites: [Rhetorical functions of sources and citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft), [Disciplinary knowledge-making](#acudisciplinary-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="citcitation-politics"></a>
#### Whose knowledge is cited

`cit.citation-politics` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Recognise that citation, indexing and ranking practices shape whose knowledge becomes visible and treated as authoritative — including the dominance of English-language, Global North and highly indexed journals in databases, reading lists and metrics — and make deliberate, justified choices about what to cite. Learners examine the range of perspectives in a reference list and include relevant scholarship from other regions, languages and communities where it serves the argument, applying the same quality criteria as to any other source.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation) (soft), [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite), [Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy](#idnstandards-legitimacy) (soft)

### Academic Integrity

Academic integrity is taught here as values and practices rather than a list of offences. The domain moves from shared values and an informed understanding of plagiarism (including the developmental status of patchwriting and the contested role of intention) to collusion, contract cheating, reuse of one's own work and research integrity, and to the procedural knowledge learners need: interpreting similarity reports, navigating policies and processes, knowing their procedural rights, and managing pressure without shortcuts. Rules differ between institutions and modules, so teaching centres on locating and reading the rules that apply. Similarity and AI-detection scores are never treated as verdicts, the burden of establishing misconduct lies with the institution, and institutions share responsibility for the conditions (clear briefs, workable deadlines, accessible support) in which integrity is possible.

<a id="intintegrity-values"></a>
#### Values of academic integrity

`int.integrity-values` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Explain the values that underpin academic integrity — honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage, as set out by the International Center for Academic Integrity — and connect them to everyday practices such as acknowledging sources, contributing fairly to group work and reporting data truthfully. A values framing presents integrity as a shared commitment of a scholarly community rather than only a set of prohibitions.

<a id="intplagiarism"></a>
#### Understanding plagiarism

`int.plagiarism` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Explain plagiarism as presenting others' words, ideas, data or work as one's own without adequate acknowledgement, and distinguish its forms: verbatim copying, patchwriting, missing or misleading citation, and borrowing structure or ideas. Learners should also understand why institutions weigh intention differently, why patchwriting is widely regarded as a developmental stage rather than deception, and that notions of textual ownership are historically and institutionally situated rather than universal.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft)

<a id="intrecycling-own-work"></a>
#### Reusing one's own work

`int.recycling-own-work` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Understand when and how one's own previous work may be reused. In coursework, most institutions treat resubmitting previously assessed work for credit as misconduct unless explicitly permitted; in research writing, reuse of text, data or findings (redundant or duplicate publication, text recycling) is governed by institutional, journal and publisher policies. Transparency — citing the earlier work, disclosing overlap and seeking permission where required — is the common principle.

Prerequisites: [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="intcollusion"></a>
#### Collusion and legitimate collaboration

`int.collusion` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Distinguish collusion — unauthorised collaboration on work assessed as individual, such as sharing drafts, answers or code, co-writing, or completing part of someone else's work — from legitimate collaboration such as discussing ideas, study groups, structured peer review and assessed group work. The boundary is set by each assessment's rules, so learners need to read the brief and ask when unsure.

Prerequisites: [Purposes of group work](#grwpurposes) (soft), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft)

<a id="intcontract-cheating"></a>
#### Contract cheating

`int.contract-cheating` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Recognise contract cheating — having a third party complete assessed work, whether paid or unpaid, through essay mills, 'tutoring' or 'editing' services that write content, file-exchange sites, friends or family — and the risks it carries, including detection, serious academic penalties, fraud, loss of personal data and blackmail by providers. Knowing how providers market themselves helps learners resist offers when under pressure.

Prerequisites: [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism)

<a id="intsimilarity-reports"></a>
#### Interpreting similarity reports

`int.similarity-reports` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Read a text-matching (similarity) report critically: it shows overlaps between a submission and indexed material, not plagiarism; matches must be interpreted by type (quoted and cited text, reference entries, common phrases, patchwriting, unattributed copying); and, where permitted, reports can be used formatively to improve source use. An overall percentage has no fixed meaning, and whether misconduct has occurred is decided by people applying policy, not by software.

Prerequisites: [Patchwriting](#srcpatchwriting) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism)

<a id="intpolicy-processes"></a>
#### Integrity policies and processes

`int.policy-processes` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Locate and interpret the integrity regulations that apply: definitions of misconduct, module-level rules, procedures for suspected cases, possible outcomes, and rights to support, representation and appeal. Knowing the process reduces fear, helps learners respond appropriately and in time if concerns are raised, and makes clear that policies and terminology vary between institutions and modules. Learners should also know their procedural rights — to see the evidence, to respond in a language and format they can use (including interpreting, language support or reasonable adjustments where offered), to be accompanied, and to have indicators such as similarity scores, AI-detector output or differences in writing style weighed by people alongside their own account — and that the institution, not the learner, must establish that misconduct occurred.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism), [Interpreting similarity reports](#intsimilarity-reports) (soft), [Formal statements and appeals](#prgformal-statements) (soft)

<a id="intpressure-risks"></a>
#### Managing integrity risk under pressure

`int.pressure-risks` · STRATEGIC · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Recognise the conditions under which shortcuts become tempting — clustered deadlines, illness, caring or work commitments, financial or visa-related pressure, worry about language demands and unclear tasks — and act early: plan workload, request extensions or submit extenuating-circumstances claims through proper channels, use academic and wellbeing support, and ask for clarification rather than guessing or outsourcing. Many of these conditions are shaped by institutions, which are responsible for clear briefs, workable deadline patterns and accessible, well-publicised support; the strategy is the learner's, but responsibility for the conditions is shared.

Prerequisites: [Contract cheating](#intcontract-cheating) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Using academic support services](#lrnsupport-services), [Making requests](#prgrequests) (soft)

<a id="intresearch-integrity"></a>
#### Research integrity

`int.research-integrity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Understand the standards of responsible research: honesty in proposing, conducting and reporting research; the avoidance of fabrication, falsification and plagiarism; questionable research practices such as selective reporting, undisclosed changes to hypotheses or analyses and inappropriate authorship; and data management that makes research verifiable. Research students apply these standards to their own data, records, theses and publications.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

## Argument & Critical Thinking

### Critical Thinking

This domain treats critical thinking as a contested, partly discipline-specific set of practices and dispositions — questioning, weighing evidence, recognising inference patterns and their limits, considering perspectives fairly and reflecting on one's own position — that can be made explicit and taught. It rejects the deficit framing that some groups of learners 'cannot think critically': hesitancy to critique published work is common among novice writers of all backgrounds and is treated as a matter of assessment genres, novice status and licence to question, not as a cognitive or cultural deficit. The domain supplies the reasoning that the Academic Argumentation domain turns into written and spoken argument.

<a id="crtconcept"></a>
#### Critical thinking in academic contexts

`crt.concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Understand critical thinking in university study as a contested cluster of practices and dispositions — questioning claims, weighing evidence, judging reasoning and acknowledging uncertainty — whose concrete form varies by discipline and task. Treating it as explicit, learnable academic practice rather than a personal or cultural trait lets learners see what markers actually reward.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="crtdescription-analysis-evaluation"></a>
#### Description, analysis and evaluation

`crt.description-analysis-evaluation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Distinguish three kinds of academic move — describing what something is or says, analysing how or why it works (its parts, causes, relationships and implications) and evaluating its value, validity or significance against stated criteria — and recognise which moves a task and its markers expect. Most 'too descriptive' feedback signals an imbalance among these moves rather than a lack of knowledge.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Critical thinking in academic contexts](#crtconcept) (soft)

<a id="crtquestioning"></a>
#### Critical questioning and question sets

`crt.questioning` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Habitually interrogate claims, texts, data and one's own ideas with questions such as how do we know, according to whom, compared with what, under what conditions and so what; when reading, apply a compact question set to a text's purpose and audience, claims, evidence and reasoning, assumptions and perspective, omissions, and usefulness for the reader's purpose. Question sets are scaffolds to be adapted to the discipline and task rather than checklists, and questioning can be practised as a deliberate routine before it becomes a habit of mind.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation) (soft)

<a id="crtevidence-types"></a>
#### Types and strength of evidence

`crt.evidence-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Recognise the main kinds of evidence used in academic argument — experimental and statistical data, qualitative accounts, documentary and textual evidence, expert testimony and anecdote — and judge what each can and cannot establish for a particular claim. Evidence hierarchies, such as those used in evidence-based medicine, are useful but field-bound tools, not universal rankings.

Prerequisites: [Distinguishing claims, evidence and their status](#rdsfact-opinion-claim), [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft)

<a id="crtreasoning-types"></a>
#### Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning

`crt.reasoning-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Recognise and use the main patterns of inference: deduction, in which the conclusion follows necessarily if the premises are true; induction, which generalises or predicts from observed cases with probability rather than certainty; and abduction, which infers the most plausible explanation for observations. Knowing which pattern an argument uses tells the reader what degree of certainty it can claim and how it might fail.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="crtcorrelation-causation"></a>
#### Correlation and causation

`crt.correlation-causation` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (sciences, health-medicine, psychology, social-sciences, economics)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Distinguish associations between variables from causal relationships by considering confounding, reverse causation, chance and selection effects, and know in broad terms which research designs (randomised experiments, natural experiments, controlled longitudinal designs) can support causal claims. This underpins accurate reading and reporting of research and the choice of causal verbs in writing.

Prerequisites: [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary) (soft)

<a id="crtfallacies"></a>
#### Recognising fallacies

`crt.fallacies` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Recognise common informal fallacies — straw man, ad hominem, false dilemma, hasty generalisation, slippery slope, appeal to popularity and post hoc reasoning — as misuses of otherwise legitimate argument patterns, and explain what makes a particular instance fallacious in its context. The aim is diagnostic precision when evaluating and constructing arguments, not label-spotting.

Prerequisites: [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="crtcognitive-biases"></a>
#### Cognitive biases

`crt.cognitive-biases` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand how systematic tendencies in human judgement — especially confirmation bias and motivated reasoning, but also availability and anchoring — shape what we search for, notice, believe and report, and adopt procedural safeguards in reading and research, such as seeking disconfirming evidence, setting criteria in advance and inviting others' scrutiny. Awareness alone rarely removes bias; routines help.

Prerequisites: [Critical questioning and question sets](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtmultiple-perspectives"></a>
#### Considering multiple perspectives

`crt.multiple-perspectives` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Identify the range of defensible positions on an issue and represent each fairly — including those one rejects — in terms its proponents would recognise, before weighing them against explicit criteria. Fair representation is a precondition for credible counter-argument, synthesis and discussion writing, and protects against straw-man reasoning.

Prerequisites: [Identifying writer stance and attributed views](#rdswriter-stance) (soft), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft), [Critical questioning and question sets](#crtquestioning) (soft)

<a id="crtepistemic-beliefs"></a>
#### Beliefs about knowledge

`crt.epistemic-beliefs` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Reflect on and develop one's beliefs about the nature of knowledge — its certainty, simplicity, source and justification — drawing on models, developed largely with US undergraduates, that describe a broad tendency rather than fixed stages: from treating knowledge as fixed facts transmitted by authorities, through treating all views as equally valid, towards evaluating claims as better or worse supported. These beliefs shape how learners read conflicting sources, respond to open questions and feedback, and build arguments.

Prerequisites: [Critical thinking in academic contexts](#crtconcept) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtevaluating-theories"></a>
#### Evaluating theories and models

`crt.evaluating-theories` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Evaluate a theory, model or framework against criteria such as explanatory power, scope, empirical support, internal coherence, parsimony and usefulness for a stated purpose, and compare rival frameworks on those criteria. This moves learners from reporting what a theory says to justifying why they use, adapt or reject it.

Prerequisites: [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory) (soft)

<a id="crtapplying-theory"></a>
#### Applying theory to cases

`crt.applying-theory` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (business-management, law, social-sciences, education)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Use concepts, theories or models as analytical tools to explain a case, dataset, text or situation: selecting a relevant concept, mapping its components onto specific evidence, and drawing out what the analysis reveals, including where the case does not fit. Application is the core move in case studies, problem questions and many dissertation analyses.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="crtproblem-solving"></a>
#### Analytical problem-solving

`crt.problem-solving` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP (engineering, business-management, computing)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Define a problem precisely (its symptoms, causes, stakeholders and constraints), generate a range of possible responses, evaluate them against explicit criteria such as effectiveness, feasibility, cost and ethics, and justify a recommendation with its trade-offs acknowledged. This process underlies problem–solution essays, case analyses, design reports and policy writing.

Prerequisites: [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution) (soft), [Critical questioning and question sets](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtreflexivity"></a>
#### Reflexivity and positionality

`crt.reflexivity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP (social-sciences, education, health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Recognise how one's own position — background, experiences, values, languages, disciplinary training and relationship to participants — shapes the questions asked, the evidence noticed and the interpretations made, and account for this transparently. Reflexivity is expected in qualitative and practice-based research and increasingly in reflective and critical genres across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Cognitive biases](#crtcognitive-biases) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs)

### Academic Argumentation

This domain treats argument as the organising purpose of most assessed academic writing and much academic talk: taking a position, supporting claims with evidence and explicit reasoning, anticipating and answering objections, and sequencing sub-claims into a cumulative case. Hirvela (2017) argues that L2 writing pedagogy has given argumentation less attention than its centrality warrants, and Wingate (2012) found that students often receive feedback on 'argument' without being shown what it consists of. Toulmin's model, argumentation schemes and argument mapping are offered as analytical tools rather than templates, and disciplinary variation in what counts as a good argument (Andrews, 2010) is made explicit.

<a id="argargument-concept"></a>
#### What counts as an academic argument

`arg.argument-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Understand an academic argument as a reasoned position on a question, developed through claims supported by evidence and explicit reasoning and responsive to other views — not a quarrel, a one-sided opinion or a well-organised collection of facts. Grasping this reframes most assessed writing as argument and explains feedback such as 'What is your argument?'

Prerequisites: [Distinguishing claims, evidence and their status](#rdsfact-opinion-claim) (soft)

<a id="argclaim-types"></a>
#### Types of claims

`arg.claim-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Distinguish claims of fact (what is the case), definition (what something is or should be classed as), cause (what produces what), value (how good, important or right something is) and policy (what should be done), and recognise that each type calls for different kinds of support. Identifying the claim type an assignment question calls for clarifies what evidence and reasoning the answer needs.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argtoulmin"></a>
#### Toulmin model of argument

`arg.toulmin` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Use Toulmin's model — claim, data or grounds, warrant, backing, qualifier and rebuttal — to analyse how a single argument works and where it is vulnerable. Its value in EAP lies in making visible the usually implicit link (warrant) between evidence and claim and the role of qualification and exceptions, not in providing an essay template.

Prerequisites: [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argsupporting-claims"></a>
#### Supporting claims

`arg.supporting-claims` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Link each claim to evidence and reasoning that are relevant, sufficient and appropriate to the claim's type and strength — drawing on sources, data, examples and explanation — and make clear how each piece of support bears on the claim. This is the basic unit of argumentative writing and speaking, from foundation paragraphs to thesis chapters.

Prerequisites: [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argwarrants"></a>
#### Making reasoning explicit

`arg.warrants` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

State, where readers need it, the reasoning that connects evidence to claim — the principle, mechanism, assumption or criterion that makes the evidence relevant — and support that reasoning when it may be contested. Explicit warrants distinguish analysis from the juxtaposition of facts and claims, especially for readers who do not share the writer's assumptions.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Toulmin model of argument](#argtoulmin), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argcounterarguments"></a>
#### Anticipating counter-arguments

`arg.counterarguments` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Identify the strongest objections, alternative explanations and contrary evidence that a knowledgeable reader could raise against one's position, and decide which must be addressed in the text. Anticipating objections is a feature of well-developed argument and of higher-rated student essays, but most writers, in any language, rarely do it without explicit teaching.

Prerequisites: [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argrebuttal-concession"></a>
#### Rebuttal and concession

`arg.rebuttal-concession` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Respond to counter-arguments by conceding what is valid, rebutting what is not with evidence or reasoning, and qualifying one's own position where necessary, using language that acknowledges the other view while keeping the writer's stance dominant. Well-managed concession and rebuttal build credibility and show dialogic engagement with the field.

Prerequisites: [Opening and closing space for other voices](#stadialogic-positioning) (soft), [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language), [Anticipating counter-arguments](#argcounterarguments)

<a id="argposition"></a>
#### Taking and sustaining a position

`arg.position` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Commit to a defensible, appropriately qualified position on a question, state it where the genre expects (often in the introduction) and maintain it consistently so that every section of the text contributes to it. A sustained position is what readers and marking criteria most often mean by 'argument' and distinguishes argumentative from merely informative writing.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argextended-argument"></a>
#### Structuring an extended argument

`arg.extended-argument` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Sequence sub-claims, evidence and responses to objections into a cumulative case in which each section advances the main position and depends logically on what precedes it, with signposting that makes the argumentative structure visible. This is required in long essays, dissertations and theses, where structures organised by topic or by source often fail to build an argument.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession) (soft), [Taking and sustaining a position](#argposition), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

<a id="argrhetorical-appeals"></a>
#### Rhetorical appeals

`arg.rhetorical-appeals` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Recognise and use appeals to reason (logos), to the writer's credibility (ethos) and to the reader's values and emotions (pathos) as they operate in academic contexts, where logos dominates but credibility is built through accurate citation, calibrated claims and disciplinary language, and value appeals are usually restrained. Recognising appeals helps learners read persuasive texts critically and adjust persuasion for academic and public audiences.

Prerequisites: [Metadiscourse](#stametadiscourse) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argargument-mapping"></a>
#### Argument mapping

`arg.argument-mapping` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Represent the structure of an argument as a diagram of claims, reasons, co-premises and objections, showing how each supports or challenges another, in order to analyse a source's reasoning or to plan one's own. Mapping externalises reasoning that is otherwise held in working memory, making gaps, unstated premises and weak links visible.

Prerequisites: [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims) (soft)

<a id="argoral-argument"></a>
#### Arguing orally

`arg.oral-argument` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Construct, support and defend arguments in real-time academic talk — seminars, tutorials, Q&A sessions and supervision — by stating positions concisely, supporting them with reasons and sources, responding to challenges, and conceding or refining positions as discussion develops. Oral argument differs from written argument in its pace, its co-construction across speakers and its reliance on mitigation to maintain collegiality.

Prerequisites: [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession) (soft)

<a id="argexploratory-adversarial"></a>
#### Exploratory and adversarial argument

`arg.exploratory-adversarial` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Distinguish argument as collaborative, open-ended inquiry — exploring a question, testing ideas and reaching provisional conclusions — from argument as persuasive advocacy for a settled position, and recognise which orientation a genre, task or interaction calls for. Many academic genres and events (discussion essays, literature reviews, seminars) are largely exploratory, whereas others (debates, proposals, policy briefs) are more adversarial.

Prerequisites: [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argevaluating-arguments"></a>
#### Evaluating arguments

`arg.evaluating-arguments` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Judge the quality of an argument by assessing whether its premises are acceptable, relevant to the conclusion and jointly sufficient to support it, whether its warrants hold, and how well it deals with objections — and express that judgement in measured academic language.
