This is the core analytical move in critical reviews, literature reviews and peer review.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation) (soft), [Recognising fallacies](#crtfallacies) (soft), [Making reasoning explicit](#argwarrants), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

## Academic Genres

### Essay & Examination Genres

This domain covers the essay, examination, review and source-based genres most often assessed in foundation, pre-sessional and taught programmes, including the extended source-based project that caps many pre-sessional and foundation courses, together with the genre concepts and analytic strategies learners need to handle genres they have not been explicitly taught. Comparison, explanation and problem–solution essays are treated as rhetorical patterns within the essay family rather than as separate genres, and each genre topic integrates component skills from the writing, sources, argument, discourse and reading strands. Because conventions vary by discipline, department and assignment brief, every topic stresses analysis of local exemplars, briefs and marking criteria rather than a single template; the domain draws on the ESP, Sydney School and New Rhetoric genre traditions and on corpus evidence about genre families in UK student writing.

<a id="gesgenre-concept"></a>
#### What a genre is

`ges.genre-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Understand a genre as a recognisable, purpose-driven type of communicative event (an essay, a lab report, a case study, an email to a tutor) whose typical stages, content and language are shaped by the shared purposes and expectations of a discourse community. Seeing conventions as motivated responses to purpose, reader and context, rather than as arbitrary rules, lets learners explain why texts differ across modules and adapt to genres they have not been taught.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft)

<a id="gesgenre-analysis"></a>
#### Analysing genres independently

`ges.genre-analysis` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Analyse exemplars of a target genre, alongside the assignment brief and marking criteria, to identify its purpose, stages or moves, typical content and characteristic language, and turn these observations into decisions for one's own text. This rhetorical consciousness-raising lets learners master genres an EAP course cannot cover and protects them against a generic model that the local department does not use.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [What a genre is](#gesgenre-concept)

<a id="gesgenre-families"></a>
#### Genre families and essay types in university assessment

`ges.genre-families` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Know the range of genre families in which students are assessed (essays, critiques, explanations, exercises, case studies, literature surveys, methodology recounts such as lab reports, research reports, proposals, design specifications, problem questions, narrative or reflective recounts, and empathy writing for non-specialist or professional readers) and, within the essay family, the main essay types (exposition or argument, discussion, factorial and consequential explanation, comparison, problem–solution and challenge or commentary essays), together with how each purpose shapes the thesis, organisation and conclusion. This map helps learners recognise what an assignment is asking for, see that the essay is not the dominant genre in many disciplines, and plan for questions that blend types; labels vary across departments, courses and textbooks, so the brief and criteria outrank any label.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure) (soft), [What a genre is](#gesgenre-concept), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="gesargumentative-essay"></a>
#### Argumentative essay

`ges.argumentative-essay` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Produce a thesis-led essay that takes a defensible position on a contested question and builds a cumulative, source-supported case for it, engaging with alternative views and qualifying the position where the evidence requires. Typical stages are an introduction that frames the issue and states the thesis, body paragraphs that each advance a reason with evidence and commentary, treatment of counter-positions, and a conclusion that answers the question in the light of the argument; characteristic language includes stance markers, hedges and boosters, concession–counter patterns and evaluative reporting of sources.

Prerequisites: [Integrating sources in paragraphs](#parintegrating-evidence), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [Taking and sustaining a position](#argposition), [Structuring an extended argument](#argextended-argument) (soft)

<a id="gesdiscussion-essay"></a>
#### Discussion essay

`ges.discussion-essay` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Produce an essay that represents two or more perspectives on an issue fairly, weighs them against evidence and explicit criteria, and reaches a reasoned, qualified conclusion. Typical stages are statement of the issue, presentation of perspectives (in blocks or point by point), evaluation and judgement; characteristic language includes attribution and reporting verbs, contrastive connectors, concession and evaluative lexis. In UK higher education even a 'discuss' essay is usually expected to arrive at a position, and often to signal it early.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Integrating sources in paragraphs](#parintegrating-evidence) (soft), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives)

<a id="gesexam-essay"></a>
#### Examination essay

`ges.exam-essay` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write an essay under timed, usually closed-book conditions that answers the set question directly from revised knowledge, with a clear position, a quickly planned structure and selective use of remembered evidence. Compared with coursework essays, examiners typically expect a compact introduction, sustained relevance and less precisely referenced sources; expectations about citation, length and structure vary by department and should be checked in past papers, examiners' comments and module guidance.

Prerequisites: [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft), [Planning revision](#exmrevision-planning) (soft), [Timed writing](#exmtimed-writing)

<a id="gesshort-answer"></a>
#### Short-answer responses

`ges.short-answer` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP (sciences, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Write concise, accurate answers, from a sentence to a paragraph, that define, identify, explain or briefly justify, as required in coursework exercises, short-answer exam questions and in-class tests. Markers reward precision, correct terminology and completeness relative to the marks available rather than introductions or elaboration; typical language includes formal definitions, compact causal and procedural explanation, and dense noun phrases.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft)

<a id="gescritique"></a>
#### Critical review of an article

`ges.critique` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (psychology, health-medicine, education, social-sciences)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a review of a single research article or theoretical text that summarises its aims, approach and main claims accurately and concisely, then evaluates it against appropriate criteria (such as quality of evidence, methodological soundness, coherence of argument, contribution and relevance) and reaches a balanced overall judgement. Typical stages are context and purpose, summary, evaluation of strengths and limitations, and conclusion; characteristic language includes evaluative reporting verbs, mitigated criticism, praise–criticism pairs and hedged judgements.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique) (soft), [Summary–response paper](#gessummary-response) (soft)

<a id="gesbook-review"></a>
#### Book review

`ges.book-review` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP (humanities, social-sciences)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write a review of a scholarly book for an academic readership that situates the book in its field, describes its aims, scope and structure selectively, evaluates its argument, evidence and contribution, and recommends it, or not, to specified readers. Unlike an article critique set as coursework, a published book review is a public evaluative act within a discipline: reviewers balance and often mitigate criticism, and follow venue conventions on length, structure and bibliographic header.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Critical review of an article](#gescritique)

<a id="gesreflective-writing"></a>
#### Reflective writing

`ges.reflective-writing` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (education, health-medicine, business-management)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write structured reflection that describes a significant experience selectively, examines one's responses and assumptions, analyses the experience using relevant theory or evidence, and draws conclusions for future practice or learning. Assessed in education, health, business, engineering and EAP itself, the genre combines first-person, past-tense recount with present-tense analysis and future-oriented action planning; markers usually reward depth of analysis and links to theory rather than description or disclosure, and briefs differ widely on structure and citation.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory) (soft), [Reflective learning logs](#lrnreflective-logs) (soft)

<a id="gesannotated-bibliography"></a>
#### Annotated bibliography

`ges.annotated-bibliography` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Compile an annotated bibliography in which each correctly formatted reference is followed by a concise annotation that summarises the source's purpose, approach and main findings or argument and, depending on the brief, evaluates its quality and explains its relevance to the writer's project. The genre isolates and trains selective summarising, source evaluation and relevance judgement; annotations are usually short paragraphs, report the source's claims with present-tense reporting verbs and mark evaluative and relevance comments as the writer's own.

Prerequisites: [Reading to write: from task to source-based text](#wprreading-to-write) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="gesliterature-review-assignment"></a>
#### Literature review assignment

`ges.literature-review-assignment` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write a coursework literature review, stand-alone or as a section of a larger assignment, that maps and synthesises the literature on a defined question thematically, evaluates the strength and limits of the evidence, identifies agreements, tensions and gaps, and positions a question or argument in relation to them. Typical stages are scope and rationale, thematic sections in which sources are placed in dialogue, and a conclusion summarising the state of knowledge and its gaps; characteristic language includes varied integral and non-integral citation, evaluative reporting verbs, comparison, and the present perfect for the state of a field. This topic covers coursework reviews set on taught programmes (pre-sessional PG, undergraduate and taught master's modules); the review that positions the writer's own study in a thesis, article or dissertation chapter is res.literature-review, which normally builds on this one.

Prerequisites: [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions), [Writing synthesis paragraphs](#synsynthesis-paragraphs), [Annotated bibliography](#gesannotated-bibliography) (soft)

<a id="gessummary-response"></a>
#### Summary–response paper

`ges.summary-response` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: develop

Write a paper that first summarises a source text accurately and even-handedly, then responds to it with a reasoned position (agreeing, disagreeing, qualifying or applying it to another context) supported by reasons and evidence. The genre makes explicit the boundary between reporting another writer's ideas and evaluating them and is a common bridge from reading to source-based argument; characteristic language includes attributive signal phrases and reporting verbs in the summary, and stance markers and concession in the response.

Prerequisites: [Formulating a critical response](#rdccritical-response), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Distinguishing own voice from source voice](#srcvoice-boundaries)

<a id="gesexpository-essays"></a>
#### Expository essays organised by rhetorical pattern

`ges.expository-essays` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: develop

Produce essays, or essay sections, whose organisation is driven mainly by one rhetorical pattern: explanation of causes, consequences or a mechanism; comparison of theories, cases, methods or policies against explicit criteria; or problem–response–evaluation leading to a justified recommendation. These are treated as patterns used within the essay family (and inside explanations, case studies and reports) rather than as separate genres, because departments rarely name them and real questions often combine them; each pattern still needs a controlling claim that says what the explanation, comparison or evaluation shows. The topic builds on the corresponding rhetorical functions (cause and effect, comparing and contrasting, problem–solution and evaluating), adding whole-text staging, source support and calibrated claims.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process) (soft), [Comparing and contrasting](#funcomparing-contrasting), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Integrating sources in paragraphs](#parintegrating-evidence) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation) (soft), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft)

<a id="gesextended-source-based-project"></a>
#### Extended source-based project

`ges.extended-source-based-project` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core

Plan, research and write an extended essay or report (typically 2,000–4,000 words in UK pre-sessional and foundation programmes) on a self-chosen, focused question in or near the learner's target field, using sources the learner has found and evaluated, and develop it through staged submissions such as a topic plan, annotated sources, drafts discussed in tutorials and, often, a reflective commentary on the process. Unlike a short source-based essay, the genre assesses sustained management of a question, a body of sources and a drafting cycle, and it is the capstone and frequently the main progression evidence of many pre-sessional and foundation EAP courses. It is normally source-based rather than empirical (projects with primary data draw on grp.empirical-project-report), and length, format and weighting vary by institution and context.

Prerequisites: [Managing extended writing projects](#wprmanaging-long-projects) (soft), [Integrating sources in paragraphs](#parintegrating-evidence), [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision), [Search strategies](#infsearch-strategies), [Distinguishing own voice from source voice](#srcvoice-boundaries), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept), [Citation accuracy and consistency](#citaccuracy-consistency), [Annotated bibliography](#gesannotated-bibliography) (soft), [Acting on feedback](#fbkacting-on-feedback) (soft)

### Report & Practice-Based Genres

This domain covers report-based, practice-based and professional genres in which students document investigations, analyse cases, justify designs, propose projects, summarise for decision-makers, reflect on practice and write for non-academic readers. Most are discipline-specific in their sections, terminology and evidence, so each topic sets out common purposes, stages and language while stressing that lab manuals, templates, handbooks and assignment briefs override any generic model. The domain draws on genre-family research on UK student writing, analyses of particular student genres such as the lab report and the business case report, and research on reflective writing.

<a id="grpreport-conventions"></a>
#### Report conventions

`grp.report-conventions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Understand how academic and professional reports typically differ from essays: they respond to a defined problem, question or brief for a specified reader, are divided into numbered, headed sections that support selective reading, separate findings from interpretation, integrate captioned tables and figures, and often end with conclusions and recommendations. Section names and order vary widely across lab, project, business, technical and policy reports, so learners need to read the brief and departmental templates rather than assume a single report format.

Prerequisites: [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure) (soft), [Genre families and essay types in university assessment](#gesgenre-families) (soft), [Figure and table captions](#datcaptions-labelling) (soft)

<a id="grplab-report"></a>
#### Laboratory report

`grp.lab-report` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a laboratory report that records and interprets a practical investigation, typically with an introduction stating the relevant theory and the aim or hypothesis, a method, results presented in tables and figures with data commentary, a discussion that interprets the results against theory and expected values and evaluates sources of error and uncertainty, and a conclusion. Although it resembles the IMRaD research article, the student lab report usually serves to demonstrate understanding of established theory, procedure and data handling, often for an experiment with a known outcome, and its sections, tense and voice conventions vary with discipline, module and lab manual.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft), [Referring to visuals in text](#datreferring-to-visuals), [Language of experimentation](#dsehypothesis-language) (soft)

<a id="grpempirical-project-report"></a>
#### Empirical project report

`grp.empirical-project-report` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a report of a small-scale empirical study that the student has designed and conducted (for example a survey, interview study, observation or small experiment), presenting the rationale and research questions, a focused review of literature, methods with justification and ethical considerations, findings, a discussion relating findings to the literature, limitations and conclusions. Common in undergraduate and master's research modules and in some postgraduate pre-sessional projects, the genre apprentices learners into research reporting; most pre-sessional projects are source-based rather than empirical (see ges.extended-source-based-project), and sections, length and quantitative or qualitative conventions vary by discipline and brief.

Prerequisites: [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment) (soft), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions), [Wording survey and interview questions](#resinstrument-wording) (soft), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft)

<a id="grpcase-study"></a>
#### Case study analysis

`grp.case-study` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (business-management, health-medicine, education, law, social-sciences)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a case study analysis that applies disciplinary concepts, theories or frameworks to a real or hypothetical case (an organisation, patient, client, project, school or event) to diagnose the key issues, evaluate options and, usually, recommend action. Typical stages are a brief case overview, identification and prioritisation of issues, analysis through theory or frameworks, evaluation of options, and recommendations, sometimes with implementation; the text may be report-like with headings or essay-like, and conventions differ sharply across business, health, law, education and engineering.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Analytical problem-solving](#crtproblem-solving), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions) (soft)

<a id="grpbusiness-report"></a>
#### Business report

`grp.business-report` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (business-management, finance-accounting, economics)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a business report that investigates an organisational problem or opportunity for a specified decision-maker, analyses relevant evidence and data (often with management frameworks), presents findings and conclusions, and makes prioritised, feasible recommendations. Typical sections include an executive summary, introduction with terms of reference, findings or analysis, conclusions, recommendations and appendices; because the reader is assumed to be busy and decision-oriented, front-loading, informative headings and visuals matter, and assignment briefs often simulate a client or manager.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending), [Analytical problem-solving](#crtproblem-solving) (soft), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft)

<a id="grptechnical-design-report"></a>
#### Technical and design report

`grp.technical-design-report` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (engineering, computing, arts-design)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write a technical or design report that documents an engineering, computing or design project: the problem and requirements or specification, constraints, generation and evaluation of options against criteria, the selected design and its justification, implementation, testing or validation against requirements, and evaluation with recommendations for further work. The reader must be able to follow, assess and potentially reproduce decisions, so the genre relies on precise technical description, requirement and specification language, comparative evaluation, and close integration of diagrams, tables and calculations.

Prerequisites: [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Referring to visuals in text](#datreferring-to-visuals) (soft)

<a id="grpproject-proposal"></a>
#### Project proposal (student)

`grp.project-proposal` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write a proposal for a taught-programme project or dissertation (final-year undergraduate or taught master's) that defines the topic and research questions or aims, justifies them in relation to the literature and a gap or practical need, outlines methods and data sources, addresses ethics and feasibility, and presents a realistic timeline. The proposal persuades a supervisor or module team that the project is worthwhile, answerable and achievable within a fixed taught timetable; characteristic language includes future and conditional forms, purpose and justification expressions, and hedged statements about expected outcomes. Doctoral, MPhil and funding proposals, which must argue for an original contribution to a review panel, are covered by res.research-proposal; the short topic plan agreed at the start of a pre-sessional project belongs to ges.extended-source-based-project.

Prerequisites: [Managing extended writing projects](#wprmanaging-long-projects) (soft), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions)

<a id="grpexecutive-summary"></a>
#### Executive summary

`grp.executive-summary` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (business-management, engineering, social-sciences)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write an executive summary that lets a decision-maker grasp a report's purpose, key findings, conclusions and recommendations without reading the full document. It stands alone, usually appears at the front, is short relative to the report, orders content by importance to the reader rather than by the report's section order, and states recommendations explicitly; unlike an academic abstract, it foregrounds implications and actions over method.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions)

<a id="grppolicy-brief"></a>
#### Policy brief

`grp.policy-brief` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · ESAP (social-sciences, economics, health-medicine, education)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write a short, evidence-based document that presents a policy problem, summarises and weighs the relevant evidence, sets out options with their trade-offs and recommends action to a non-specialist policy audience such as officials, practitioners or NGOs. Typical features are a front-loaded set of key messages, a concise problem statement, accessible synthesis of research with its strength signalled, options, and concrete recommendations addressed to named actors, presented in a few pages with headings and visuals; tone is authoritative but calibrated to the evidence, and organisations use their own templates.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Recommending](#funrecommending), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Executive summary](#grpexecutive-summary) (soft)

<a id="grpprofessional-portfolio"></a>
#### Professional practice portfolio

`grp.professional-portfolio` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (health-medicine, education, social-sciences, engineering, business-management)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Compile and write a professional practice portfolio for a placement, practicum or work-based module: dated logs of activities, reflective accounts of significant incidents, and evidence mapped to professional standards or competencies, held together by a commentary that synthesises development and plans further learning. Assessed in nursing and allied health, teacher education, social work, engineering and business placements, the genre combines factual recording, reflection and evidence-based argument, requires strict attention to confidentiality, and follows highly specific institutional and professional-body templates.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing), [Reflective learning logs](#lrnreflective-logs) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria) (soft)

<a id="grppublic-writing"></a>
#### Academic blogs and public-facing writing

`grp.public-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write academic blog posts, opinion pieces and other outreach texts that communicate research or disciplinary perspectives to non-specialist or mixed readers. Compared with academic genres, these texts lead with the main point, use a more personal and engaging voice, concrete examples, short paragraphs and links rather than formal citation, and signal uncertainty without technical hedging; they also require judgements about representing evidence fairly, the writer's public identity and platform norms.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Reader engagement](#staengagement-markers) (soft), [Rhetorical appeals](#argrhetorical-appeals) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="grpdissertation-overview"></a>
#### Undergraduate and master's dissertation

`grp.dissertation-overview` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core

Understand and plan the undergraduate or taught master's dissertation as a genre: an extended, largely independent piece of research or scholarship whose typical macrostructures include empirical IMRaD-like chapters, topic-based or theoretical structures and practice-based variants, with expectations of scope, length and originality that are lower than for doctoral theses. Learners need to see how proposal, literature review, methodology, findings, discussion and conclusion form one argument across chapters, and how supervision and assessment work; because expectations vary across disciplines and institutions, the handbook, marking criteria and past dissertations are the key sources. Doctoral thesis structures are covered by res.thesis-macrostructure.

Prerequisites: [Analysing genres independently](#gesgenre-analysis) (soft), [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment) (soft), [Empirical project report](#grpempirical-project-report) (soft), [Project proposal (student)](#grpproject-proposal) (soft)

## Research Communication

### Research Writing

Research Writing covers the part-genres, macrostructures and practices through which postgraduate and early-career writers report and position their own research: introductions built on CARS-type moves, literature reviews, framework and methods sections, results, discussions, conclusions, abstracts, theses (including theses by publication), proposals, systematic and scoping reviews, and research instruments. Move analyses (Swales 1990, 2004; Kanoksilapatham 2005; Yang & Allison 2003; Kwan 2006) are descriptive models to test against disciplinary exemplars, not templates, and IMRaD is dominant in experimental fields but far from universal. The domain also covers the sociopragmatic and self-regulatory work of doctoral candidature, such as supervision, progression reviews, teaching as a graduate teaching assistant, sustaining output and writing reflexively, which Kamler and Thomson (2014) treat as both text work and identity work.

<a id="resimrad"></a>
#### IMRaD and its variants

`res.imrad` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine, psychology, social-sciences)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Recognise the Introduction–Methods–Results–Discussion macrostructure as a conventional way of reporting empirical research, explain what each section does for the reader, and identify the variants used in one's own field, such as merged Results and Discussion, a separate Conclusions section, a theory section before Methods, or argument-led organisation in the humanities. Knowing the macrostructure lets writers put content where readers expect it, while recognising IMRaD as a disciplinary convention rather than a universal norm.

Prerequisites: [Navigating research articles](#rdsresearch-article-navigation), [What a genre is](#gesgenre-concept) (soft), [Disciplinary knowledge-making](#acudisciplinary-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="resintroductions-cars"></a>
#### Research article introductions

`res.introductions-cars` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Write research introductions that create a research space: establishing a territory (in Swales's 1990 model by claiming centrality, making topic generalisations and reviewing previous research; in the 2004 revision through cited topic generalisations of increasing specificity), establishing a niche (indicating a gap or adding to what is known), and presenting the present work (the 1990 'occupying the niche'), by announcing the research and its questions and, optionally, its methods, findings, value and structure. Writers adapt the model to their field and genre, including the cyclical moves, extended background and thesis-structure statements common in some disciplines and in PhD introductions, and they use it as an analytical lens on exemplars rather than as a fill-in template.

Prerequisites: [Writing introductions](#essintroductions) (soft), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resliterature-review"></a>
#### Literature review in research writing

`res.literature-review` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Write the literature review of a dissertation, thesis or article as an argument that positions the writer's own study: selecting and organising literature by theme, debate or problem, evaluating rather than merely reporting it, and building cumulatively towards the niche the study fills. The research literature review is judged by how well it justifies the present study's questions, framework and design. It is taught to master's dissertation writers and doctoral researchers; the stand-alone literature review assignment set in undergraduate and taught-master's modules is a separate student genre.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping), [Writing synthesis paragraphs](#synsynthesis-paragraphs), [Rhetorical functions of sources and citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft), [Whose knowledge is cited](#citcitation-politics) (soft), [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resmethods"></a>
#### Methods sections

`res.methods` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine, psychology, social-sciences, education, business-management)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Write methods sections that describe the study (setting, participants or materials, instruments, procedures, data analysis) in enough detail for evaluation or replication and that establish credibility by justifying choices, citing precedents and addressing validity, reliability or trustworthiness. Move structure, level of detail, voice and the balance of description and justification vary considerably across disciplines and between laboratory, survey and qualitative traditions, so writers calibrate against field exemplars and reporting guidelines (for example CONSORT, STROBE, COREQ or SRQR, collected by the EQUATOR Network).

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence) (soft), [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad)

<a id="resresults"></a>
#### Results sections

`res.results` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine, psychology, social-sciences, education, business-management)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Present findings in a logical order that answers the research questions, using visuals and prose together: locating the reader in a table or figure, highlighting what is significant, and adding the degree of commentary the field allows (from minimal to substantial interpretation). Writers decide what to report in text and what to leave in visuals, separate reporting from interpretation where the genre requires it, and keep claims proportionate to the data.

Prerequisites: [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary)

<a id="resdiscussion"></a>
#### Discussion sections

`res.discussion` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Write discussion sections that move from results to meaning: restating key findings in relation to the research questions, comparing them with previous research, explaining expected and unexpected outcomes, evaluating the study's limitations, and drawing implications. Discussions are typically cyclical, repeating statement–comparison–explanation sequences for each major finding, and their persuasiveness depends on claims calibrated to the evidence and on alternative explanations considered rather than dismissed.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources) (soft), [Results sections](#resresults), [Writing about limitations](#reslimitations) (soft)

<a id="resconclusions-implications"></a>
#### Conclusions and implications

`res.conclusions-implications` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Write concluding sections and chapters that consolidate the research: briefly restating aims and key findings, answering the research questions, articulating the contribution, drawing calibrated implications for theory, practice or policy, evaluating the study and recommending specific further research. In theses, the conclusion also looks across chapters and back to the field, and conventions vary on whether conclusions form a separate section in articles.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Writing conclusions](#essconclusions) (soft), [Discussion sections](#resdiscussion), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft)

<a id="resabstracts"></a>
#### Abstracts for student and research writing

`res.abstracts` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Write informative abstracts for student projects, reports, posters and dissertations, and for research articles and theses, that represent the whole work within a tight word limit: typically situating the work, stating its purpose, describing the method, summarising specific results and stating a conclusion or implication, as continuous prose or under the headings of a structured abstract. The same core moves serve both, but student abstracts are judged mainly on accuracy, completeness and concision, whereas research abstracts are also read by searchers and editors as promotional texts whose move emphasis varies across disciplines and journals. Conference abstracts written before results are available are a separate genre.

Prerequisites: [Tense when reporting research](#grmtense-in-reporting) (soft), [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft)

<a id="reslimitations"></a>
#### Writing about limitations

`res.limitations` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Identify the limitations that genuinely affect how a study's findings can be interpreted (sample, design, measurement, analysis, scope) and write about them so that readers can judge the claims, by stating each limitation, its likely effect on the findings and, where possible, how it was mitigated or could be addressed. Well-written limitations protect the contribution by showing the writer's command of the design, whereas generic or apologetic lists undermine it.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft)

<a id="resresearch-questions"></a>
#### Research questions and hypotheses

`res.research-questions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Formulate and present an aim, research questions and, where the design calls for them, hypotheses that are answerable, appropriately scoped and aligned with the stated gap and the chosen methods. Writers also choose how to present them, as numbered questions, objectives or a purpose statement, and use consistent wording across the introduction, methods and discussion. Well-formed questions are the reference point that examiners and reviewers use to judge the coherence of the whole study.

Prerequisites: [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising) (soft), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions) (soft)

<a id="resgap-niche"></a>
#### Establishing a research gap

`res.gap-niche` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Construct the textual case that a niche exists, through counter-claiming, indicating a gap, raising a question, continuing a tradition or adding to what is known, using the lexicogrammar that realises these moves: contrastive connectors, negative or restrictive quantifiers (few studies, little attention), present perfect for the state of research and evaluative lexis. The niche must follow from the literature reviewed and lead to the present study, and it should be stated with appropriate caution, since an overstated gap ('no research exists') is easily refuted.

Prerequisites: [Tense when reporting research](#grmtense-in-reporting) (soft), [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language) (soft), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions)

<a id="rescontribution"></a>
#### Claiming contribution

`res.contribution` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

State what the research adds (new evidence, a new method, a new context, a theoretical refinement or a practical application) and why it matters, at a level of confidence the evidence supports. Writers relate the contribution explicitly to the niche established earlier, choose between self-mention and impersonal framing, and balance promotional and cautious language according to field and genre. Doctoral examiners and journal reviewers look for this statement, and its absence or overstatement is a common reason for criticism.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resthesis-macrostructure"></a>
#### Thesis macrostructure

`res.thesis-macrostructure` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Choose and justify an overall organisation for a dissertation or thesis — traditional simple (IMRaD-like), traditional complex (several studies), topic-based, or publication-based (compilation of articles with framing chapters, assembled during candidature or, in some systems, retrospectively as a PhD by published works), alongside practice-based and professional-doctorate variants that combine an artefact or portfolio with an exegesis or commentary — and plan chapters that fit the research, the institution's regulations and disciplinary norms. Writers understand what each chapter type contributes, how a publication-based thesis builds coherence across stand-alone papers, and what examiners will look for in the whole document.

Prerequisites: [Undergraduate and master's dissertation](#grpdissertation-overview) (soft), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process) (soft)

<a id="reschapter-metatext"></a>
#### Chapter introductions, conclusions and metatext

`res.chapter-metatext` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Guide readers through a long document with metatext above the paragraph level: chapter introductions that state purpose and preview structure, chapter conclusions that summarise and link forward, cross-references between chapters, and sign-posting that reminds the reader how the current section serves the thesis argument. Writers calibrate the density of metatext so that it orients the reader without padding or repeating content.

Prerequisites: [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers), [Transitions between paragraphs and sections](#cohparagraph-transitions) (soft), [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Thesis macrostructure](#resthesis-macrostructure) (soft)

<a id="restheoretical-framework"></a>
#### Theoretical and conceptual frameworks

`res.theoretical-framework` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 (core band C1) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, business-management, humanities, health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Write a theoretical or conceptual framework section that explains which theories, models or concepts the study uses, why they were chosen over alternatives, how key constructs are defined and operationalised, and how the framework shapes the questions, data collection and analysis. The section should do analytical work later in the text rather than function as a free-standing summary of theory, and it should acknowledge disciplinary differences in whether and how frameworks are presented.

Prerequisites: [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review) (soft), [Working with concepts](#dssoperationalising-concepts) (soft)

<a id="resqualitative-reporting"></a>
#### Reporting qualitative findings

`res.qualitative-reporting` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, health-medicine, psychology, business-management)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Present qualitative findings as an argued interpretation: organising them by theme or category, selecting and introducing participant extracts that illustrate and evidence analytic claims, commenting on each extract, and keeping the analyst's voice distinct from participants' voices. Writers show the range and prevalence of patterns without spurious counting, attend to negative cases, and present extracts ethically and consistently, with pseudonyms, transcription conventions and edits marked. Field notes, analytic memos and accounts of the analysis process are covered by a separate social-science topic.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution) (soft), [Integrating quotations](#srcintegrating-quotations), [Results sections](#resresults) (soft)

<a id="resquantitative-reporting"></a>
#### Reporting quantitative findings

`res.quantitative-reporting` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (sciences, health-medicine, psychology, social-sciences, education, business-management, economics)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Report descriptive and inferential statistics in the conventional format of the field or style guide: test statistics, degrees of freedom, exact p-values (reported as p < .001 when very small), effect sizes and confidence intervals in text and tables, with numbers, symbols and units styled correctly. Writers interpret statistical results in words a reader can follow, distinguish statistical significance from practical importance, and avoid the language of proof or of 'trends towards significance'.

Prerequisites: [Results sections](#resresults), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics)

<a id="resresearch-proposal"></a>
#### Research proposals

`res.research-proposal` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write research proposals for admission to doctoral study and for the first-year doctoral plan that persuade reviewers a project is significant, original, feasible and well designed: establishing the problem and gap, stating aims and questions, outlining methods, timeline and resources, anticipating risks and ethics, and articulating expected contributions. Proposals are promissory and evaluative, written in future and conditional terms for readers who must decide quickly. This research genre is distinct from the student project or dissertation proposal written at undergraduate and taught-master's level; later doctoral milestone reports and funding applications have their own topics.

Prerequisites: [Project proposal (student)](#grpproject-proposal) (soft), [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review) (soft), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft)

<a id="resethics-documentation"></a>
#### Ethics applications and participant documents

`res.ethics-documentation` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (social-sciences, health-medicine, psychology, education)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Complete research ethics applications that describe procedures, risks, benefits, consent, confidentiality and data management accurately, and write participant information sheets and consent forms in plain, accessible and, where needed, translated language that enables genuinely informed consent. The genres serve two audiences, the ethics committee and participants, with different register demands, and they commit the researcher to procedures that later methods sections must match.

Prerequisites: [Inclusive and bias-free language](#reginclusive-language) (soft), [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process) (soft), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity)

<a id="ressupervision"></a>
#### Working with supervisors

`res.supervision` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Manage the supervisory relationship through talk and writing: negotiating expectations, preparing agendas and submitting drafts with specific requests for feedback, interpreting and responding to written comments, disagreeing respectfully, and recording decisions and actions after meetings. Supervisory conventions vary across institutions, disciplines and individuals, so learners make these expectations explicit rather than assume them.

Prerequisites: [Running group meetings](#grwmeetings) (soft), [Seeking feedback](#fbkseeking-feedback), [Office hours and tutorials](#prgoffice-hours) (soft)

<a id="reswriting-productivity"></a>
#### Sustaining thesis writing

`res.writing-productivity` · STRATEGIC · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Sustain writing across a multi-year project by establishing regular, low-stakes writing routines, setting and monitoring realistic writing goals, using writing groups, retreats and accountability partners, separating generative writing from revision, and treating writing as part of doing research rather than as 'writing up' at the end. The aim is steady output and recovery from setbacks without compromising wellbeing.

Prerequisites: [Managing extended writing projects](#wprmanaging-long-projects), [Overcoming writing blocks](#wprwriting-blocks) (soft), [Sustainable study habits and wellbeing](#lrnwellbeing) (soft)

<a id="respositionality-writing"></a>
#### Writing positionality statements

`res.positionality-writing` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 (core band C1) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, health-medicine, psychology, arts-design)

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write positionality and reflexivity statements that explain how the researcher's identities, experiences, relationships to participants and theoretical commitments may have shaped access, data and interpretation, and what was done about it. Effective statements are analytic and specific to the study rather than confessional or biographical, use first-person reference according to field conventions, and are integrated with methodological decisions rather than isolated in a separate box.

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention), [Reflexivity and positionality](#crtreflexivity), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft)

<a id="resacknowledgements"></a>
#### Acknowledgements

`res.acknowledgements` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write the acknowledgements section of dissertations, theses and articles: thanking supervisors, participants, funders, institutions and others in a conventional order and register, including the funding, contribution and competing-interest statements that institutions and journals require, and distinguishing acknowledgement from authorship. In theses it is a short but socially significant part-genre that combines personal gratitude with institutional and ethical obligations; in articles it is brief and formulaic and sits alongside other required declarations.

Prerequisites: [Undergraduate and master's dissertation](#grpdissertation-overview) (soft), [Ethics applications and participant documents](#resethics-documentation) (soft), [Authorship and contribution](#pubauthorship) (soft)

<a id="resprogression-review"></a>
#### Doctoral progression and confirmation reviews

`res.progression-review` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Write the report required at formal doctoral milestones, such as confirmation of candidature, MPhil-to-PhD upgrade or transfer and annual progression review, and discuss it with a review panel. The report typically summarises progress, presents revised questions and design, reports preliminary findings or includes a sample chapter, and sets out a plan, timeline, risks and training needs; the panel judges feasibility, coherence and the candidate's ability to explain and defend choices orally. Names, timings, components and outcomes vary widely across institutions and countries (North American programmes, for example, use qualifying examinations and prospectus defences instead), so local regulations define the genre.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions) (soft), [Research and conference presentations](#prsresearch-presentations) (soft), [Research proposals](#resresearch-proposal), [Working with supervisors](#ressupervision) (soft)

<a id="respublication-based-thesis"></a>
#### Thesis by publication

`res.publication-based-thesis` · GENRE · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP (sciences, health-medicine, engineering, psychology)

Stages — pgr: develop

Write the integrative texts that turn a set of published, submitted or publishable papers into one examinable thesis: an introductory exegesis that frames the research problem and shows how the papers address it, linking passages or chapter prefaces that explain the progression between papers, a concluding chapter that synthesises findings and states the overall contribution, and statements of the candidate's contribution to co-authored papers. Examiners assess the coherence of the whole, not only the quality of each paper. Rules on the number, status and authorship of papers, word limits and the reproduction of published text vary by institution, discipline and country, and the retrospective PhD by published works, with a critical commentary on earlier publications, is a related variant.

Prerequisites: [Reusing one's own work](#intrecycling-own-work) (soft), [Thesis macrostructure](#resthesis-macrostructure), [Chapter introductions, conclusions and metatext](#reschapter-metatext) (soft), [Authorship and contribution](#pubauthorship)

<a id="ressystematic-review-writing"></a>
#### Writing systematic and scoping reviews

`res.systematic-review-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP (health-medicine, social-sciences, psychology, education, business-management, computing)

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write the protocol and report of a systematic, scoping or other structured review: stating a focused review question, registering or publishing a protocol where the field expects it, reporting search sources, strategies and selection transparently with a flow diagram, describing risk-of-bias or quality appraisal, writing the narrative or quantitative synthesis, and stating conclusions calibrated to the amount and certainty of the evidence. Reporting guidelines such as PRISMA 2020 and its extensions, including PRISMA-ScR for scoping reviews, set readers' expectations. Structured reviews are now common master's and doctoral outputs in health, education, social policy, management, psychology and software engineering, not only in medicine.

Prerequisites: [Systematic and scoping searching](#infsystematic-searching), [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review) (soft), [Methods sections](#resmethods), [Critical appraisal of health research](#dhlcritical-appraisal) (soft)

<a id="resinstrument-wording"></a>
#### Wording survey and interview questions

`res.instrument-wording` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (social-sciences, psychology, education, business-management, health-medicine)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write questionnaire items, response scales and interview prompts that participants can understand and answer as intended: one idea per item, neutral rather than leading wording, no unexplained jargon or double negatives, response options that are balanced, exhaustive and non-overlapping, and open interview prompts with planned follow-up probes. Writers pilot the instrument with people like the intended participants, check how items are understood and revise; where participants use other languages, translation and checking with fluent speakers are part of the wording process. Badly worded items produce data that later analysis cannot repair.

Prerequisites: [Inclusive and bias-free language](#reginclusive-language) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions), [Working with concepts](#dssoperationalising-concepts) (soft)

<a id="resteaching-as-pgr"></a>
#### Teaching, demonstrating and marking as a doctoral researcher

`res.teaching-as-pgr` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: develop

Communicate as a graduate teaching assistant, tutor or demonstrator: explaining concepts and procedures to undergraduates at an appropriate level, asking and handling questions, managing small-group discussion, giving clear practical or laboratory instructions including safety information, and writing feedback on student work that is specific, criterion-referenced and consistent with the module's marking guidance. Many institutions screen or train international teaching assistants' spoken English, but how well students understand also depends on organisation, checks of understanding and listeners' expectations, so the topic treats teaching communication as shared and learnable rather than as an accent problem.

Prerequisites: [Facilitating discussion](#semfacilitating) (soft), [Explaining concepts and readings to peers](#semexplaining-concepts), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria) (soft)

### Publication & Dissemination

Publication & Dissemination covers the genres, practices and politics through which research reaches readers and is examined and funded: the publishing process, journal choice, the occluded genres around submission (cover letters, reviewer reports, responses to reviewers), open-research statements, conference abstracts and conference participation, the doctoral viva and thesis corrections, turning theses into articles, authorship and contributorship, funding applications, public engagement and academic self-presentation, including personal statements for postgraduate study. Many of these genres are hidden from novices because they are rarely published, so making exemplars and expectations visible is central to teaching. The domain also addresses the opportunities and inequities multilingual scholars meet in English-medium publishing (Flowerdew 1999; Lillis & Curry 2010) and treats them as structural issues, not individual deficits.

<a id="pubpublishing-process"></a>
#### The academic publishing process

`pub.publishing-process` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Understand the stages through which a manuscript becomes a publication — submission, editorial screening and desk rejection, peer review (single-anonymous, double-anonymous, open or transparent, and models such as registered reports and review of preprints), decisions (accept, minor or major revision, reject and resubmit, reject), revision, acceptance, production and post-publication correction or retraction — and the roles of editors, reviewers and authors in each. Understanding the process as a negotiation within a discourse community helps novice authors interpret decisions, plan timelines and avoid treating rejection as a final verdict on their work.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft)

<a id="pubjournal-selection"></a>
#### Selecting a journal

`pub.journal-selection` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Choose a target journal by matching the manuscript's contribution, method and audience to a journal's aims and scope, readership, article types, recent content, review practices, indexing, open-access options and costs, and turnaround, while screening out predatory or deceptive venues. Writers then read and apply the author guidelines and shape the manuscript for that journal's conversation, ideally choosing the journal before drafting rather than after.

Prerequisites: [Predatory publishing, retractions and misinformation](#infproblematic-publishing), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubcover-letters"></a>
#### Cover letters to editors

`pub.cover-letters` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: develop

Write submission letters that identify the manuscript and article type, state concisely what it contributes and why it fits the journal's scope and readership, and make the declarations the journal requires (originality, no simultaneous submission, conflicts of interest, ethics, data availability, suggested or excluded reviewers). The letter is an occluded genre, rarely seen by novices, whose readers are busy editors deciding whether to send a paper for review, so it must be brief, specific and professional.

Prerequisites: [Reusing one's own work](#intrecycling-own-work) (soft), [Selecting a journal](#pubjournal-selection), [Open research practices and statements](#pubopen-research) (soft), [Emails to academic staff](#prgemails-to-staff) (soft)

<a id="pubresponding-reviewers"></a>
#### Responding to reviewers

`pub.responding-reviewers` · GENRE · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Write response-to-reviewer letters that thank reviewers, address every comment point by point, describe precisely what was changed and where, and explain politely and with evidence when a requested change has not been made. The genre requires managing face, since authors must disagree with gatekeepers while remaining cooperative, as well as clear organisation and consistency between the response and the revised manuscript.

Prerequisites: [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process), [Acting on feedback](#fbkacting-on-feedback) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="pubpeer-review-reports"></a>
#### Writing peer review reports

`pub.peer-review-reports` · GENRE · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: develop

Write reviewer reports for journals or conferences that summarise the manuscript and its claimed contribution, evaluate its significance, methods, evidence and argument, distinguish major from minor issues, make actionable suggestions and give a recommendation consistent with the comments. Reports must be constructive, appropriately mitigated and respectful, and reviewers must meet ethical obligations of confidentiality, competence, timeliness and disclosure of conflicts of interest.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique), [Peer review of drafts](#edtpeer-review) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process), [Privacy, data and intellectual property with AI](#gaiprivacy-ip) (soft)

<a id="pubconference-abstracts"></a>
#### Conference abstracts

`pub.conference-abstracts` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Write abstracts and proposals for conference submission that persuade reviewers of relevance to the conference theme, interest and feasibility, often before the research is complete. Writers adapt the research-abstract moves to a call for papers: framing the topic for the conference audience, stating the aim and approach, reporting results or expected outcomes honestly, and signalling what attendees will gain, within strict word limits and format requirements.

Prerequisites: [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising) (soft), [Abstracts for student and research writing](#resabstracts)

<a id="pubviva"></a>
#### Thesis defence and viva

`pub.viva` · GENRE · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Prepare for and take part in the oral examination of a thesis: understanding the local format and possible outcomes, re-reading the thesis strategically, anticipating questions about contribution, design, findings, limitations and future work, and answering by explaining, defending, conceding and clarifying in extended spoken interaction with expert examiners. Formats vary widely, from closed UK-style vivas with internal and external examiners, whose recommendation follows the oral, to public defences in much of continental Europe that take place only after a committee has approved the written thesis, often with one or more opponents, and committee defences in North America that follow earlier proposal defences or qualifying examinations, while in some systems, such as many Australian universities, there is no oral examination.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Arguing orally](#argoral-argument), [Writing about limitations](#reslimitations) (soft), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution), [Doctoral progression and confirmation reviews](#resprogression-review) (soft), [Oral examinations](#exmoral-exams) (soft)

<a id="pubauthorship"></a>
#### Authorship and contribution

`pub.authorship` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Understand what qualifies someone for authorship, using criteria such as the ICMJE's, and how contributions are described with contributor-role taxonomies such as CRediT (which record who did what but do not by themselves decide who qualifies as an author), how author order conventions differ across disciplines, and what counts as honorary, guest or ghost authorship. Researchers should be able to discuss authorship early in collaborations, including with supervisors, and to write accurate contribution statements and acknowledgements, including declarations of the use of AI tools, which journals do not accept as authors.

Prerequisites: [Collaborative writing](#grwcollaborative-writing) (soft), [Assessing contributions](#grwcontribution-assessment) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubmultilingual-scholars"></a>
#### Publishing as a multilingual scholar

`pub.multilingual-scholars` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: develop

Understand the opportunities and inequities of English-medium publishing for multilingual scholars: the dominance of English in high-status indexes, the extra time and cost of writing in an additional language, the material constraints faced by scholars outside well-resourced institutions, reviewer and editor attitudes to non-standard English, and the role of literacy brokers such as co-authors, editors, translators and language professionals. Scholars can use this understanding to make informed choices about language, venue, collaboration and support, and to recognise structural barriers as structural rather than personal failings.

Prerequisites: [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process), [Making informed choices about academic conventions](#acucritical-eap) (soft), [Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy](#idnstandards-legitimacy) (soft), [Translingual writing and code-meshing](#idntranslingual-writing) (soft)

<a id="pubresearch-engagement"></a>
#### Communicating research beyond academia

`pub.research-engagement` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Communicate research to non-academic audiences such as the public, practitioners, policymakers and industry through lay summaries, blogs, social media, talks, visual summaries and impact case material, adapting content, structure, register and claims to each audience while keeping them accurate. Researchers also plan engagement deliberately, as many funders and evaluation systems expect, and manage the risks of oversimplification and online visibility.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft), [Infographics and visual summaries](#mmdinfographics) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="pubacademic-cv-bio"></a>
#### Academic CVs and biographical statements

`pub.academic-cv-bio` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write the self-presentation genres of academic careers: academic CVs, with conventional sections, consistently listed outputs and evidence of research, teaching and service, including the narrative CV formats some funders now use; and short biographical statements for conferences, journals, profiles and applications, often in the third person. Each genre balances self-promotion with modesty according to its conventions, and conventions differ across countries, sectors and career stages. Personal statements for postgraduate study are covered separately.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [Academic and writer identity](#idnacademic-identity) (soft)

<a id="pubfunding-applications"></a>
#### Funding, fellowship and grant applications

`pub.funding-applications` · GENRE · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write the sections of applications for studentships, travel and small grants and early-career fellowships: the case for support or project description, a lay summary, an impact or pathways-to-benefit statement, a track record or narrative CV, a justification of resources and, where required, a data management plan. Assessors, often from outside the applicant's specialism, read applications against published criteria, so writers argue significance, feasibility and their fit with the scheme explicitly, adapt promotional language to funder norms and follow templates, word limits and eligibility rules exactly. Formats and expectations, including the shift from publication lists to narrative CVs, differ across countries, funders and schemes.

Prerequisites: [Research proposals](#resresearch-proposal), [Communicating research beyond academia](#pubresearch-engagement) (soft), [Academic CVs and biographical statements](#pubacademic-cv-bio) (soft), [Open research practices and statements](#pubopen-research) (soft)

<a id="pubconference-participation"></a>
#### Participating in conferences

`pub.conference-participation` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Take part in conferences and research seminars beyond giving a paper: asking concise, relevant questions from the floor, chairing a session by introducing speakers, keeping time and managing discussion, and networking in breaks, poster sessions and online channels, then following up contacts by email. Conference discussion follows conventions of turn allocation, politeness and evaluation that vary across fields, events and formats, including online and hybrid ones, and newcomers can learn them by observation and by preparing reusable moves.

Prerequisites: [Asking questions and seeking clarification](#semquestions-clarification), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions) (soft), [Research and conference presentations](#prsresearch-presentations) (soft), [Informal academic interaction and networking](#prginformal-academic-interaction) (soft)

<a id="pubpersonal-statements"></a>
#### Personal statements for postgraduate study and funding

`pub.personal-statements` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write personal statements, statements of purpose and scholarship essays for applications to master's and doctoral programmes and funding schemes, showing fit with the programme or scheme, academic and professional motivation and capability, each supported by specific evidence, within the stated length and prompts. The genre is promotional and is judged against selection criteria that are often implicit. Expectations differ, for example between research-focused statements of purpose for North American graduate programmes and shorter UK personal statements, and between academic applications and employment applications, which are covered separately.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims), [Academic and writer identity](#idnacademic-identity) (soft)

<a id="pubthesis-corrections"></a>
#### Examiners' reports and thesis corrections

`pub.thesis-corrections` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Interpret the outcome of a thesis examination and the examiners' reports or lists of required changes (for example minor or major corrections, or revise and resubmit), turn them into a prioritised corrections plan, make and record the changes, and write the response document or corrections table that maps each required change to what was done and where. Writers distinguish required from advisory comments, handle language and presentation corrections (often with editorial help within the institution's rules), communicate with the examiner or committee member who approves the corrections and meet regulatory deadlines. Whether this stage follows a viva, precedes a public defence or follows a written-only examination depends on the system.

Prerequisites: [Acceptable third-party proofreading](#edtproofreading-ethics) (soft), [Responding to reviewers](#pubresponding-reviewers) (soft), [Thesis defence and viva](#pubviva) (soft), [Acting on feedback](#fbkacting-on-feedback)

<a id="pubthesis-to-article"></a>
#### From thesis to articles

`pub.thesis-to-article` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pgr: core

Turn thesis chapters into journal articles, or reuse published articles within a thesis: identifying a single publishable argument within the larger thesis, re-scoping it for a target journal's readership, compressing the literature review and methods, rewriting the introduction and contribution for a stand-alone paper, and agreeing authorship with supervisors and collaborators. In both directions writers follow text-recycling, disclosure, copyright and permission rules, for example stating that an article is based on a thesis, citing their own earlier publications and checking publishers' policies on reusing published text.

Prerequisites: [Reusing one's own work](#intrecycling-own-work), [Thesis macrostructure](#resthesis-macrostructure), [Selecting a journal](#pubjournal-selection), [Authorship and contribution](#pubauthorship) (soft)

<a id="pubopen-research"></a>
#### Open research practices and statements
