`pub.open-research` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand and write the texts that open-research practices require: preprints and their versions, preregistrations and registered reports, data and code availability statements, data management plans, licence choices, persistent identifiers such as ORCID iDs and data DOIs, and statements that meet funder and institutional open-access and rights-retention requirements. Writers state accurately what is shared, where, under what conditions and why some data cannot be shared, and keep the plan, the preregistration and the final report consistent with each other and with consent documents. Norms differ across disciplines, for example between quantitative and qualitative research, and policies change often, so writers check current requirements.

Prerequisites: [Open access and legal access](#infopen-access) (soft), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity), [Ethics applications and participant documents](#resethics-documentation) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

## Data, Numeracy & Multimodality

### Data Commentary & Visuals

Data commentary is where numerical and visual evidence becomes argument: learners must read visuals accurately, select what matters, and write or speak about it with claims calibrated to the evidence rather than exhaustive description. The domain runs from visual literacy (visual types, reading graphs and tables, recognising misleading displays) through the lexicogrammar of trends, proportions and diagrams, to the genre of data commentary and the conventions for labelling, referring to and creating visuals. Test description tasks, such as IELTS Academic Writing Task 1, are treated as a contrast case: it rewards selective description of key features, whereas university data commentary also interprets, explains, compares and qualifies.

<a id="datvisual-types"></a>
#### Types of visuals and their functions

`dat.visual-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Recognise the main visual forms in academic texts (line graphs, bar charts, pie charts, scatterplots, tables, flowcharts, diagrams, maps and photographs) and the relationship each is designed to show: change over time, comparison between categories, part–whole composition, distribution, association between variables, sequence or spatial layout. Knowing what a visual is for guides how it is read and which language is needed to describe it.

<a id="datreading-graphs"></a>
#### Reading graphs and charts

`dat.reading-graphs` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Read graphs and charts systematically: check the title, axes, scales, units, legend and source before interpreting; then extract values, compare across series, and identify the overall pattern, exceptions and notable features. Accurate reading at the levels of individual data, relationships between data and inferences beyond the data underpins both data commentary and critical evaluation of others’ visuals.

Prerequisites: [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="datreading-tables"></a>
#### Reading tables

`dat.reading-tables` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Navigate tables by identifying what rows and columns represent, the units, totals, notes, abbreviations and significance markers; locate specific values; and compare across rows or columns to identify the main pattern. Research articles often carry findings in tables that the prose mentions only briefly, so efficient table reading is essential for accurate source use.

Prerequisites: [Scanning for specific information](#rdsscanning) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="datdescribing-trends"></a>
#### Describing trends and change

`dat.describing-trends` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Describe change over time precisely using verbs (rise, fall, fluctuate, level off, peak), nouns (an increase in, a decline of), degree and speed modifiers (slightly, sharply, a gradual rise), prepositions of quantity and time (from … to, by, at, over the period) and appropriate tense. Precise trend language lets writers summarise patterns economically and select only the changes that matter to their point.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect) (soft), [Unaccusative and ergative verbs](#grmunaccusative-verbs) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs)

<a id="datdescribing-proportions"></a>
#### Describing proportions and comparisons

`dat.describing-proportions` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit

Express proportions and comparisons in data precisely, using fractions, percentages, ratios and multiples (a third of, 42% of, a ratio of 3:1, twice as many as), quantifiers (the majority, a substantial minority, nearly all) and comparative and superlative structures with numbers. This allows exact, checkable comparisons between groups and categories rather than vague impressions.

Prerequisites: [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="datdata-commentary"></a>
#### Data commentary

`dat.data-commentary` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (sciences, engineering, social-sciences, psychology, health-medicine, economics, business-management)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Write commentary that uses data as evidence: locate the visual and summarise what it shows, highlight the findings that matter for the writer’s purpose, and interpret them by explaining, comparing with expectations or previous studies, acknowledging limitations and drawing implications whose strength matches the evidence. Data commentary is the core of results and discussion writing across disciplines and differs from description-only test tasks such as IELTS Academic Writing Task 1.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Describing trends and change](#datdescribing-trends), [Describing proportions and comparisons](#datdescribing-proportions), [Referring to visuals in text](#datreferring-to-visuals)

<a id="datdescribing-diagrams"></a>
#### Describing processes, diagrams and maps

`dat.describing-diagrams` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP (sciences, engineering)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Verbalise non-numerical visuals: the stages, branches and loops of processes and cycles in flowcharts; the components and spatial relations in diagrams of structures and systems; and spatial change shown in maps and plans. This draws on sequencing language, voice choice, purpose and result expressions and locative prepositions, and supports explaining scientific, technical and planning visuals in reports, examinations and presentations.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="datcaptions-labelling"></a>
#### Figure and table captions

`dat.captions-labelling` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Number, title and caption figures and tables according to the required style, write informative titles and notes that let the visual stand alone, define abbreviations, symbols and units, and acknowledge the source of reproduced or adapted visuals. Correct labelling makes visuals referable in the text and, when visuals or data come from others, is part of academic integrity.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="datcreating-visuals"></a>
#### Creating effective visuals

`dat.creating-visuals` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Choose the visual form that best serves a message and audience, and design it for clarity and honesty: an appropriate chart type, honest scales and baselines, minimal non-data decoration, direct labelling, accessible colour and enough context (units, sample size, uncertainty). Well-designed visuals let readers check claims quickly and are increasingly assessed in reports, posters and theses.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Recognising misleading visuals](#datmisleading-visuals)

<a id="datmisleading-visuals"></a>
#### Recognising misleading visuals

`dat.misleading-visuals` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Recognise design and data choices that distort a visual’s message (truncated or inconsistent axes, selective time windows, dual axes, area and 3D distortion, mismatched chart types, missing denominators or uncertainty) and judge whether conclusions drawn from the visual are justified. This is a component of statistical literacy and of the critical evaluation of sources.

Prerequisites: [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs)

<a id="datreferring-to-visuals"></a>
#### Referring to visuals in text

`dat.referring-to-visuals` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Integrate visuals into prose by referring to them explicitly and purposefully (Figure 2 shows; as shown in Table 1; see Figure 3), placing references where the reader needs them, choosing verbs that suit visual subjects and the strength of the claim (show, indicate, illustrate, suggest), and telling the reader what to notice. This links visual evidence to the argument instead of leaving visuals to speak for themselves.

Prerequisites: [Verbs for academic and research processes](#vocacademic-verbs) (soft), [Integrating text and visuals](#rdsvisuals-in-text) (soft), [Figure and table captions](#datcaptions-labelling) (soft)

### Academic Numeracy Language

Academic numeracy language is the lexicogrammar and conceptual precision needed to talk and write about quantities, measurements, statistics and mathematical relations in English. Errors here are rarely ‘just language’: confusing per cent with percentage points, using ‘significant’ loosely or misplacing a unit changes the claim. The domain runs from accurate number expressions and approximation, through units and mathematical expression, to statistical vocabulary and consumer-level interpretation of statistical claims, drawing on research into statistical literacy (Gal, 2002) and risk communication (Gigerenzer et al., 2007). EAP teachers without statistical training may wish to co-teach the later topics with subject or mathematics-support colleagues.

<a id="numnumber-expressions"></a>
#### Expressing and presenting quantities accurately

`num.number-expressions` · LINGUISTIC · A2+–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Express numbers, fractions, decimals, percentages, ratios and rates accurately in speech and prose, with the grammar around them (partitives such as a third of, agreement, per cent versus percentage, number of versus amount of, rates and multiplicative forms), and present them in running text according to the required style guide: numerals or words, no sentence-initial numerals, consistent decimal and digit-grouping marks, ranges, and % or per cent. Accurate, consistently presented quantities are the basis of every claim made from data, and number style is checked by markers and editors.

Prerequisites: [Subject–verb agreement with complex subjects](#grmsubject-verb-agreement) (soft), [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers) (soft), [Punctuation and mechanics beyond the comma](#senpunctuation-mechanics) (soft)

<a id="numapproximation"></a>
#### Approximation and precision

`num.approximation` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Approximate and round quantities appropriately (approximately, roughly, just under, well over, in the region of, nearly a third, an estimated) and decide when exact figures or approximation better serve the reader and purpose. Approximators also function as hedges: they signal the writer’s commitment to exactness and must be consistent with the figures and with the precision of the measurement.

Prerequisites: [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="numstatistical-vocabulary"></a>
#### Basic statistical vocabulary

`num.statistical-vocabulary` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (mathematics-statistics, sciences, social-sciences, psychology, health-medicine, economics)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand and use core statistical terms accurately in reading and writing: population and sample, variable, mean, median, mode, range, standard deviation, distribution, correlation, outlier, significance, error and bias. Several of these words have everyday meanings that differ from their technical sense (significant, normal, error, random, confidence, bias), so precise use is essential for credible methods and results writing.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="numinterpreting-statistics"></a>
#### Interpreting statistical claims

`num.interpreting-statistics` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP (mathematics-statistics, psychology, health-medicine, social-sciences, economics, sciences)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Read and interpret inferential statistics at an informed consumer level: understand what p-values, confidence intervals and effect sizes do and do not indicate, distinguish statistical significance from practical importance, and recognise how sample size and design limit conclusions. This allows learners to evaluate quantitative sources and report them accurately without overstating them.

Prerequisites: [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary), [Relative and absolute change](#numrelative-absolute) (soft)

<a id="numrelative-absolute"></a>
#### Relative and absolute change

`num.relative-absolute` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP (health-medicine, economics, social-sciences, mathematics-statistics)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Distinguish relative from absolute change (per cent versus percentage points; relative versus absolute risk; rates versus counts) and choose and interpret framings that do not mislead. The same result can be presented as ‘a 50% reduction’ or as ‘a fall from 2 in 1,000 to 1 in 1,000’, and readers and writers need to recognise how framing shapes judgement.

Prerequisites: [Describing trends and change](#datdescribing-trends) (soft), [Describing proportions and comparisons](#datdescribing-proportions)

<a id="numunits-measurement"></a>
#### Units and measurement

`num.units-measurement` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Report measurements using SI units and symbols correctly (symbols versus names, a space between number and unit, prefixes, no plural or full stop on symbols, compound units such as m/s or kg m⁻³) and follow discipline and style-guide conventions where these differ. Correct use of units is a precondition for credibility and replicability in science, engineering and health writing.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="nummathematical-expression"></a>
#### Language of mathematical expression

`num.mathematical-expression` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (mathematics-statistics, sciences, engineering, economics, computing)

Stages — foundation: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Read and write mathematical expressions in academic prose: verbalising equations and inequalities, introducing and defining variables (where x denotes; let n be), integrating displayed equations into sentences with appropriate punctuation, and using conventional phrasing for relationships (is proportional to, tends to, is a function of). This is needed wherever formulae carry the argument, as in science, engineering, economics and quantitative social science.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Expressing and presenting quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

### Multimodal Composition

Academic communication increasingly combines writing with images, layout, colour, sound and movement, and university assessment now includes infographics, podcasts, video essays and e-portfolios. This domain treats multimodal composition as meaning-making with distinct semiotic resources (Kress & van Leeuwen, 2006), addresses the design of digital and visual-summary genres (Hafner, 2014), and treats accessibility as a design requirement from the outset rather than an afterthought (World Wide Web Consortium, 2023). Academic expectations of argument, evidence and attribution carry over into these genres even though the means of realising them change. Poster design is treated together with presenting posters in the presentations strand (prs.posters).

<a id="mmdmultimodal-literacy"></a>
#### Multimodal literacy

`mmd.multimodal-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Understand how image, layout, colour, typography and text combine to make meaning, and analyse how a multimodal text directs attention through salience, reading path, framing and the placement of information. This awareness underpins both critical reading of academic multimodal texts and the design of posters, slides and digital assignments.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="mmdinfographics"></a>
#### Infographics and visual summaries

`mmd.infographics` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Condense information into visual summaries (infographics, visual abstracts and one-page summaries) for academic and public audiences: select the essential message, structure it visually, and preserve accuracy, attribution and appropriate qualification. Visual summaries are increasingly used in assessment and research dissemination, and they risk oversimplification when impact is pursued at the expense of accuracy.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences) (soft), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="mmddigital-assessments"></a>
#### Video essays and digital assessments

`mmd.digital-assessments` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Produce assessed multimodal genres (podcasts, video essays, narrated presentations, e-portfolios and websites), understanding their purposes, criteria and conventions, and orchestrating speech, text, image and sound to build an academic argument with appropriate attribution of sources and media. These genres keep the core academic expectations of argument, evidence and citation while changing the means of expression.

Prerequisites: [Recorded and online presentations](#prsrecorded-online) (soft), [Citing diverse source types](#citdiverse-sources) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="mmdaccessibility"></a>
#### Accessible academic documents

`mmd.accessibility` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Produce documents, slides and media that are accessible to readers with disabilities and to assistive technologies: structured headings created with styles, meaningful alternative text for images and charts, sufficient colour contrast, meaning not conveyed by colour alone, descriptive link text, readable typography, and captions or transcripts for audio-visual content. Accessibility is an institutional and often legal expectation, and it improves clarity for all readers.

Prerequisites: [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing), [Assistive technology and study adjustments](#digassistive-technology) (soft)

## Learning & Autonomy

### Self-Regulated Learning

This domain covers the knowledge and strategies that let learners direct their own academic and language development: metacognitive knowledge, self-regulation and learner autonomy, motivation, goal setting, time management, independent study, study techniques, language learning strategies, reflective logs, support services, wellbeing and learning content through English. It is anchored in self-regulated learning research (Zimmerman, 2002; Flavell, 1979), autonomy research (Holec, 1981; Benson, 2011) and cognitive research on durable learning (Dunlosky et al., 2013). The conceptual topics are taught explicitly mainly at foundation level and revisited later, while the strategic topics are best taught through learners' real tasks and evidence (feedback, self-test results, time logs) rather than as detached 'study skills'.

<a id="lrnself-regulation"></a>
#### Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy

`lrn.self-regulation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: revisit, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Understand self-regulated learning as a cycle of forethought (analysing the task, setting goals, planning strategies, appraising one's self-efficacy), performance (self-monitoring and controlled strategy use) and self-reflection (evaluating outcomes, attributing causes and adapting), and autonomy as the developing capacity to take control of these decisions (objectives, content, methods and evaluation) with support, not as studying alone. Together they give learners and teachers a shared language for locating where independent learning breaks down and for handing decisions over to learners step by step.

Prerequisites: [Motivation and the L2 self](#lrnmotivation) (soft), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge)

<a id="lrngoal-setting"></a>
#### Setting language and academic goals

`lrn.goal-setting` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Set specific, near-term and checkable language and academic goals derived from actual course and assessment demands, and turn them into planned actions, including if–then plans for predictable obstacles. Well-formed goals turn a general wish to 'improve my English' into decisions about what to practise this week and how progress will be judged.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation)

<a id="lrntime-management"></a>
#### Time and workload management

`lrn.time-management` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Plan, prioritise and protect study time across concurrent deadlines: estimating task duration realistically, scheduling backwards from deadlines, allocating time to independent study as well as taught sessions, and recognising and countering procrastination. For L2 learners, realistic estimates must include the extra time that reading and writing in English may take.

Prerequisites: [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting) (soft)

<a id="lrnindependent-study"></a>
#### Independent study

`lrn.independent-study` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand the scale and purpose of independent study in higher education systems where expected study hours far exceed contact hours (in the UK, for example, one credit conventionally represents about ten notional learning hours, and ECTS credits are likewise defined by total student workload), and plan that time productively: preparing for and consolidating taught sessions, following up reading, practising language and progressing assessments without waiting for instructions.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft)

<a id="lrnstudy-techniques"></a>
#### Evidence-based study techniques

`lrn.study-techniques` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Choose and use study techniques that cognitive research rates highly for durable learning (above all retrieval practice and distributed practice, with interleaving and self-explanation as promising options) instead of relying on lower-utility habits such as rereading, highlighting and copying notes. The techniques apply to language items (vocabulary, formulaic phrases, citation conventions) as well as disciplinary content.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge)

<a id="lrnlanguage-learning-strategies"></a>
#### Language learning strategies

`lrn.language-learning-strategies` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Select, combine and evaluate strategies for continuing to develop academic English beyond the course: for example, noticing and recording useful language from disciplinary reading, deliberate vocabulary study, seeking opportunities for spoken interaction and feedback, using the L1 as a resource, and managing affect. Strategies are chosen to fit the task and goal rather than out of habit.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft)

<a id="lrnreflective-logs"></a>
#### Reflective learning logs

`lrn.reflective-logs` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Keep a structured learning log or journal that records goals, actions taken, evidence of progress and next steps, and use it to make decisions. The key distinction is between analytical reflection, which explains and leads to action, and description of activities or feelings alone.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting)

<a id="lrnsupport-services"></a>
#### Using academic support services

`lrn.support-services` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Identify the academic and personal support a university offers (library and subject librarians, writing or academic skills centres, in-sessional EAP, maths and statistics support, disability, wellbeing and advice services) and use it early, with a prepared and specific request, as a normal part of good study practice rather than a last resort.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures) (soft)

<a id="lrnwellbeing"></a>
#### Sustainable study habits and wellbeing

`lrn.wellbeing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Sustain study across a term or degree by anticipating workload peaks, protecting sleep, breaks and social connection, recognising early signs that stress is affecting learning, and knowing when and how to seek help. Wellbeing is treated here as a condition for learning, not as a clinical intervention.

Prerequisites: [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Using academic support services](#lrnsupport-services) (soft)

<a id="lrnmotivation"></a>
#### Motivation and the L2 self

`lrn.motivation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: revisit, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Understand motivation for continued language and academic development through Dörnyei's L2 Motivational Self System (the ideal L2 self, the ought-to L2 self and the learning experience), and use a specific, plausible image of oneself as a competent academic user of English, linked to concrete current actions, to sustain effort beyond an entry requirement or the end of an EAP course.

<a id="lrnmetacognitive-knowledge"></a>
#### Metacognitive knowledge

`lrn.metacognitive-knowledge` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: revisit, pre-sessional-pg: revisit, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Knowledge of oneself as a learner and user of English, of what particular academic tasks demand, and of which strategies suit which tasks and conditions (Flavell's person, task and strategy variables). Accurate metacognitive knowledge allows learners to plan, monitor and adjust their learning rather than relying on habit or on how familiar material feels.

<a id="lrncontent-through-english"></a>
#### Learning subject content through English

`lrn.content-through-english` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand that in foundation, English-medium and degree contexts, disciplinary concepts and the English used to express them are learned together: technical terms, characteristic text types and ways of explaining are part of the content. Learners use this understanding to plan for the double load, for example by previewing terminology, using L1 resources strategically and checking conceptual understanding as well as language.

Prerequisites: [Lexical coverage and vocabulary size](#voclexical-coverage) (soft), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft), [Language learning strategies](#lrnlanguage-learning-strategies) (soft), [EMI and transnational education contexts](#acuemi-tne) (soft)

## Assessment & Progression

### Feedback & Assessment Literacy

This domain covers how learners come to understand university assessment and use feedback: assessment purposes and rules, grading systems, marking criteria, feedback literacy, acting on feedback, using written corrective feedback, seeking feedback and self- and peer-assessment. It draws on Sadler's (1989) argument that learners must come to share the assessor's concept of quality, on feedback as a support for self-regulation (Nicol & Macfarlane-Dick, 2006; Hattie & Timperley, 2007), on student feedback literacy (Carless & Boud, 2018) and on the contested evidence on written corrective feedback (Truscott, 1996; Ferris, 1999; Bitchener & Ferris, 2012; Kang & Han, 2015). Grading scales, feedback conventions and rules on pre-submission help differ between institutions and national systems, so topics are framed around the learner's target context rather than a single national model. In EMI and transnational programmes (see acu.emi-tne), learners may meet local and partner-institution conventions at once, such as two grading scales, or feedback practices that differ from those of an anglophone campus.

<a id="fbkassessment-literacy"></a>
#### University assessment literacy

`fbk.assessment-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand how university assessment works: the difference between formative and summative assessment; how learning outcomes, assessment briefs and marking criteria relate; the range of assessment types; and the rules on weighting, deadlines, word limits, extensions, mitigating (or extenuating) circumstances and resits, whose names and details vary between institutions. This knowledge lets learners focus effort where it counts and recognise formative opportunities that carry no marks.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="fbkmarking-criteria"></a>
#### Interpreting marking criteria

`fbk.marking-criteria` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core

Unpack the marking criteria, rubrics and grade descriptors for a specific task: identify what each criterion values, how adjacent bands differ, and what evaluative language such as 'critical', 'well-structured' or 'appropriate use of sources' means in observable textual terms, using exemplars to make tacit standards concrete.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy), [Grading systems and mark scales](#fbkgrading-systems) (soft), [Tacit expectations](#acuhidden-curriculum) (soft)

<a id="fbkgrading-systems"></a>
#### Grading systems and mark scales

`fbk.grading-systems` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand how marks are expressed and interpreted in the learner's target context (for example, UK-style percentage bands with degree classifications and master's pass, merit and distinction thresholds; letter grades and grade point averages in North American and many other systems; ECTS grades; or national numeric scales) and what grade bands signal about the quality of work. This prevents marks being over- or under-interpreted through expectations carried over from another system, including in transnational programmes that use two scales at once.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft)

<a id="fbkfeedback-literacy"></a>
#### Feedback literacy

`fbk.feedback-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand feedback as information that learners must actively make sense of and use, not something simply delivered to them. This involves appreciating its purpose, developing the capacity to make evaluative judgements, managing emotional responses and taking action (Carless and Boud's four features). Feedback is recognised in many forms: written comments, rubric marks, peer comments, discussion in class, tutorials and automated tools.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft), [Tacit expectations](#acuhidden-curriculum) (soft)

<a id="fbkacting-on-feedback"></a>
#### Acting on feedback

`fbk.acting-on-feedback` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Turn feedback into a feed-forward action plan: categorise comments, identify issues that recur across assignments, decide which apply to the next (often different) task, act on them and check whether the change worked.

Prerequisites: [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy)

<a id="fbkcorrective-feedback"></a>
#### Using written corrective feedback

`fbk.corrective-feedback` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Use written corrective feedback on language (direct corrections, correction codes, underlining or margin indications) to understand the nature of errors, self-correct where feedback is indirect, and track recurring error patterns, recognising that one-off corrections rarely carry over to new writing without follow-up.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft)

<a id="fbkseeking-feedback"></a>
#### Seeking feedback

`fbk.seeking-feedback` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Request feedback that is specific, timely and usable (on a draft, plan or idea) from tutors, supervisors and peers, framing the request politely and within institutional norms, such as what tutors may comment on before submission, and following up to clarify comments.

Prerequisites: [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy), [Making requests](#prgrequests) (soft)

<a id="fbkself-peer-assessment"></a>
#### Self- and peer-assessment

`fbk.self-peer-assessment` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Judge the quality of one's own and peers' work against criteria and exemplars, give feedback that is specific and criterion-referenced, and calibrate these judgements over time by comparing them with tutors' judgements. The aim is evaluative judgement, not self-grading.

Prerequisites: [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft)

### Exams & Timed Performance

This domain covers university examinations and timed performance: understanding exam formats, planning revision, writing under time pressure, approaching objective-format questions, open-book, take-home and online time-windowed exams, oral examinations and managing test anxiety; analysing exam questions is taught within task analysis (wpr.task-analysis). It draws on cognitive research on retrieval and spacing (Dunlosky et al., 2013; Roediger & Karpicke, 2006), research on planning in L2 writing (Ellis & Yuan, 2004), writing assessment (Weigle, 2002) and test anxiety (Putwain, 2008; Horwitz et al., 1986). Emphasis is on matching preparation and in-exam behaviour to the demands of each format rather than on generic exam tips, and on the adjustments (extra time, rest breaks, assistive technology, alternative arrangements) that make timed formats accessible.

<a id="exmexam-formats"></a>
#### University exam formats

`exm.exam-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand the main formats of university examination (unseen and seen written papers, open-book and take-home exams, multiple-choice and short-answer tests, problem-based papers, oral and practical examinations such as OSCEs in health programmes, and computer-based or online variants) and what kind of knowledge and performance each rewards, so that preparation and in-exam behaviour match the format.

Prerequisites: [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="exmrevision-planning"></a>
#### Planning revision

`exm.revision-planning` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Plan revision as a spaced, active schedule that begins weeks before the exam period: mapping the syllabus and past-paper demands, distributing sessions across topics, building sessions around retrieval and timed practice, and adjusting the plan according to self-test results.

Prerequisites: [Reviewing and reworking notes](#ntkreviewing-notes) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmtimed-writing"></a>
#### Timed writing

`exm.timed-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Produce coherent, relevant written answers under time pressure, handwritten or typed: planning rapidly (a brief outline and a position), allocating time across questions in proportion to the marks available and between planning, writing and checking, writing fluently without sources to hand, and checking for one's own most frequent errors. Applies to exam essays, short-answer papers, online timed papers and timed tasks in language tests and progression assessments; interpreting the question itself is covered by task analysis.

Prerequisites: [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis), [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining), [Drafting](#wprdrafting) (soft)

<a id="exmobjective-questions"></a>
#### Objective question strategies

`exm.objective-questions` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP (sciences, health-medicine, business-management)

Stages — foundation: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Approach multiple-choice, true/false, matching and short-item questions strategically: reading stems precisely (including negatives and qualifiers), predicting an answer before reading the options, eliminating distractors for stated reasons, managing time and flagged items, and applying the paper's rules on guessing and negative marking.

Prerequisites: [Hedging and qualifying generalisations](#stahedging) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmopen-book"></a>
#### Open-book, take-home and online time-windowed exams

`exm.open-book` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Prepare for and complete open-book, take-home and online time-windowed exams (for example, papers released on a virtual learning environment with a fixed submission window, sometimes remotely invigilated), recognising that they usually assess application, analysis and synthesis rather than recall: organising and indexing permitted materials in advance, managing the window (including typing, uploading and technical contingencies), and observing paper-specific rules on collaboration, permitted resources, citation and generative AI use.

Prerequisites: [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies) (soft)

<a id="exmoral-exams"></a>
#### Oral examinations

`exm.oral-exams` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Prepare for and perform in oral assessments such as questioning on submitted work, individual oral examinations, interactive orals and the speaking components of language assessments: anticipating question types, structuring spoken answers, asking for clarification or repetition, gaining thinking time appropriately and recovering from breakdowns.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmtest-anxiety"></a>
#### Managing test anxiety

`exm.test-anxiety` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Recognise how anxiety in evaluative situations (written exams, timed tests, oral assessments and high-stakes proficiency tests) can interfere with performance, particularly through worry that takes up attention, and use preparation, reappraisal and in-exam techniques to reduce its impact, while knowing when anxiety calls for professional support or reasonable adjustments.

Prerequisites: [Sustainable study habits and wellbeing](#lrnwellbeing) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

### Proficiency Tests & Progression

This domain helps learners understand the language assessments that control access to and progression through university study: the purposes of different assessments, CEFR levels and entry requirements, score reports and test choice, the task types and constructs of academic proficiency tests, the differences between test tasks and university tasks, and pre-sessional progression assessment; integrated reading- and listening-into-writing tasks, including their test versions, are taught in syn.integrated-tasks. It draws on language assessment theory (Bachman & Palmer, 2010; Weigle, 2002), the CEFR (Council of Europe, 2001, 2020; North, 2014) and research on test washback and on the gap between test and university writing (Green, 2007; Moore & Morton, 2005). Test formats, score-use rules and visa requirements change, and major tests have been redesigned in recent years, so content is framed at the level of task types and constructs, and learners are directed to current official sources.

<a id="tstassessment-purposes"></a>
#### Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment

`tst.assessment-purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Distinguish the purposes of different language assessments (proficiency, placement, diagnostic, achievement, and progression or gatekeeping decisions) and understand that a score is evidence supporting a particular interpretation and decision, not a fixed property of the learner. This helps learners interpret their results realistically and use each assessment for its intended purpose.

<a id="tstcefr-entry"></a>
#### CEFR levels and entry requirements

`tst.cefr-entry` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop

Understand what CEFR levels describe (communicative ability in can-do terms across activities, including 'plus' levels and uneven profiles across skills), how university and visa entry requirements are expressed through test scores and levels, and why the relationship between a test score and a CEFR level rests on test owners' published alignment claims, which are approximate and debated. Requirements are set by institutions and government bodies and change over time.

Prerequisites: [Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment](#tstassessment-purposes)

<a id="tsttest-formats"></a>
#### Academic proficiency test formats

`tst.test-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop

Understand the task types and constructs of the major academic English proficiency tests used for university entry (for example, reading with selected-response items, lecture-style listening, independent writing and, in some tests only, integrated reading- or listening-into-writing or -speaking tasks, interview-based or computer-delivered speaking, and computer-adaptive item selection), including differences in delivery mode and in human, automated or hybrid scoring, so that preparation targets what each test actually measures.

Prerequisites: [Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment](#tstassessment-purposes) (soft)

<a id="tsttest-vs-university"></a>
#### Test tasks versus university tasks

`tst.test-vs-university` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Recognise how proficiency-test tasks differ from university tasks. Test writing is typically short, timed, drawn from personal knowledge or brief inputs, on general topics and scored against generic criteria; university writing is typically extended, source-based, discipline-specific, varied in genre and judged against disciplinary expectations. Learners use this understanding to avoid carrying test templates into university work.

Prerequisites: [Rhetorical functions of sources and citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft), [Genre families and essay types in university assessment](#gesgenre-families) (soft), [Academic proficiency test formats](#tsttest-formats)

<a id="tstpresessional-progression"></a>
#### Pre-sessional progression assessment

`tst.presessional-progression` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core

Understand how pre-sessional and similar pathway, bridging or direct-entry programmes assess progression, typically through coursework such as an extended source-based project, seminars or presentations, a reflective portfolio and sometimes a timed integrated reading-into-writing task (in person or online), and how criteria, weightings, integrity rules and progression thresholds work, so that learners can plan effort across components and use formative stages.

Prerequisites: [Extended source-based project](#gesextended-source-based-project) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria), [CEFR levels and entry requirements](#tstcefr-entry) (soft), [Test tasks versus university tasks](#tsttest-vs-university) (soft)

<a id="tstscore-reports"></a>
#### Score reports, validity and test choice

`tst.score-reports` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop

Read a proficiency-test score report (overall and component scores, and any single-skill retake or combined-sitting arrangement the test offers) and judge whether it meets a stated requirement, taking account of score validity periods, the delivery modes (test centre, computer-based, at home or remotely proctored) that a given institution or visa authority accepts, and the integrity rules of high-stakes testing. Because these rules differ between tests and receiving bodies and change often, every judgement is checked against current official sources before a test is booked or a score is submitted.

Prerequisites: [CEFR levels and entry requirements](#tstcefr-entry), [Academic proficiency test formats](#tsttest-formats) (soft)

## Academic Socialisation, Identity & Communication

### Academic Culture & Expectations

Makes explicit how universities and disciplines are organised, what they expect of learners and how those expectations are communicated, often tacitly. The domain moves from orientation knowledge (structures, teaching formats, roles and address) through discourse-community and disciplinary awareness to making informed choices about conventions, and includes two teacher-facing principles (the academic literacies perspective, and designing for EMI and transnational contexts) that shape how the rest is taught. Difficulty is framed as a mismatch between practices, not a learner deficit, and tacit expectations affect first-generation, mature, part-time and widening-participation home students as well as international students. Structures, roles and expectations differ in EMI programmes and transnational branch campuses, where an awarding institution's regulations may sit alongside local academic culture (see acu.emi-tne).

<a id="acuuniversity-structures"></a>
#### How universities are organised

`acu.university-structures` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Identify the organisational units (faculty, school, department, programme, module), staff roles (module leader, personal tutor, programme director, supervisor, professional services staff) and decision-making processes (programme regulations, assessment boards, student representation) that affect one's study, and use this knowledge to find information and the right person to contact. Organisational knowledge underpins effective help-seeking and correspondence, and terms and structures vary considerably between institutions and countries.

<a id="acuteaching-formats"></a>
#### Teaching and learning formats

`acu.teaching-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Recognise the purposes, participation expectations and preparation demands of the main formats of university teaching (lectures, seminars, tutorials, laboratory and studio sessions, workshops, and VLE-based or blended learning) and the relationship between timetabled contact and independent study. Knowing what each format is for lets learners prepare appropriately and interpret what counts as taking part.

<a id="acuacademic-expectations"></a>
#### Expectations of university learners

`acu.academic-expectations` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Interpret commonly stated expectations of university learners (independent study, critical engagement with sources, contribution to discussion, responsibility for one's own learning) as situated practices whose meaning varies by discipline, level and institution, rather than as fixed personal traits. Making these expectations concrete helps learners act on them and recognise where their prior experience already meets them.

<a id="acudiscourse-communities"></a>
#### Discourse communities and disciplines

`acu.discourse-communities` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand disciplines and other academic groups as discourse communities with shared goals, channels of communication, characteristic genres and specialised lexis, in which membership develops through participation over time. The concept helps learners see academic conventions as purposeful practices of particular communities rather than universal rules, while recognising that communities are internally diverse and their boundaries contested.

<a id="acudisciplinary-knowledge"></a>
#### Disciplinary knowledge-making

`acu.disciplinary-knowledge` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Compare how disciplines construct knowledge (what counts as evidence, how claims are warranted, the role of theory, cumulative versus interpretive knowledge-building) and relate these differences to the genres, citation practices and stance valued in one's own field. Recognising epistemological difference explains why writing advice that works in one field misleads in another.

Prerequisites: [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities)

<a id="acuhidden-curriculum"></a>
#### Tacit expectations

`acu.hidden-curriculum` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Identify the unstated conventions behind university tasks and feedback, such as what tutors mean by 'argument', 'structure', 'critical' or 'too descriptive', how criteria are applied in practice, and which behaviours are valued but not taught, and use questioning, exemplars and feedback to make them explicit. Tacit expectations disadvantage students whose prior schooling or family experience did not share them, including first-generation, mature and returning students as well as those new to the national system, so surfacing them is a matter of equity as well as skill.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations)

<a id="acuacademic-literacies"></a>
#### Academic literacies perspective

`acu.academic-literacies` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP · audience: teacher

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

A curriculum principle for teachers and course designers: reading and writing are social practices shaped by identity, power and institutional context, so difficulty often arises from contested and unstated expectations rather than from individual deficit (the academic literacies model, as distinct from study-skills and academic socialisation models). In teaching it means making expectations explicit, opening dialogue about meaning-making and treating students' identities and prior literacies, including those of first-generation, mature and multilingual students, as resources; postgraduates in education, applied linguistics and the social sciences may also study it as content.

Prerequisites: [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft), [Tacit expectations](#acuhidden-curriculum) (soft)

<a id="acutransition"></a>
#### Transition and adjustment

`acu.transition` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Manage academic and sociocultural adjustment to a new educational context by identifying specific unfamiliar practices and sources of pressure, learning the practices of the new setting, drawing on prior study, work and life experience and multilingual repertoires, and building peer, academic and professional support. Transition affects home students as well as international ones, including first-generation, mature, part-time and commuting students and those balancing caring or paid work; adjustment is uneven rather than a fixed sequence of stages, and it involves the institution adapting as well as the student.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="acuroles-address"></a>
#### Academic roles and forms of address

`acu.roles-address` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Choose appropriate forms of address and reference for academic and professional staff (titles such as Dr and Professor, first names, or no title) by checking local practice, the person's own signature or introduction, and the institutional relationship. Norms vary across institutions, countries, disciplines and individuals, and misjudged address in either direction can affect how a message is received.

Prerequisites: [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures) (soft)

<a id="acucritical-eap"></a>
#### Making informed choices about academic conventions

`acu.critical-eap` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Distinguish conventions that are fixed by assessment or regulation from those that vary across contexts or are open to negotiation, make and justify informed choices about conventions in one's own work (for example self-mention, structure, or the use of other languages and non-anglophone sources), and use legitimate channels such as tutor queries and student representation to question requirements. The topic draws on critical EAP's question of whose norms are taught and whose interests they serve, but its learner-facing aim is informed agency rather than rejection of conventions, which still carry real consequences.

Prerequisites: [Myths and change in academic style](#regstyle-myths-variation) (soft), [Tacit expectations](#acuhidden-curriculum) (soft)

<a id="acuemi-tne"></a>
#### EMI and transnational education contexts

`acu.emi-tne` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Analyse the language ecology of one's own English-medium programme in a non-anglophone university (EMI) or in transnational provision such as a branch campus or an articulation, franchised or joint programme: which languages are used for which purposes, what content lecturers expect of students' English, and which regulations follow the awarding institution, and use this analysis to set language priorities. For teachers and course designers the same analysis is a design principle: EAP for EMI and TNE should be shaped by the local context rather than by anglophone-campus assumptions.

Prerequisites: [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

### Academic Pragmatics & Correspondence

Covers the sociopragmatic and pragmalinguistic resources learners need to interact with staff, peers and employers outside the core academic genres: politeness and face, requests, apologies and accounts, emails, office hours, formal institutional statements, forum posts, channel choice, informal academic talk, and applications and interviews for placements and graduate roles. Interlanguage pragmatics research shows that pragmatic competence does not keep pace with grammatical development without attention, and that staff judge messages on perceived politeness and clarity as well as accuracy. Norms vary across institutions, countries and individuals, and in EMI and TNE settings forms of address, channels (including messaging apps) and procedures may follow local rather than anglophone conventions (see acu.emi-tne); the aim is informed, context-sensitive choice, not one correct formula.

<a id="prgpoliteness"></a>
#### Politeness and face in academic interaction

`prg.politeness` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Analyse student–staff and peer interaction in terms of face, social distance, relative power and the size of an imposition, and choose linguistic strategies (mitigation, indirectness, acknowledging the other's time and autonomy) accordingly. Politeness norms depend on context and vary across institutions and individuals, so the aim is informed choice rather than a single formula.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Academic roles and forms of address](#acuroles-address) (soft)

<a id="prgemails-to-staff"></a>
#### Emails to academic staff

`prg.emails-to-staff` · GENRE · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Write emails to lecturers, supervisors and professional services staff with an informative subject line, an appropriate greeting and form of address, an early statement of purpose, sufficient context, a clear request or question, and a suitable closing and signature with identifying details. Email is often the first and most frequent written contact with staff, and its form shapes perceptions of the writer.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Academic roles and forms of address](#acuroles-address), [Making requests](#prgrequests)

<a id="prgrequests"></a>
#### Making requests

`prg.requests` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Make academic requests (for extensions, meetings, references, clarification, feedback or resources) in writing and speech that state clearly what is wanted, give a relevant reason, and are mitigated in proportion to the imposition. Clarity of purpose and appropriate mitigation together determine whether a request is understood and granted, and some requests must go through formal procedures rather than personal appeal.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="prgoffice-hours"></a>
#### Office hours and tutorials

`prg.office-hours` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Prepare for and conduct one-to-one or small-group meetings with academic staff, in person or online: booking through the expected channel, bringing specific questions and materials, framing the purpose at the start, negotiating and checking understanding, noting agreed actions and closing within the time available. Where such meetings exist they are a key but under-used route to tacit expectations and individual feedback; arrangements vary widely, and some institutions and EMI programmes have no office-hour system.

Prerequisites: [Asking questions and seeking clarification](#semquestions-clarification) (soft)

<a id="prgformal-statements"></a>
#### Formal statements and appeals

`prg.formal-statements` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write formal institutional texts (extenuating or mitigating circumstances statements, academic appeals and complaints) that follow the relevant procedure, give a factual, chronological and evidenced account, relate it to the grounds the regulations allow, state the outcome sought and keep a measured, non-accusatory tone. These texts affect progression and are judged against regulations, so procedural fit matters more than persuasion or emotional appeal.

Prerequisites: [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration) (soft), [Making requests](#prgrequests), [Apologising and explaining](#prgapologising-explaining) (soft)

<a id="prgapologising-explaining"></a>
#### Apologising and explaining

`prg.apologising-explaining` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Apologise and give accounts for missed deadlines, absences, lateness or other lapses in a way that acknowledges responsibility proportionately, explains briefly and relevantly, proposes repair, and avoids both under-apology and excessive self-abasement or over-disclosure. Effective apologies maintain working relationships and often connect the student to the formal process they actually need.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="prgforum-posts"></a>
#### Online discussion forum posts

`prg.forum-posts` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write asynchronous discussion posts and replies on VLE forums that respond to the prompt, make a clear point supported by course readings or evidence, engage explicitly with others' contributions, and use a register between conversation and formal academic prose. Forum posts are often assessed and make reasoning visible to peers, so they reward both argument and collegial engagement.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Building on others' contributions](#sembuilding-on-others) (soft)

<a id="prgmessaging-channels"></a>
#### Messaging channels and norms

`prg.messaging-channels` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Choose the appropriate channel (email, VLE messages or forums, enquiry and ticketing systems, group chats, messaging apps, collaboration platforms) for a given purpose, recipient, level of privacy and urgency, and adjust tone, length and expectations of response time accordingly. Channel norms differ across institutions, countries and teams and are often unstated; a messaging app that is the official staff channel in one institution may blur professional boundaries in another.

Prerequisites: [Emails to academic staff](#prgemails-to-staff), [Virtual learning environments](#digvle) (soft)

<a id="prginformal-academic-interaction"></a>
#### Informal academic interaction and networking

`prg.informal-academic-interaction` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Take part in informal talk with peers, lab or studio colleagues and staff before and after classes, in shared workspaces and at departmental or social events: opening and sustaining small talk, choosing shared and low-risk topics, showing interest, moving between informal and academic talk (for example from weekend plans to a question about an assignment) and closing politely. Informal interaction builds the relationships through which much tacit knowledge, help and opportunity circulates, but participation remains a choice and can take several forms.

Prerequisites: [Grammar of spoken academic interaction](#grmspoken-grammar) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft), [Intercultural communication](#idnintercultural-communication) (soft)

<a id="prgprofessional-applications"></a>
#### Applications and interviews for placements and graduate roles

`prg.professional-applications` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write cover letters and competency-based application answers, and give interview answers, that translate study, projects, part-time work and other experience into evidence matched to an employer's stated criteria, using a structured account of situation, action and result and stating one's own contribution clearly. The topic is bounded to the language and genre demands of placement and graduate-role applications; careers guidance and occupation-specific English remain with careers services and ESP.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Emails to academic staff](#prgemails-to-staff) (soft)

### Identity, Plurilingualism & Intercultural Competence

This domain addresses who learners are as academic language users: how writing and speaking construct identity, how plurilingual repertoires can be used strategically from the start of study, when other languages and Englishes may appear in academic texts, how English functions as a lingua franca and in established World Englishes, and how intercultural and rhetorical differences can be understood without stereotyping. It adopts a resource rather than a deficit orientation, treats culture as dynamic, multiple and partly institutional, names native-speakerism and racialised language judgements, and presents contrastive rhetoric and language separation in assessed texts as contested. Several topics pair a learner skill with a curriculum principle for teachers (audience 'both'), and practice differs in EMI and transnational programmes, where other languages are often part of everyday study.

<a id="idnacademic-identity"></a>
#### Academic and writer identity

`idn.academic-identity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C2 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Understand that academic writing and speaking construct an identity — through choices of stance, self-mention, citation, register and topic — and that entering a discipline involves negotiating new identities alongside existing ones. Seeing identity as discoursally constructed helps learners make deliberate choices about how they present themselves, and makes tensions between existing selves and academic conventions discussable rather than private.

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="idnplurilingual-repertoire"></a>
#### Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire

`idn.plurilingual-repertoire` · STRATEGIC · A2+–C1 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use all one's languages strategically for academic learning — reading and note-making across languages, discussing ideas in shared languages, planning in the first language and checking terminology bilingually — while meeting the language rules of each assessment. Whether assessed texts may themselves be translingual (for example quoting and glossing sources in other languages, or mixing languages in reflective genres) is contested and varies by task (see Translingual writing), and learners can ask. Treating the repertoire as an integrated starting resource, rather than as interference, supports comprehension, conceptual depth and confidence from the first stage of study.

Prerequisites: [Language learning strategies](#lrnlanguage-learning-strategies) (soft)

<a id="idnelf"></a>
#### English as a lingua franca in academia

`idn.elf` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand that much academic communication in English takes place among multilingual users for whom English is a shared additional language, and that many users speak established varieties such as Indian, Nigerian or Singaporean English as a first or dominant language; that effectiveness in such interaction depends on intelligibility, accommodation and negotiation of meaning rather than conformity to a native-speaker model; and that all participants, including first-language English users, share responsibility for mutual understanding.

Prerequisites: [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft), [Linguistic diversity as resource](#idndiversity-as-resource) (soft)

<a id="idnintercultural-communication"></a>
#### Intercultural communication

`idn.intercultural-communication` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Manage differences in expectations, communication styles and assumptions in academic interaction with peers, tutors and supervisors by noticing when misunderstanding may stem from differing norms, suspending quick judgements, checking interpretations and negotiating shared ways of working. Intercultural competence here treats culture as dynamic and multiple — institutional, disciplinary and group-level as well as national — rather than as fixed national traits.

Prerequisites: [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

<a id="idnintercultural-rhetoric"></a>
#### Intercultural rhetoric

`idn.intercultural-rhetoric` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand how views of rhetorical variation across languages and cultures have developed — from Kaplan's contrastive rhetoric, through Connor's intercultural rhetoric, to critical contrastive rhetoric — and use this to analyse why writing conventions differ and change, without assuming that any culture's rhetoric is deficient or that an individual's writing is determined by their first language. The practical outcome is treating conventions as learnable, situated choices.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [What a genre is](#gesgenre-concept)

<a id="idndiversity-as-resource"></a>
#### Linguistic diversity as resource
