`idn.diversity-as-resource` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 (core band B1) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Recognise and respond to deficit framings that present multilingual students — or first-generation, mature and other non-traditional home students — as lacking language, critical thinking or autonomy, and identify the linguistic, educational and intercultural resources in one's own and peers' backgrounds that can contribute to learning, collaboration and knowledge-making. As a curriculum principle for teachers and programmes, it means making academic expectations explicit for all students rather than remediating some.

Prerequisites: [Transition and adjustment](#acutransition) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="idnstandards-legitimacy"></a>
#### Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy

`idn.standards-legitimacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C2 (core band B2+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Examine critically the assumption that native-speaker English is the benchmark for academic English, distinguishing the conventions of academic genres (which all writers must learn) from nativeness, recognising established World Englishes as legitimate varieties, and understanding how native-speakerism, gatekeeping and language ideologies — including the tendency to judge the same English differently depending on a speaker's perceived race, nationality or accent — affect assessment, publication and participation. This enables learners to claim legitimacy as multilingual academic users while making informed choices about conventions.

Prerequisites: [Myths and change in academic style](#regstyle-myths-variation) (soft), [Making informed choices about academic conventions](#acucritical-eap) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

<a id="idntranslingual-writing"></a>
#### Translingual writing and code-meshing

`idn.translingual-writing` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C2 (core band B2+) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand translingual approaches in which writers deliberately bring other languages or Englishes into academic texts — glossing a term from another language, quoting a source in the original with a translation, writing in an established local variety of English, meshing languages in reflective or creative genres, or submitting work in more than one language where a programme allows it — and judge where such choices are accepted, contested or prohibited. Treating language separation in assessed texts as a convention that varies by task, genre and institution, rather than as a universal rule, lets writers make informed choices and negotiate them with tutors and readers.

Prerequisites: [Making informed choices about academic conventions](#acucritical-eap) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire), [Intercultural rhetoric](#idnintercultural-rhetoric) (soft), [Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy](#idnstandards-legitimacy) (soft)

## Digital Literacy & AI

### Digital Scholarship Tools

Academic work is mediated by digital tools (learning platforms, word processors, reference managers, shared documents, translation tools, editing and automated feedback tools, and assistive technologies) whose effective and ethical use is rarely taught explicitly. This domain treats these tools as part of academic literacy practices rather than as separate technical skills (Lea & Jones, 2011), emphasising critical and transparent use: tools support but do not replace learners’ judgement, their outputs must be checked, and their permitted use is set by institutional rules. Assistive technologies and study adjustments are treated as legitimate academic practice, and because products change quickly, topics are framed around transferable functions and decisions rather than named applications.

<a id="digvle"></a>
#### Virtual learning environments

`dig.vle` · PROCEDURAL · A2+–B2 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Navigate the institution’s virtual learning environment and related systems to find module information, assessment briefs, readings, recordings, announcements and feedback; submit work correctly through submission portals; and keep track of deadlines and notifications. Missed instructions, unread feedback and failed submissions are avoidable sources of difficulty, especially in a first term.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft)

<a id="digreference-managers"></a>
#### Reference management software

`dig.reference-managers` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use reference management software to collect, organise and annotate sources, insert in-text citations and generate reference lists in a required style, and check the output for errors. Reference managers save time and reduce inconsistency, but they reproduce whatever metadata they are given, so users still need to know what a correct reference looks like.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft)

<a id="digword-processing"></a>
#### Academic word processing

`dig.word-processing` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: revisit

Use the word-processing functions that support academic documents: paragraph and heading styles, automatic numbering, generated contents pages, captions and cross-references, section breaks, headers, footers and page numbers, templates, and comments. Structure-based formatting saves time in long documents, keeps formatting consistent and makes documents accessible and easier to revise.

<a id="digmachine-translation"></a>
#### Translation tools in reading and drafting

`dig.machine-translation` · STRATEGIC · A2+–C1 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use translation tools strategically and transparently in reading and drafting, whether dedicated machine translation (MT) or general-purpose AI chat tools: to support comprehension of difficult texts, to check understanding, to explore wording and, where permitted, to draft or revise, while recognising characteristic errors (terminology, register, lost or added hedging, fluent but inaccurate output), verifying output and complying with assessment rules. Translation tools are one legitimate resource within a plurilingual repertoire; undeclared or wholesale use in assessed writing can breach integrity rules, and how far other languages should shape drafting is a task-dependent and contested choice.

Prerequisites: [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Strategic use of first language and translation in reading](#rdpplurilingual-reading) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="digdigital-collaboration"></a>
#### Digital collaboration tools

`dig.digital-collaboration` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Use shared documents, comments, suggestion and track-changes modes, version history and cloud workspaces to co-author and review academic work: setting permissions, giving and responding to comments constructively and concisely, resolving suggestions, and keeping a clear record of contributions. Digital collaboration is now the default medium for group projects and for much supervisor feedback.

Prerequisites: [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft), [File and version management](#digfile-management) (soft)

<a id="digfile-management"></a>
#### File and version management

`dig.file-management` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 (core band B1) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: revisit, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Organise, name, version and back up academic files systematically: consistent folder structures and file names, clear distinction between working and submitted versions, local and off-device backups, and secure storage of sensitive material. Good file management prevents lost work and confusion between drafts and, for research students, is the basis of research data management.

<a id="digdigital-etiquette"></a>
#### Digital etiquette and online presence

`dig.digital-etiquette` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Communicate appropriately in digital academic spaces (forums, group chats, video calls, social media and professional networks), adapting tone, formality and response expectations to channel and audience, and manage an academic online presence and privacy (profiles, researcher identifiers, what to share publicly). Digital interactions persist and are searchable, so pragmatic misjudgements can have lasting effects.

Prerequisites: [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="digautomated-feedback-tools"></a>
#### Editing and automated feedback tools

`dig.automated-feedback-tools` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit, pgr: develop

Use spelling and grammar checkers, readability measures, automated writing evaluation (AWE) platforms and AI-based feedback and rewriting features critically, as one input among others: know what each detects well and poorly, accept or reject suggestions on principled grounds checked against reference sources, recognise what tools cannot judge (argument, content, disciplinary appropriateness) and use them within institutional rules on permitted assistance. Uncritical use can introduce errors, change meaning and flatten the writer’s voice, and rewriting features can breach assessment rules.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring recurrent grammatical errors](#grmerror-monitoring) (soft), [Editing for grammatical accuracy](#edtgrammar-editing), [Proofreading](#edtproofreading) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="digassistive-technology"></a>
#### Assistive technology and study adjustments

`dig.assistive-technology` · STRATEGIC · A2+–B2+ (core band B1) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Use assistive and accessibility technologies (read-aloud and text-to-speech, dictation, screen readers and magnification, spelling and word-prediction support, live captions and transcription, and mind-mapping, reading-support and planning tools) and institutional adjustments (such as extra time, rest breaks, alternative formats and alternative modes of participation or assessment) as legitimate parts of academic practice. This includes knowing how adjustments are requested in the target context and how a tool’s functions, especially the generative-AI features now built into many of them, sit within the rules for each assessment.

Prerequisites: [Using academic support services](#lrnsupport-services) (soft), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies) (soft)

### Generative AI Literacy

Generative AI literacy is treated here as part of academic literacy: understanding how large language models generate text and why they fail; reading and applying AI policies that vary by institution, module and task; acknowledging and verifying AI use; and using AI as a tutor and practice partner in ways that build rather than replace learners' own language, reasoning and voice. The domain also covers the limits of AI-text detection, including documented bias against writers using English as an additional language and why detector output cannot establish misconduct, the contested question of where AI assistance ends and authorship begins, and privacy and intellectual-property risks. Tools and policies change quickly, so topics are framed around durable principles and verification habits rather than particular products.

<a id="gaihow-genai-works"></a>
#### How generative AI works and fails

`gai.how-genai-works` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Explain in non-technical terms how large language models generate text — by predicting likely continuations from patterns learned from very large training datasets, with further tuning to follow instructions — and why this produces fluent but unverified output, fabricated facts and references, biases inherited from training data, and gaps beyond the training data. This understanding underpins every judgement about when AI output can be trusted and how it must be checked.

<a id="gaipolicies"></a>
#### Interpreting AI policies

`gai.policies` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ (core band B1+) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Locate and interpret the rules on generative AI that apply to a specific task — institutional policy, programme and module guidance, the assessment brief and, for researchers, publisher and funder policies — including which kinds of use are permitted and what must be declared. Policies differ between and within institutions and change over time, so learners need a routine for checking each task and for asking when the rules are unclear.

Prerequisites: [Analysing and answering assignment and exam tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="gaiacknowledging-use"></a>
#### Acknowledging and citing AI use

`gai.acknowledging-use` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: core, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Declare and, where required, cite generative AI use in the form the institution, assessment or publisher specifies — typically stating which tools were used, for what purpose and at which stage, and keeping prompts and outputs as records — and distinguish acknowledging assistance from citing AI output as content. Records make declarations accurate and support reflection on how the tool was used; they are an ordinary good habit, not a means by which learners are expected to prove their innocence.

Prerequisites: [Citing diverse source types](#citdiverse-sources) (soft), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies)

<a id="gaiverifying-output"></a>
#### Verifying AI output

`gai.verifying-output` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: core, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Check AI output before relying on it: verify factual claims against authoritative sources, confirm that every reference exists and says what is attributed to it, check quotations and figures against the originals, and look for omissions and bias. Fabricated and erroneous citations are a documented failure of language models, so verification is a non-negotiable step whenever AI output informs academic work.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Lateral reading](#inflateral-reading) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

<a id="gailanguage-support"></a>
#### Using AI as a tutor and language-learning resource

`gai.language-support` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: revisit

Use generative AI as a tutor or practice partner — prompting for explanations at a chosen level, questions rather than answers, worked examples followed by practice, counter-arguments to test one's reasoning, and feedback that locates rather than silently fixes language errors — in ways that keep the learner doing the cognitive work and stay within the task's rules. Effective use means giving context and constraints, iterating prompts, evaluating suggestions critically, and checking afterwards, without the tool, whether understanding and unassisted writing or speaking have improved.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning and learner autonomy](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [Using written corrective feedback](#fbkcorrective-feedback) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies) (soft)

<a id="gaiauthorship-voice"></a>
#### AI, authorship and voice

`gai.authorship-voice` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Consider who is the author of a text produced with AI assistance — whose ideas, reasoning and words it contains, and who is accountable for them — and maintain one's own authorial voice and stance when using AI. AI-polished text can become generic and can misrepresent a writer's actual reasoning, so learners decide, with reference to the task's purpose and policy, where assistance must stop for the work to remain their own. Where that line lies is contested: AI language polishing can also reduce the penalties multilingual writers face for non-standard English, much as proofreaders and literacy brokers do.

Prerequisites: [Authorial voice](#staauthorial-voice), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="gaidetection-limits"></a>
#### Limits of AI detection

`gai.detection-limits` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · EGAP · audience: both

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Understand that AI-text detectors estimate the likelihood that text was machine-generated, produce both false positives and false negatives, can be evaded, and have been shown to misclassify writing by people who use English as an additional language as AI-generated at high rates. Detector output therefore does not establish misconduct; institutions remain responsible for not treating such scores as evidence, and learners benefit from knowing their rights in integrity processes and from keeping ordinary records of their writing process (notes, drafts, version history), without being expected to prove their innocence.

Prerequisites: [Interpreting similarity reports](#intsimilarity-reports) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

<a id="gairesearch-tools"></a>
#### AI-assisted research tools

`gai.research-tools` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Evaluate and use AI-based literature discovery, question-answering and summarisation tools, understanding their limits in coverage, ranking, reproducibility and accuracy, including how they inherit the coverage and ranking patterns of the indexes they draw on. These tools can speed up orientation to a field, but they cannot replace documented database searching, reading the sources themselves, or the researcher's own judgement of relevance and quality.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text) (soft), [Whose knowledge is cited](#citcitation-politics) (soft), [Verifying AI output](#gaiverifying-output)

<a id="gaiprivacy-ip"></a>
#### Privacy, data and intellectual property with AI

`gai.privacy-ip` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · EGAP

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: core

Judge what may and may not be entered into AI tools — personal and research-participant data, confidential or unpublished material, others' copyrighted work, and one's own unpublished work — taking account of the tool's terms on data retention and training, institutional data-protection rules and research ethics approvals. Learners also recognise that copyright in AI-generated content and in training data remains legally unsettled and varies by jurisdiction.

Prerequisites: [Open access and legal access](#infopen-access) (soft), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

## Disciplinary Literacies

### Science, Engineering & Computing

This domain covers the discourse of the natural sciences, engineering, mathematics and computing: how technicality, grammatical metaphor and taxonomies build scientific knowledge, and how learners produce explanations, protocols and specifications, experimental language, research records, proofs and software documentation. These fields are typically hard disciplines in Becher and Trowler’s terms, with cumulative knowledge, high consensus on method and texts in which visuals, notation and code carry much of the meaning. Teaching works best when EAP staff analyse departmental exemplars with subject lecturers, because conventions differ between, for example, a chemistry protocol, a civil engineering specification and a computing README.

<a id="dsescientific-language"></a>
#### The language of science

`dse.scientific-language` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Recognise how scientific English builds knowledge through technical terms organised into taxonomies, chains of interlocking definitions, grammatical metaphor (especially nominalisation) and high lexical density, and explain what each does for the scientific reader. Understanding these features as functional, not as obscurity, lets learners unpack dense textbook and research prose and begin to produce it.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation) (soft), [Classifying and categorising](#funclassifying) (soft)

<a id="dsescientific-explanation"></a>
#### Scientific explanations

`dse.scientific-explanation` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop

Write and present explanations of how or why phenomena occur, choosing among sequential explanations (a chain of events in time), causal and factorial explanations (multiple causes), consequential explanations (multiple effects) and theoretical explanations that apply a model or law to a case. Explanation is one of the most frequently assessed genres in undergraduate science and engineering and underlies exam answers, lab-report discussions and textbook learning.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Describing processes, diagrams and maps](#datdescribing-diagrams) (soft), [The language of science](#dsescientific-language)

<a id="dseprotocols-specifications"></a>
#### Protocols and specifications

`dse.protocols-specifications` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, computing)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Read and write procedural and specification texts used in laboratories and engineering: experimental protocols and standard operating procedures, method statements, and requirements or design specifications. This involves numbered imperative steps with exact quantities, conditions and tolerances, safety notes placed before the hazardous step, and requirement statements that use modal verbs consistently (often shall or must for mandatory, should for recommended, may for optional) so that each statement can be verified.

Prerequisites: [Lists and bullet points](#senlists-bullets) (soft), [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Units and measurement](#numunits-measurement)

<a id="dsehypothesis-language"></a>
#### Language of experimentation

`dse.hypothesis-language` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, psychology, health-medicine)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Use the language of experimental design and measurement precisely: testable hypotheses and predictions, independent, dependent and controlled variables, control conditions, random and systematic error, accuracy versus precision, and statements of uncertainty (for example with ± and appropriate significant figures). Many of these words have everyday meanings (error, control, significant, precise) that differ from their technical sense, so misuse signals misunderstanding of method as well as of language.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy), [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary) (soft)

<a id="dsesoftware-documentation"></a>
#### Software and technical documentation

`dse.software-documentation` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (computing, engineering)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write the main documentation genres of computing for their distinct readers: README files that tell a newcomer what a project does and how to install, run and contribute to it; code comments and docstrings that explain intent and non-obvious decisions rather than restating the code; API reference entries that specify parameters, return values, errors and examples; and design documents that set out context, goals and non-goals, alternatives considered and trade-offs. Documentation is assessed in computing projects and is a core professional literacy.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Protocols and specifications](#dseprotocols-specifications)

<a id="dsemathematical-writing"></a>
#### Mathematical writing and proofs

`dse.mathematical-writing` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (mathematics-statistics, computing)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write proofs and worked solutions in which notation is embedded in grammatical, punctuated sentences and the logical steps are signalled in words: introducing objects (let, suppose, define), stating quantifiers (for all, there exists), marking inference (hence, it follows that, since), naming the proof strategy (by contradiction, by induction, by the contrapositive) and closing the argument. Markers in mathematics expect the reasoning, not only the answer, to be readable, and students used to answer-only assessment often lose marks for unexplained steps.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals), [Linking adverbials and connector precision](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft), [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types) (soft), [Language of mathematical expression](#nummathematical-expression)

<a id="dselab-notebooks"></a>
#### Laboratory notebooks and research records

`dse.lab-notebooks` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (sciences, engineering, computing, health-medicine, psychology)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Keep contemporaneous, dated records of laboratory, computational or field work that another competent person could follow and that meet research-integrity and data-management expectations: aims and plans, materials and identifiers, procedures and any deviations from them, raw observations and measurements with units, file, sample and code locations, and brief interpretive comments kept distinguishable from observations. Notebooks, on paper or electronic, are a primary research record and are assessed in some practical modules and research projects, but their conventions are rarely taught explicitly.

Prerequisites: [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft), [Units and measurement](#numunits-measurement), [Protocols and specifications](#dseprotocols-specifications) (soft), [Language of experimentation](#dsehypothesis-language) (soft)

### Health & Life Sciences

This domain covers the literacies of nursing, midwifery, medicine, allied health, pharmacy and related life-science programmes, where academic writing is tied to professional practice and patient safety. Its topics are evidence-based practice as an organising concept, writing clinical reasoning, care plans, case presentations, reflective practice writing, communication on placement and critical appraisal of research. Many of these genres are assessed against professional standards as well as academic criteria, so EAP teachers should work with clinical educators, use anonymised local exemplars and check regulator and programme requirements on confidentiality and record-keeping.

<a id="dhlevidence-based-practice"></a>
#### Evidence-based practice

`dhl.evidence-based-practice` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Understand evidence-based practice as the integration of the best available research evidence with clinical expertise and the values and circumstances of the patient, not as following research alone. In academic work this means framing focused clinical questions with a framework suited to the question (for example PICO for intervention questions, or PEO or SPIDER for questions about experience), recognising evidence hierarchies and their limits, and writing assignments in which recommendations for practice are justified by appraised evidence, professional judgement and patient perspective.

Prerequisites: [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics) (soft)

<a id="dhlreflective-practice"></a>
#### Reflective practice writing in health

`dhl.reflective-practice` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write structured reflections on placement or practice experiences using a model the programme specifies (for example Gibbs's reflective cycle or a What? So what? Now what? model), moving from a concise, anonymised account of an event to analysis that draws on literature and professional standards, and ending with a concrete plan for future practice. Reflective writing is assessed throughout health programmes and in professional revalidation, and is judged on depth of analysis and professional judgement, not on confession.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice) (soft), [Writing clinical reasoning](#dhlclinical-reasoning-writing) (soft)

<a id="dhlcase-reports-care-plans"></a>
#### Case presentations

`dhl.case-reports-care-plans` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Deliver and write case presentations in which patient information is selected and ordered conventionally (presenting complaint, history, examination, investigations, assessment and plan) to support clinical decision-making for a particular audience, such as a ward round, a handover or a teaching session. Effective presentations select for relevance rather than completeness, distinguish findings from interpretation, use only approved abbreviations and respect confidentiality. They differ from published clinical case reports, which follow their own reporting guidance, and from care plans (dhl.care-plans).

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration), [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience) (soft), [Writing clinical reasoning](#dhlclinical-reasoning-writing) (soft)

<a id="dhlprofessional-communication"></a>
#### Professional communication in health placements

`dhl.professional-communication` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Communicate safely and appropriately with patients, families and colleagues on placement: structuring consultations, asking open and closed questions, explaining in lay terms and checking understanding, responding to emotion, seeking consent, and giving concise structured handovers or escalations to colleagues. On placement, language is part of clinical safety and is assessed against professional standards; it also involves understanding colloquial and regional ways patients describe symptoms.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences) (soft), [Understanding varied accents](#lisaccent-variation) (soft), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="dhlcritical-appraisal"></a>
#### Critical appraisal of health research

`dhl.critical-appraisal` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: revisit

Appraise a single health research study with a structured tool suited to its design (for example a CASP or JBI checklist) and write up the appraisal as a reasoned judgement about its validity, results and applicability to a practice question. Appraisal assignments are common in health programmes and are marked on judgement, not on checklist completion. Writing systematic and scoping reviews, which builds on appraisal, is covered by res.systematic-review-writing.

Prerequisites: [Critical review of an article](#gescritique), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics) (soft), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice)

<a id="dhlclinical-reasoning-writing"></a>
#### Writing clinical reasoning

`dhl.clinical-reasoning-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Make the reasoning behind clinical judgements explicit in assignments: move from a structured assessment of a patient or scenario to identifying and prioritising problems, to justified interventions and to how outcomes will be evaluated, linking each step to cues in the data, to evidence and to the relevant professional standards. Nursing, midwifery, pharmacy and allied-health assignments are marked on this visible reasoning, often scaffolded by a clinical reasoning cycle or a similar model, rather than on correct answers alone.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory) (soft), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice)

<a id="dhlcare-plans"></a>
#### Care plans

`dhl.care-plans` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write care plans and care-planning assignments that move from a holistic assessment to prioritised, person-centred problems or needs, goals that are specific, measurable and time-bound, evidence-based interventions with rationales, and criteria and timing for evaluation. Care plans are usually structured by the nursing process (assessment, planning, implementation and evaluation, often with problem identification or nursing diagnosis as a separate step) and by the nursing model or documentation standard the programme specifies; they must be anonymised and use only approved abbreviations.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Inclusive and bias-free language](#reginclusive-language) (soft), [Writing clinical reasoning](#dhlclinical-reasoning-writing)

### Business & Economics

This domain covers the literacies of business, management, accounting, finance and economics programmes, which together attract the largest international cohorts on UG and PGT courses. Business schools assess many simulated or live professional genres (case analyses, reports, memos, pitches, consultancy reports) oriented to decision-makers, accounting and finance assess commentary on figures and disclosures, and economics builds arguments from models, assumptions and conditional reasoning. Nathan (2013) and Zhu (2004) show that these assignment genres differ from the academic essay in purpose, structure and reader, so EAP teaching should analyse the school’s own briefs and exemplars with subject staff.

<a id="dbebusiness-case-analysis"></a>
#### Business case analysis

`dbe.business-case-analysis` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (business-management)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write a business case analysis or case report: identify the central problem in an organisational case, analyse its causes with appropriate frameworks and the data in the case, generate and evaluate options against explicit criteria, and make justified, feasible recommendations for a named decision-maker. The genre is problem- and decision-oriented rather than thesis-led and is among the most common assessments in business schools.

Prerequisites: [Case study analysis](#grpcase-study), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft), [Applying management frameworks critically](#dbecritical-frameworks)

<a id="dbeeconomic-reasoning"></a>
#### Language of economic reasoning

`dbe.economic-reasoning` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (economics, finance-accounting)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Control the language economists use to reason with models: stating assumptions, holding other factors constant (ceteris paribus), conditional and counterfactual predictions, comparative statics (what happens to equilibrium when one variable changes), marginal reasoning, and the distinction between positive and normative statements. It also includes describing diagrams precisely (a shift of a curve versus a movement along it) and distinguishing causal from correlational claims in empirical economics.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs) (soft)

<a id="dbeprofessional-genres"></a>
#### Professional business genres

`dbe.professional-genres` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (business-management, finance-accounting)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write and present the simulated professional genres business schools assess: memos, briefing notes, management reports and pitches. These genres put the main point or recommendation first, are organised for a busy decision-maker, use informative headings and selective bullet points, and adjust tone and directness to the relationship between writer and reader. Their conventions differ from the academic essay and can vary between schools and industries.

Prerequisites: [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience) (soft), [Business report](#grpbusiness-report), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="dbecritical-frameworks"></a>
#### Applying management frameworks critically

`dbe.critical-frameworks` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (business-management)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Use management frameworks such as SWOT, PESTLE, Porter's Five Forces and the value chain as analytical tools: choose a framework that fits the question, support each entry with case evidence, prioritise factors by significance, draw out implications for the organisation, and acknowledge the framework's limitations. Markers commonly criticise frameworks presented as unprioritised lists without analysis or implications.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating theories and models](#crtevaluating-theories) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory)

<a id="dbeaccounting-finance-discourse"></a>
#### Language and genres of accounting and finance

`dbe.accounting-finance-discourse` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (finance-accounting)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Read and write the genres of accounting and finance study: commentary on financial statements and ratios, audit, valuation and investment reports, and analyses of corporate disclosures. This requires reporting figures and changes accurately, moving from description to explanation and evaluation for a named user of the accounts, hedging forecasts and valuations to their assumptions, and reading corporate narratives critically, since narrative sections of annual reports are written to persuade as well as to inform.

Prerequisites: [Hedging and qualifying generalisations](#stahedging) (soft), [Recognising bias and perspective](#rdcbias-perspective) (soft), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary), [Relative and absolute change](#numrelative-absolute)

<a id="dbeconsultancy-report"></a>
#### Consultancy and client project reports

`dbe.consultancy-report` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (business-management)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write reports for live or simulated consultancy projects in which a student team addresses a client organisation's problem: frame and agree the brief, present evidence-based analysis and prioritised, feasible recommendations with an implementation plan for the client, and, where required, a separate academic or reflective account for the marker. The genre serves two readers with different needs: a client who wants actionable advice in accessible language, and an academic marker who expects theory, evidence and critical reflection.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Business report](#grpbusiness-report), [Executive summary](#grpexecutive-summary) (soft), [Business case analysis](#dbebusiness-case-analysis) (soft)

### Law

This domain covers the literacies of studying law in English, with a primary focus on common-law systems such as England and Wales: the register and terms of art of legal English, reading and briefing cases, reading legislation, answering problem questions, writing legal essays, arguing orally in moots and writing law dissertations. Legal knowledge is constructed through authoritative texts (judgments and statutes) whose language is itself the object of analysis, so reading precision and the handling of authority are central (Bhatia, 1993; Twining & Miers, 2010), and doctrinal research has its own method (Hutchinson & Duncan, 2012). Many international law students, especially on LLM programmes, trained in civil-law or mixed systems, which makes it particularly important to make common-law conventions explicit.

<a id="dlwlegal-language"></a>
#### Legal language and register

`dlw.legal-language` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (law)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Recognise the distinctive features of legal English and why they exist: terms of art with fixed legal meanings (consideration, negligence, estoppel), everyday words with specialised legal senses (party, action, hold, find), Latin and Law French expressions, binomials such as null and void, deontic modals, long sentences with embedded qualifications, and the plain-language movement's critique of archaic drafting. Legal terms are tied to a particular legal system, so their meaning cannot be assumed from another jurisdiction or from a bilingual dictionary.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy), [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft)

<a id="dlwcase-reading"></a>
#### Reading and briefing cases

`dlw.case-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (law)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: core

Read law reports strategically and brief them: use the citation, court and headnote to orient (knowing that the headnote is an editor's summary, not authority), then identify the material facts, procedural history, legal issues, the parties' arguments, the decision, the ratio decidendi and any obiter dicta, and note concurring or dissenting judgments. Case reading is the foundation of common-law study because legal rules are extracted from, and argued about through, judgments.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Legal language and register](#dlwlegal-language)

<a id="dlwproblem-questions"></a>
#### Legal problem questions

`dlw.problem-questions` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (law)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Answer hypothetical fact scenarios by advising a party: identify every legal issue the facts raise, state the relevant rule with authority, apply it to the specific facts (including by analogy with and distinction from decided cases), consider alternative interpretations where facts are uncertain, and reach a reasoned, appropriately hedged conclusion. IRAC (Issue, Rule, Application, Conclusion) and variants such as CLEO are widely taught scaffolds, but lecturers differ on them and critics note that applied mechanically they encourage rule-stating without application.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Reading and briefing cases](#dlwcase-reading), [Reading legislation](#dlwstatutes) (soft)

<a id="dlwstatutes"></a>
#### Reading legislation

`dlw.statutes` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (law)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Navigate and interpret legislation: use its structure (long and short title, parts, sections, subsections, paragraphs, schedules, interpretation and commencement provisions) to find relevant provisions, follow cross-references and definitions (including the difference between 'means' and 'includes'), parse qualifications such as subject to, notwithstanding and unless, and recognise both the traditional textbook approaches to interpretation (literal, golden and mischief rules) and the contextual, purposive approach that UK courts now describe as the modern method. Statutory text is drafted for precision and comprehensiveness, so reading it requires slow, structural parsing.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences), [Legal language and register](#dlwlegal-language)

<a id="dlwlegal-essays"></a>
#### Legal essays

`dlw.legal-essays` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (law)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write essays that argue a position about the law or legal policy: explain the current law accurately from primary authority, analyse its coherence and development, evaluate it from doctrinal, theoretical, socio-legal or law-reform perspectives using academic commentary and official reports, and sustain a thesis in answer to the question. Legal essays rely on a hierarchy of authority, use reporting verbs with legal meanings (a court holds, a judge observes, counsel submits) and are usually referenced with footnotes in OSCOLA.

Prerequisites: [Reporting verbs and signal phrases](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft), [Note-based citation](#citnote-based), [Argumentative essay](#gesargumentative-essay), [Reading and briefing cases](#dlwcase-reading)

<a id="dlwmooting"></a>
#### Mooting and oral legal argument

`dlw.mooting` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · ESAP (law)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Prepare and deliver oral legal submissions in moots and other assessed advocacy exercises: draft a skeleton argument, order the grounds, cite authority orally in the conventions used in court, answer interventions from the bench, concede or distinguish where appropriate, and use the forms of address and register the court expects. Mooting is assessed on many LLB programmes and some LLMs, and judges reward responsiveness to questions as much as prepared delivery.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions) (soft), [Arguing orally](#argoral-argument), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft), [Legal problem questions](#dlwproblem-questions)

<a id="dlwlegal-research-writing"></a>
#### Legal research and dissertation writing

`dlw.legal-research-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · ESAP (law)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Plan and write an LLB, LLM or doctoral law dissertation: choose and justify a method (doctrinal, socio-legal, comparative or theoretical), build a research question from a problem in the state of the law rather than only from an empirical gap, structure a sustained legal argument across chapters, and handle primary and secondary authority and note-based referencing such as OSCOLA at length. Doctrinal theses are rarely organised by IMRaD, so general research-writing models need adapting.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions) (soft), [Thesis macrostructure](#resthesis-macrostructure) (soft), [Legal essays](#dlwlegal-essays)

### Social Sciences & Education

This domain covers the literacies of sociology, politics, social policy, international relations, psychology, education and related fields. Key concepts are often contested, several research paradigms coexist and writers are expected to justify their conceptual and methodological choices explicitly, while psychology combines this with the tightly conventionalised experimental report and education with practice genres such as lesson-plan rationales and teaching reflections. The topics address working with contested concepts, justifying methodology, the process texts of qualitative research, policy analysis, psychology reports and education practice genres, which recur from UG coursework to doctoral theses. Because expectations vary between fields and between supervisors, analysis of local exemplars with subject staff is essential.

<a id="dssoperationalising-concepts"></a>
#### Working with concepts

`dss.operationalising-concepts` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, psychology)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Handle contested social concepts (for example class, identity, power, inclusion, globalisation) explicitly: show that a concept is defined differently in different theoretical traditions, state and justify the definition adopted, and, in empirical work, operationalise it as indicators, measures or coding categories, or treat it as a sensitising concept in qualitative work. Markers in the social sciences reward conceptual precision and penalise treating contested terms as self-evident.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources) (soft)

<a id="dssqualitative-writing"></a>
#### Field notes, analytic memos and analysis accounts

`dss.qualitative-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Produce the working and process texts of qualitative research: field notes that separate observational description from reflective and analytic comment; analytic memos that record developing codes, interpretations and questions; and accounts of the analysis process, for example in a methods chapter or appendix, that make coding and theme development transparent and position the researcher reflexively. Writing up findings, including the use of participant extracts, is covered by res.qualitative-reporting.

Prerequisites: [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation), [Reflexivity and positionality](#crtreflexivity) (soft), [Justifying methodology](#dssmethodology-justification) (soft)

<a id="dsspolicy-analysis"></a>
#### Policy analysis

`dss.policy-analysis` · GENRE · B2+–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (social-sciences, education)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write academic analyses of public or educational policy: define and critically examine how a policy problem is framed, identify stakeholders and interests, assess the evidence base, compare policy options against explicit criteria such as effectiveness, equity, cost and feasibility, and reach a justified recommendation or critical conclusion. Policy analysis ranges from technical option appraisal to critical approaches that ask how problems are represented, and assignments may expect either.

Prerequisites: [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution), [Identifying assumptions and values](#rdcassumptions), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft)

<a id="dssmethodology-justification"></a>
#### Justifying methodology

`dss.methodology-justification` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 (core band C1) · ESAP (social-sciences, education, business-management, health-medicine)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Justify research design in writing: state the philosophical worldview or paradigm informing the study (for example post-positivist, constructivist, transformative or pragmatist), explain how the chosen approach and design follow from the research questions, justify sampling, data collection and analysis with reference to methodological literature, acknowledge trade-offs and ethical considerations, and distinguish methodology (the rationale) from methods (the procedures). Methodology chapters are where examiners judge coherence between questions, design and claims.

Prerequisites: [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs) (soft), [Methods sections](#resmethods), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions)

<a id="dsspsychology-research-report"></a>
#### Psychology research reports

`dss.psychology-research-report` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (psychology)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write empirical research reports in psychology in APA style: an introduction that reviews evidence and leads to explicit hypotheses; a method section reporting participants, design, materials and procedure in enough detail for replication; results reported with test statistics, effect sizes and confidence intervals in the conventional format; and a discussion that relates findings to the hypotheses and literature with calibrated claims. The report is the main assessed genre of psychology degrees, and its conventions are specified closely by the APA Publication Manual and departmental guides.

Prerequisites: [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft), [Laboratory report](#grplab-report), [Reporting quantitative findings](#resquantitative-reporting) (soft), [Language of experimentation](#dsehypothesis-language) (soft)

<a id="dsseducation-practice-genres"></a>
#### Education and TESOL practice genres

`dss.education-practice-genres` · GENRE · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (education)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core

Write the practice genres of education and TESOL programmes: lesson plans with a rationale that links aims, staging, activities and assessment to the learners and to theory; reflections on teaching practice that analyse taught or observed lessons and feedback; materials evaluations against explicit criteria; and reports of small-scale classroom research such as action research or practitioner enquiry. These genres connect teaching experience with research and theory and are central to assessment on initial teacher education and MA TESOL programmes.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing), [Justifying methodology](#dssmethodology-justification) (soft)

### Humanities

This domain covers the literacies of literary studies, history, philosophy, religious studies, classics and related fields, where knowledge is built through interpretation and argument about texts, sources and ideas rather than through accumulating findings. Becher and Trowler characterise these 'soft-pure' fields as reiterative and interpretive, and Hyland (2004) shows that their writing typically has more explicit authorial presence and evaluative engagement with sources than the sciences. The topics cover close reading, working with primary sources, interpretive argument and philosophical writing, all of which reward detailed engagement with language and a sustained individual voice.

<a id="dhuclose-reading"></a>
#### Close reading and textual analysis

`dhu.close-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (humanities)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Analyse how a passage creates meaning through its language and form, noticing patterns of word choice, imagery, syntax, sound, structure, point of view and genre, and moving from observation to interpretation by explaining what each feature does and why it matters. Close reading underpins literary studies and is also used in history, philosophy and cultural studies; a multilingual reader's sensitivity to language is a resource for it.

Prerequisites: [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms) (soft), [Making inferences](#rdsinference), [Purposeful annotation](#rdsannotation) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="dhuprimary-sources"></a>
#### Working with primary sources

`dhu.primary-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 (core band B2+) · ESAP (humanities)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: develop

Interpret primary sources such as documents, letters, images, objects and archival records by sourcing them (who produced them, when, why and for whom), contextualising them in their period, corroborating them against other sources, reading them closely and noting their silences. Write about them with appropriate caution and cite them in the conventions of the discipline, including archival locations and translations. Whether a source counts as primary depends on the research question.

Prerequisites: [Recognising bias and perspective](#rdcbias-perspective), [Types of sources](#infsource-types), [Note-based citation](#citnote-based) (soft), [Close reading and textual analysis](#dhuclose-reading) (soft)

<a id="dhuinterpretive-argument"></a>
#### Interpretive argument

`dhu.interpretive-argument` · GENRE · B2–C2 (core band B2+) · ESAP (humanities)

Stages — pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write essays that argue for an interpretation of texts, sources or events: pose an arguable interpretive thesis, build it through analysed textual or documentary evidence, position it in relation to existing critical or historiographical readings, address alternative readings and sustain an evaluative voice. Disciplinary conventions differ, for example the present tense for discussing literary works and the movement in history from recounting events to explaining and arguing about them.

Prerequisites: [Integrating quotations](#srcintegrating-quotations), [Argumentative essay](#gesargumentative-essay), [Close reading and textual analysis](#dhuclose-reading)

<a id="dhuphilosophical-writing"></a>
#### Philosophical writing

`dhu.philosophical-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band B2+) · ESAP (humanities)

Stages — in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop, pgr: develop

Write philosophy essays that reconstruct an argument charitably in premise–conclusion form, evaluate whether it is valid and whether its premises are true, raise objections (including counterexamples and thought experiments), consider replies, and defend a position with clear distinctions and definitions. Philosophical prose values clarity, precision and explicit signposting over rhetorical flourish, and commonly uses the first person to mark the writer's argumentative moves.

Prerequisites: [Reading theoretical and conceptual texts](#rdctheoretical-texts) (soft), [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

### Creative Arts & Design

This domain covers the literacies of fine art, design (graphic, product, fashion, interior), architecture, performance and related practice-based programmes, where much learning takes place in the studio and assessment centres on work produced, presented and explained. Language does specific jobs here: describing and interpreting visual work, presenting and defending work in critiques, writing statements and rationales that make practice legible to tutors, audiences and juries, and, at master’s and doctoral level, writing the exegesis that argues the research contribution of a practice. Schön’s (1983) account of design as a reflective conversation with materials and Kress and van Leeuwen’s (2006) visual grammar give EAP teachers ways to describe these practices, but the tacit vocabulary of a studio is local, so close collaboration with studio tutors and observation of crits is recommended.

<a id="dadstudio-critique"></a>
#### Studio critiques

`dad.studio-critique` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (arts-design)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Take part in studio critiques (crits) in their various forms, from one-to-one desk crits to group crits and formal reviews with a panel: present work concisely by explaining intentions, process and key decisions; listen to and clarify critique; respond to challenges by explaining or reconsidering choices without defensiveness; and give peers specific, constructive critique. Crits are a central, often assessed, speech event in art and design education with conventions and a vocabulary that are rarely made explicit.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis)

<a id="dadartist-statement"></a>
#### Artist and design statements

`dad.artist-statement` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (arts-design)

Stages — foundation: develop, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Write short statements, typically 50 to 300 words, that explain the concept, materials and process, and context of a work or body of practice for a specific purpose such as an exhibition label, degree show, portfolio or application. Effective statements are concrete and specific, usually written in the present tense, in the first person for statements the artist or designer authors but often in the third person for wall labels, catalogue entries and short bios, and connect the work to influences or ideas without inflated or unexplained theoretical language.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis)

<a id="daddesign-rationale"></a>
#### Design rationale and process documentation

`dad.design-rationale` · GENRE · B1+–C1 (core band B2) · ESAP (arts-design)

Stages — foundation: develop, in-sessional-ug: core, in-sessional-pgt: develop

Document and justify the design process in sketchbooks, process journals, annotated boards and written rationales: record research, ideation, development, testing and refinement; explain each significant decision with reference to the brief, users, constraints and precedents; show alternatives considered and why they were rejected; and evaluate the outcome against the brief. Rationales make the designer's reasoning visible to assessors and are a major component of many practice-based assessments.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft), [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis) (soft)

<a id="dadvisual-analysis"></a>
#### Visual analysis

`dad.visual-analysis` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 (core band B1+) · ESAP (arts-design, media-communication)

Stages — foundation: core, pre-sessional-ug: develop, pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-ug: develop, in-sessional-pgt: revisit

Describe, analyse and interpret visual works such as artworks, designs, photographs, buildings and adverts using disciplinary vocabulary: formal elements (composition, line, colour, tone, texture, scale, space, medium), and social semiotic concepts such as salience, framing, information value and gaze. Move from what is visible to what it means in context, distinguishing observation from inference and hedging interpretations appropriately. Visual analysis underpins crits, statements, contextual studies essays and design rationales.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="dadpractice-research-exegesis"></a>
#### Practice-based research and exegesis

`dad.practice-research-exegesis` · GENRE · B2+–C2 (core band C1) · ESAP (arts-design)

Stages — pre-sessional-pg: develop, in-sessional-pgt: core, pgr: core

Write the exegesis or critical commentary that accompanies practice-based or practice-led research in art, design and performance: situate the practice in its field and in relevant theory, articulate the research questions and methods pursued through making, document the process, and argue the contribution of the work and the text together. Institutions differ in how they weight practice and writing and in the expected length and form of the text, and the relationship between artefact and exegesis is itself debated.

Prerequisites: [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review) (soft), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft), [Artist and design statements](#dadartist-statement) (soft), [Design rationale and process documentation](#daddesign-rationale)
